Title: A Comprehensive Framework for All Stages of Large-Model Training: From Data and Tokenization, through Pretraining, to Post-Training Alignment

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33451v1

Published Time: Tue, 27 Oct 2026 00:26:59 GMT

Number of Pages: 143

Markdown Content:
# A Comprehensive Framework for All Stages of Large-Model Training: From Data and Tokenization, through Pretraining, to Post-Training Alignment (2017–2026) 

## Xiangjing An 1,* Zhibin Jiao 1 Aiting Chen 1

> 1

Independent Researcher Team 

> *

Corresponding author: axjing.ai@gmail.com ∗

## 2026 

Abstract 

The capabilities of large language models (LLMs) and multimodal large language models (MLLMs) arise from the synergy of the entire pipeline—"data and tokenization → pretrain-ing → post-training (SFT → preference alignment / reinforcement learning → continual optimization)"—rather than from the contribution of any single stage. However, existing sur-veys mostly adopt a single-stage or single-technique-family perspective: data selection [392], pretraining data construction [393], discrete tokenization [394], post-training [395], RL post-training [396][399], preference optimization [397][398], and multimodality [402][403][404][405] each form their own systems, lacking a unified stage division and a horizontally comparable basis. This paper proposes a unified stage division and a six-dimensional analytical frame-work (data, objective function, optimization strategy, system constraints, evaluation, and risk and governance), and on this basis systematically reviews 395 core references, cover-ing 2017–2026 (with a few foundational earlier works), and presents a conceptual map of cross-stage effects—the forward constraint that pretraining imposes on post-training and the reverse empowerment that post-training exerts on data / pretraining. We find the fol-lowing: first, the comparability of scaling laws (Kaplan [119] and Chinchilla [16]) depends on data quality and data mixture, a long-neglected variable; second, preference alignment (RLHF [15], DPO [32]) and reasoning RL (verifiable reward) are redefining the boundaries of "post-training," but the debate between Pass@1 and Pass@K has yet to yield a systematic conclusion; third, multimodal training poses structural challenges to the pure-text paradigm in terms of tokenizer, objective, and evaluation. The full text provides a cross-stage effects diagram, a method-family map, a practical decision tree, and ≥10 verifiable open problems, and reserves for each stage an extensible skeleton for focused surveys (sub-surveys). As of the retrieval date, peer-reviewed evidence (A+B) accounts for about 79% of the 395 references. 

Keywords: large language model; pretraining; post-training; supervised fine-tuning; pref-erence alignment; reinforcement learning; tokenization; multimodality; scaling law; evidence grading

# 1 Introduction 

## 1.1 Motivation: Why a "General Framework for All Stages" Is Needed 

Conclusion first: the stages of the full training pipeline are not independent of one another; they exhibit strong coupling and feedback, and therefore any single-stage survey cannot fully explain where capabilities come from. The objective function and data mixture determined during pre-training directly set the attainable ceiling of "alignment" in the post-training stage—a model pretrained on a low-quality, narrowly distributed corpus will struggle to recover the knowledge and reasoning abilities it never acquired, no matter how extensive the instruction tuning. Con-versely, the failure modes exposed during post-training (such as over-refusal, hallucination, and format non-compliance) in turn drive revisions to the data side and to the pretraining recipe (such as curriculum, two-stage data mixture, and tokenizer design). This bidirectional relation-ship of "forward constraint + reverse empowerment" is the starting point that distinguishes this paper from existing stage-specific surveys. The rapid overlapping of training paradigms over the past three years has further intensified the risk of fragmentation. Taking 2022 as the dividing line, the Chinchilla training-type scal-ing law [16] re-estimated the data-to-parameter ratio, making "more tokens, better data" the mainstream orientation; subsequently, instruction tuning [163][172], RLHF [15], DPO [32], and reasoning RL with verifiable rewards expanded "post-training" from a lightweight finishing step into a capability-shaping stage on a par with pretraining [395][396]. At the same time, multi-modal models [402][404] reopened questions of tokenization, objective functions, and evaluation. If these developments are read separately, researchers easily arrive at mutually incompatible ex-periences and conclusions; only by comparing them under a unified set of stages and a unified set of dimensions can one judge which are universal regularities and which are products of specific recipes. The positioning of this paper is that of a "general framework" rather than a "most comprehensive survey": we strive to provide, for each stage, a structure sufficient to guide practice (definition—boundaries—method families—controversies—recommendations), with ev-idence strength explicitly annotated, rather than attempting to exhaust every variant of a given technique. The value of this approach lies in the following: when readers need to delve into a particular subfield, this paper offers an extensible skeleton and a unified terminology that can be expanded into focused surveys, rather than replacing dedicated surveys. 

## 1.2 Differences from Existing Surveys 

Existing high-quality surveys cover multiple "localities" of this topic but lack a unified framework that stitches them together. On the data dimension, Albalak et al. [392] systematically reviewed data selection methods, with an emphasis on pretraining and in-cluding chapters on SFT / alignment / ICL, but they do not connect the full chain of "data →objective →system →evaluation →governance," nor do they address multimodal tokeniza-tion; Parmar et al. [393] focused on full-pipeline ablations of pretraining data construction, lacking post-training and system constraints; Jia et al. [394] focused on the mechanisms and applications of discrete tokenizers without discussing the coupling between tokenization and training stages. On the pretraining dimension, existing work either takes the scaling law as its main thread or focuses on system efficiency, and rarely discusses objective functions, ar-chitecture, optimization stability, and distributed systems side by side. On the post-training dimension, Tie et al. [395] take five post-training paradigms (fine-tuning, alignment, reasoning, efficiency, and integration) as their sole main axis, without connecting backward to the pretrain-ing empowerment relationship; the RL post-training surveys [396][399] and the reasoning RL surveys [400][401] cover intra-algorithm details in greater depth but have a weaker cross-stage constraint and system / governance perspective; the DPO survey series [397][398] and the reward All Stages of Large-Model Training 7

model survey [408] focus on the preference optimization family. On the multimodal dimension, MM-LLMs [402], the data-centric multimodal survey [403], Omni-MLLM [404], and the efficient multimodal survey [405] each form their own systems and are not compared with pure-text train-ing along the same six dimensions. In addition, although works such as data-mixture inference [406] and two-stage pretraining [407] touch upon cross-stage mixtures, they remain single-point methodological studies. By contrast, the differences of this paper can be summarized in four points: a unified stage division (explicitly distinguishing the broad / narrow senses of "post-training"); a unified six-dimensional analytical framework with mutually orthogonal dimensions; an explicit cross-stage influence diagram (forward constraint and reverse empowerment); and a consistent evidence grad-ing and Cited-by-evidence conclusion-strength annotation throughout. Accordingly, this paper does not compete with the above surveys in coverage; rather, it provides a "meta-framework" that can align each of them to the same coordinate system. 

## 1.3 List of Contributions 

The main contributions of this paper are as follows: 1. Unified stage division and terminological conventions : We specify five stages—"data and tokenization →pretraining →post-training (SFT →preference alignment/RL →continual optimization)"—and explicitly clarify the broad and narrow ambiguities of "post-training" (Chapter 2). 2. Six-dimensional analytical framework : We characterize any training method along six orthogonal dimensions—data, objective function, optimization strategy, system constraints, evaluation, and risk and governance—enabling textual and multimodal methods, and pre-training and post-training, to be compared horizontally (Chapters 3–4, Chapter 11). 3. Cross-stage influence diagram : We present the explicit relationships of forward constraint (pretraining →post-training) and reverse empowerment (post-training/evaluation →data and pretraining design), with evidence strength annotated (Chapter 11). 4. Uniformly graded comparison and conclusion strength : We grade the 391 core ref-erences from A to D, and every comparison reports the parameter count, token count, and compute basis, avoiding direct comparisons of numbers across datasets (throughout the book, Appendices A/E). 5. Practical decision tree and failure-mode checklist : We provide candidate routes and trade-offs oriented toward budget, modality, context length, and compliance constraints (Chapter 13). 6. ≥10 verifiable open problems : We transform field disagreements into experimentally testable research questions (Chapters 14–15, with the complete pool in Appendix F). 7. Extensible focused-survey skeleton : Each stage chapter can be independently expanded into a focused survey and reuses a uniform writing template (Appendices A–F). 

## 1.4 Scope, Audience, and Structure 

Scope : This paper focuses on the training process of large language models and multimodal large language models, with retrieval up to 2026-10-24. Included: data and tokenization, pretraining, instruction tuning (SFT/PEFT), preference alignment and reinforcement learning, continual optimization (continued pretraining, long-context extension, distillation/pruning/quantization, model editing and unlearning), a cross-cutting view of multimodal training, and evaluation and governance. Excluded : inference and serving deployment engineering, industry implementation cases, traditional non-LLM training, pure reinforcement learning theory, and hardware circuitry and cognitive science mechanisms. 

Audience : Researchers and engineers engaged in model training, graduate students who need to understand training trade-offs across stages, and readers outside the field who are in-All Stages of Large-Model Training 8

terested in the evolution of training paradigms. We assume readers possess basic knowledge of deep learning and Transformers (if needed, see [108]). 

Structure : Chapter 2 presents the glossary and an overview of training stages; Chapter 3 formalizes the language and multimodal modeling, stage splitting, and evaluation notation; Chapter 4 presents the taxonomy; Chapters 5–9 discuss data and tokenization, pretraining, SFT, preference alignment/RL, and continual optimization, respectively; Chapter 10 discusses multimodal training from a cross-cutting perspective; Chapter 11 makes cross-stage and cross-modal comparisons; Chapter 12 reviews datasets and evaluation; Chapter 13 provides a practical guide; Chapters 14–16 discuss challenges, future directions, and conclusions; and Appendices A–F provide the literature matrix, hyperparameter and compute comparisons, reproducibility checklists, retrieval logs, and focused-survey outlines. 

## 1.5 Methodology and Evidence Conventions 

Conclusion first: the credibility of this survey comes not from its coverage but from the explicit binding of "conclusion—evidence"; to this end, this paper establishes verifiable conventions at three levels. 

Retrieval and screening. The methodology follows the PRISMA process: we searched arXiv and Semantic Scholar with 11 topic clusters, identified approximately 640 records, screened them down to approximately 470 according to inclusion/exclusion criteria, and finally verified 391 core references (deduplicating 59 duplicate identifiers). A few foundational works predate the 2017–2026 window. The search terms, time periods, and inclusion/exclusion criteria are summarized in Appendix E; the reference-assembly scripts and metadata cache are provided as supplementary material and support offline recomputation. 

Evidence grading (A–D). This paper annotates each cited work with its source relia-bility: A for peer-reviewed with public reproducibility artifacts; B for peer-reviewed; C for preprint/technical report/unconfirmed publication status; D for non-peer-reviewed sources such as blogs and model cards. In the current literature base, A+B account for approximately 79%, C for approximately 21%, with no ungraded items. Wherever judgment of conclusion strength is involved, this paper refers back to this grading (distribution in Appendix E). 

Comparison discipline. Whenever works are compared across studies, the bases of pa-rameter count N , training token count D, and compute C must be given; when unavailable, the marker [to be verified] is used, and no conclusion of numerical superiority or inferiority is drawn. Citation verbs are strictly distinguished—"propose," "claim," "prove," and "report"—to reflect the evidential status of the original literature; technical reports and blogs are annotated with "non-peer-reviewed + version date," and marketing conclusions with "(claimed)." The pur-pose of these conventions is to allow readers to independently judge which conclusion can be safely reused and which can serve only as a hypothesis (see Chapter 12 and Appendix E for details). 

## 1.6 Reading Guide 

This paper can be read in three ways. By stage : read Chapters 5–9 in sequence to obtain the full training workflow; by problem : first read the practical guide in Chapter 13 to locate decision points, then return to the corresponding chapters for evidence and costs; by comparison : first read the horizontal comparison in Chapter 11 and the open problems in Chapter 14, then delve deeper as needed. The book includes five core figures and tables: Figure 1, the overall diagram of the full training workflow (Chapter 2); Figure 2, the stage relationship table (Chapter 4); Figure 3, the method-family map (with four colors for classic/mainstream/frontier/controversial, Chapter 4); Figure 4, the practical decision tree (Chapter 13); and Figure 5, the evidence map and open-problems table (Chapter 11). The "representative work table" and "key controversies" sections All Stages of Large-Model Training 9

of each chapter are separable minimal reading units; Appendices A–F provide the raw matrices, templates, and focused-survey skeleton for readers who wish to further verify or expand. 

How to judge the strength of a conclusion. When reading, we recommend a three-step check: 1○ Does the conclusion have A/B-level evidence, or does it come only from a single technical report (C)? 2○ Does the comparison give the (N, D, C ) bases, or does it compare numbers directly across datasets? 3○ Does the conclusion distinguish "mechanistic hypotheses" from "recipe details"—the former are generally transferable, while the latter are highly dependent on specific settings. A conclusion that passes all three steps can be safely reused; any that fails can serve only as a hypothesis pending verification. The "traffic-light" interpretation method given later in Chapter 12 is precisely the concretization of this check in the evaluation context. 

# 2 Glossary and Overview of Training Stages 

## 2.1 Glossary 

Conclusion first: terminological ambiguity is the primary cause of failures in cross-stage com-parison, so this section fixes the vocabulary used throughout the survey. The core terms are as follows: • Autoregressive language modeling (AR / NTP) : a training paradigm whose objective is to "predict the next token"; it is the dominant objective for current decoder-only LLMs [121]. • Masked language modeling (MLM) : randomly masks input tokens and predicts the masked ones, exemplified by BERT [47]. • Sequence-to-sequence (seq2seq) : unifies encoder–decoder into "text-to-text", exemplified by T5 [49]. • Tokenizer : a component that splits raw text into subword units; representative methods include BPE [1] and Unigram / SentencePiece [5]. • Vocabulary : a finite set of tokens; its size and coverage determine the boundary of fairness for multilingual and multimodal settings. • Pretraining : the stage that acquires general representations and knowledge from large-scale unlabeled corpora via self-supervised objectives. • Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) / instruction tuning (IFT) : supervised training on "instruction–response" pairs so that the model follows instructions [163][172]. • Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) : a family of fine-tuning methods that update only a small number of parameters (e.g., LoRA [12], QLoRA [30], Adapter, Prefix/P-Tuning [161][164]). • Post-training : in the broad sense , all training after pretraining, including SFT, preference alignment/RL, and continual optimization; in the narrow sense , only the preference align-ment and reinforcement learning after SFT [395][396]. This survey defaults to the broad sense and explicitly marks every use of the narrow sense. • Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) : first trains a reward model, then optimizes the policy with RL (e.g., PPO) [15]. • Reinforcement learning from AI feedback (RLAIF) : uses AI (models) to replace part of the human annotation in providing feedback. • Direct preference optimization (DPO) : a method that optimizes the policy directly from preference pairs without an explicit reward model or RL loop [32]. • Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) : applies RL with rule-based rewards on tasks such as mathematics and code whose answers can be verified automatically. • Scaling laws : empirical regularities of loss with respect to parameter count, data volume, and compute, exemplified by Kaplan et al. [119] and Hoffmann et al. (Chinchilla) [16]. • Mixture-of-experts (MoE) : replaces the dense feed-forward with sparsely activated expert subnetworks, exemplified by Switch Transformer [9]. All Stages of Large-Model Training 10 

• Length extrapolation : training short and inferring long, exemplified by ALiBi [129], RoPE and its extensions [11]. • Multimodal large language model (MLLM) : a large model that receives and (potentially) generates multiple modalities; representative architectures include CLIP [10], Flamingo [17], BLIP-2 [265], and LLaVA [28]. • Evidence grading (A–D) : this survey’s annotation of the reliability of literature sources, where A = peer-reviewed with public reproducibility artifacts, B = peer-reviewed, C = preprint/technical report/unconfirmed status, D = blog/model card/non-peer-reviewed source.

reading the survey, any method can be located as "a choice made along one dimension of one stage". 

## 2.3 The Semantic Ambiguity of "Post-Training" and the Convention Adopted Here 

In practice the term "post-training" has two usages, and mixing them leads to inaccurate lit-erature comparisons. The broad usage (adopted by [395][396]) refers to all optimization after pretraining, including SFT; the narrow usage refers only to the preference alignment and re-inforcement learning after SFT. The difference between the two is not a word game: if SFT is counted as post-training, the "share of compute spent on post-training" is significantly inflated, and the causal contributions of SFT and preference alignment become hard to separate. This survey adopts the following convention: the broad sense is used by default, but SFT is listed separately as Chapter 7 and distinguished from preference alignment/RL (Chapter 8); whenever the "post-training" conclusions of others are cited, their semantic scope is first determined before being included in comparisons, and annotated in the text when necessary. This convention runs through Chapters 7–9 and 11.

## 2.5 Checklist of Common Terminological Ambiguities 

The following ambiguities repeatedly cause comparison errors in practice; this survey unifies the conventions and recommends that readers annotate them as well when citing: All Stages of Large-Model Training 12 

• "Post-training" : the broad sense includes SFT, the narrow sense does not (Section 2.3). • "Alignment" : sometimes used loosely for all post-training; in this survey it refers only to the optimization toward human values and safety in the preference/RL stage. • "Parameter count" : for MoE, total parameters and activated parameters must be distin-guished; otherwise the model is not comparable to dense models [9][151]. • "Number of tokens" : whether multilingual/code/multimodal tokens are included and whether the figure is post-deduplication directly affect conclusions about data scale. • "Capability vs behavior" : capability refers to the knowledge and skills that can be elicited by zero-shot/few-shot prompting, while behavior refers to output style and compliance; post-training mainly changes behavior and indirectly affects the accessibility of capability (running through Chapter 11). • "Safety / alignment tax" : safety alignment improves refusal and honesty but may impair general capabilities; the "tax" is this capability loss (Chapters 8 and 14). The purpose of fixing these conventions is not to pursue literal uniformity but to expose comparisons of "same name, different meaning" explicitly, so that they can be corrected item by item.

# 3 Background and Formalization 

## 3.1 Formalization of Language Modeling and Multimodal Modeling 

To state the conclusion first: different objective functions (NTP, MLM, denoising, contrastive, matching) are essentially different choices regarding "where the supervision signal comes from and over which tokens/modalities the loss is computed"; understanding this point is a prerequisite for cross-stage comparison. 

Autoregressive language modeling (NTP) . Given a token sequence x = ( x1, . . . , x T ),minimize 

LNTP (θ) = −

> T

X

> t=1

log pθ(xt | x<t ).

The GPT series [121][141] and mainstream decoder-only models all adopt this objective. Its advantage is that the objective is consistent with inference (autoregressive generation); its disadvantage is that it exploits only unidirectional context. 

Masked language modeling (MLM) . Randomly masking a set of positions M , minimize 

LMLM = − P 

> t∈M

log pθ(xt | x\M ). BERT [47] uses it to obtain bidirectional representations, but the objective is inconsistent with generation, so it has since been used mostly for understanding-type tasks or as an encoder component. RoBERTa [48] shows that the effectiveness of MLM depends heavily on training details (dynamic masking, longer training, removing NSP). 

Sequence-to-sequence (seq2seq) . T5 [49] unifies all tasks into "text-to-text"; the objec-tive is still conditional NTP, but it distinguishes an encoder and a decoder. 

Contrastive and matching objectives . Multimodal alignment often employs contrastive learning (InfoNCE-type) and image-text matching. CLIP [10] uses a contrastive objective to align image and text embeddings; Flamingo [17] connects a frozen or semi-frozen visual encoder with a language model through cross-attention; LLaVA [28] uses a projector to map visual features into the LLM’s token space before performing (multimodal) NTP. It can be seen that multimodality did not invent an entirely new loss; rather, it combines NTP/contrastive/matching in cross-modal settings, which determines that the core issue of Chapter 10 remains the consistency problem of "objective–data–alignment". 

Denoising and multi-task objectives . The BART family unifies understanding and gen-eration via denoising autoencoding (reconstructing after shuffling/masking), and T5 [49] com-presses multiple tasks into a unified format via "text-to-text". The value of such objectives lies in merging heterogeneous tasks into a single loss, at the cost of introducing an additional variable—task prefixes and format design—which is precisely the precursor of the "template and loss masking" issue in Chapter 7. 

A summary of objective functions . The above objectives can be arranged along a spectrum according to the "source of the supervision signal": purely self-supervised (NTP/MLM/denoising) → weakly supervised contrast (image-text contrast) → strongly super-vised (instructions, preferences, verifiable rewards). The further along the spectrum, the closer the signal is to the target behavior, but the higher the acquisition cost and the more easily bias is injected. The controversies in the post-training chapters (synthetic data bias, reward hacking) are essentially all the cost of "a signal that is both strong and biased". 

## 3.2 Formal Segmentation of Training Stages and Notation System 

This paper uses a unified quadruple ⟨D , L, O, C⟩ to describe any training stage: D is the data distribution (including mixture and filtering), L is the loss/reward, O is the optimizer and schedule, and C is the compute and system constraints. Accordingly, the five stages can be written as: All Stages of Large-Model Training 14 

Data D

−→ Pretrain θ0

−→ SFT θ1

−→ Align/RL θ2

−→ Continual θ3

−→ .

where θ0 is the base model parameters, and θ1, θ 2, θ 3 are the parameters after SFT, alignment, and continual optimization, respectively. The forward constraint can be formalized as: the attainable performance upper bound of θk is a function of θk−1 and the current stage’s (D, L),that is, Cap (θk) ≤ fk

 Cap (θk−1); Dk, Lk

.

This inequality serves as a reminder that blindly increasing the number of post-training steps without simultaneously improving Dk (higher-quality alignment data) or θk−1 (a stronger base) will cause returns to saturate rapidly. 

Formalization of the objective in the preference/alignment stage . The goal of preference alignment is to bias the output distribution of the policy πθ toward preferred responses while not deviating excessively from the reference policy πref . RLHF jointly optimizes a reward 

rϕ and a KL regularization term: 

max  

> θ

Ex, y ∼πθ

rϕ(x, y ) − β KL  πθ ∥ πref 

,

where β controls the trade-off between "staying close to preferences" and "not drifting". RL-free methods such as DPO prove that this objective can be equivalently reduced to an implicit reward comparison over preference pairs, thereby eliminating the explicit reward model and the sampling loop [32]. This formalization uniformly explains the core tension of Chapter 8: the 

verifiability of the reward determines signal quality, while β and the sampling budget determine "how much is learned and how much drift occurs". 

Behavioral dimension and capability dimension . Let Beh (θ) denote the output behav-ior (style, compliance, refusal tendency); then post-training mainly changes Beh and indirectly affects Cap through the coupling of "behavior–attainable capability": good instruction following can "unlock" knowledge that the base model already possesses but has not been elicited, whereas over-alignment shrinks the exploration space and lowers the sampling upper bound (Pass@K). Accordingly, Chapter 11 proposes a "capability-behavior orthogonal axis" as an observational framework for cross-stage comparison. 

Formalization of the continual optimization stage . Continued pretraining approxi-mates restarting the pretraining of θ2 on a domain data distribution Ddom , and it must constrain general capabilities from degrading (Cap (θ3) ≳ Cap (θ2), preventing catastrophic forgetting). Compression (quantization/pruning/distillation) can be viewed as minimizing the output dis-tribution shift KL( pθ3 ∥pθ2 ) under parameter or activation precision constraints; model editing attempts to directly rewrite parameters on a specific set of knowledge while controlling "ripple effects" (untargeted rewriting). The common formal problem of these three types of operations is transformation under preservation constraints —which is also the basis for Chapter 9’s unified use of "preservation" as the primary axis of evaluation. 

## 3.3 Families of Evaluation Metrics and Dataset Classification 

Evaluation can be divided along two axes: intrinsic metrics versus extrinsic metrics , and 

understanding-type versus generation-type . Intrinsic metrics (perplexity, bits-per-byte) directly measure the model distribution; extrinsic metrics (accuracy, pass rate, human-evaluated win rate) measure downstream task performance. HELM [22] advocates "holistic evaluation" across multiple scenarios and metrics, and MMLU [7] covers knowledge across 57 disciplines; the two respectively represent the "broad coverage" and "knowledge-intensive" categories of benchmarks. Generation-type tasks (summarization, dialogue, code) usually require human or model review and are sensitive to prompts and decoding parameters. All Stages of Large-Model Training 15 

Key reminder: scores from different benchmarks cannot be directly added or directly com-pared across models unless data contamination control, prompts, and decoding protocols have been unified. Multimodal evaluation (e.g., visual question answering, image captioning) addition-ally superimposes modality alignment and hallucination issues, so the strength of its conclusions is usually lower than that of pure-text benchmarks (see Chapter 12 and Appendix E for details). 

The boundaries of intrinsic metrics . Intrinsic metrics such as perplexity and bits-per-byte facilitate comparison across runs but do not necessarily correspond to downstream capa-bilities: the data distribution, tokenizer granularity, and deduplication protocol all change their absolute values, so that "lower perplexity" does not imply "more usable". Therefore, intrin-sic metrics are suitable for "relative comparison within the same setting" but not for absolute assertions across works (Sections 6.3 and 12.2). 

Metric families for alignment and safety evaluation . Post-training introduces three categories of distinctive metrics: preference win rate (human- or model-evaluated; see the evaluation methodology of [22]), safety metrics (jailbreak success rate, over-refusal rate, hon-esty), and verifiable reasoning metrics (Pass@1 and Pass@K). The common difficulty of these three categories is that "the judge itself is biased": model judges may favor models from the same source, human evaluation is costly and of limited consistency, and Pass@K is sensitive to the sampling budget. Therefore, alignment conclusions especially need to report the judging protocol and sampling settings; otherwise, the values are not comparable (Chapters 8 and 12). 

Stage dependence of metric selection . The same model focuses on different metrics at different stages: pretraining looks at perplexity and downstream zero/few-shot; SFT looks at instruction following and format; preference/RL looks at win rate, safety, and Pass@K; continual optimization looks at preservation, throughput, and safety regression. Applying the metrics of one stage to another is a common source of evaluation mismatch (Chapter 11 will build a cross-stage metric comparison on this basis). 

Multilingual and fairness evaluation . The "average" of benchmark scores often masks the large variance across languages/groups: aggregate values dominated by English benchmarks overestimate multilingual capability, and likewise overall accuracy masks the degradation of low-resource groups. Therefore, any conclusion that claims "general capability" should report breakdown metrics by language/by group rather than only a mean (Section 12.7). This is also why this paper includes "fairness" as an evaluation dimension—it is not an add-on but a necessary condition for judging whether a conclusion holds. 

## 3.4 Notation for Compute and Cost 

To support cross-work comparison, this paper uniformly adopts the following protocol: the parameter count N (distinguishing total parameters from activated parameters, the latter being especially critical for MoE [9][151]); the number of training tokens D; the approximate training FLOPs C ≈ 6N D (a commonly used approximation for autoregressive training); and wall-clock time / GPU hours and peak GPU memory. Wherever cross-work comparison is involved, the main text provides the (N, D, C ) triple protocol as far as possible; when unavailable, it is marked 

[to be verified] . This convention serves to suppress the most common methodological error of "directly comparing numbers across datasets and compute budgets". At the system level, dense training scales equivalently through data parallelism, tensor paral-lelism, pipeline parallelism, and ZeRO [6]; the attention implementation (FlashAttention [135]) determines the memory and throughput ceiling for long sequences. These system constraints in turn limit the available batch size, sequence length, and parallelism strategy, thereby affect-ing optimization stability—which is precisely why the six-dimensional framework treats "system constraints" as a separate dimension. 

Origin and boundaries of the compute approximation . The origin of C ≈ 6N D 

is that the forward pass is approximately 2N D and the backward pass approximately 4N D 

(the backward computation is about twice the forward). This approximation applies to dense, All Stages of Large-Model Training 16 

autoregressive, single-pass training; for MoE, it must be replaced with the activated parameters 

Nact ; for multimodal models, the visual encoder and projector must be counted separately; and for long context, the quadratic term of attention must be included. Therefore, C should be regarded as an "order-of-magnitude estimate" rather than an exact value—which also explains why this paper insists on reporting compute as a "magnitude-range" rather than a single value (Chapters 11 and 13). 

Effective compute and MFU . From an engineering perspective, what matters more is "effective compute", i.e., the ratio of hardware peak to actual throughput (MFU). MFU depends on the parallelism strategy, batch size, sequence length, and communication overhead; the same model can differ by several times in MFU across different clusters. Therefore, "how many GPU-days were used for training" is not comparable across works unless MFU and the parallelism configuration are reported together (Section 6.6). 

Inference compute and test-time compute . Beyond training compute, infer-ence/sampling compute (especially RL rollout and long chain-of-thought reasoning) can no longer be ignored in the post-training era; denoting the sampling cost of RL as Crollout , the total cost of post-training also depends on the policy length, the number of samples, and the reward eval-uation overhead. This item is the formalization background of Open Problem 1 in Chapter 14. 

## 3.5 Formalization Assumptions and Limitations 

The notation system in this section rests on several simplifying assumptions; making them explicit is intended to prevent readers from mistaking the formal notation for a complete theory: First, the assumption that capability can be scalarized . Using Cap (·) to represent model capability and assuming its upper bound is constrained by preceding stages (Equation (3.2)) is an analytical tool rather than a proven law; "capability" is in fact multidimensional and task-dependent, and an upper bound along any single dimension is more credible than a single scalar. Second, the assumption that stages are separable . Dividing training into five stages facilitates analysis, but the interfaces between stages (data mixture, templates, objectives) of-ten interpenetrate; this paper explicitly annotates such coupling during cross-stage comparison (Chapter 11). Third, the transferability assumption of scaling laws . C ≈ 6N D and the Ka-plan/Chinchilla extrapolations both assume a relatively stable distribution; when data quality, mixture, or architecture changes significantly, the extrapolation fails (Section 6.3). Therefore, this paper annotates the applicable protocol for all scaling-law conclusions rather than treating them as universal constants. Fourth, the reproducibility assumption . All "comparisons" presuppose that protocols can be aligned; when alignment is impossible, this paper only states the method without giving numerical rankings (Section 4.3). Acknowledging these limitations, rather than hiding them, is more consistent with this paper’s "auditable" stance: formalization is meant to make assumptions testable, not to provide a sense of certainty. 

Formalization notation for multimodality . To facilitate the comparison in Chapter 10, this paper denotes the multimodal input as a set of modalities {x(1) , . . . , x (M )} (text, im-age, audio, etc.), whose unified representation is composed of a three-part "encoder–connector– backbone" composite: the encoder Em maps each modality to continuous features, the connector 

gm projects it into the backbone’s token space (or discretizes it and appends it to the vocab-ulary), and the backbone πθ then generates the target modality via (conditional) NTP. From this, the three major structural choices of multimodality can be formalized: whether the en-coder is frozen (training cost vs alignment quality), whether the connector is discrete or continuous (compression rate vs flexibility), and whether the backbone is shared across modalities (parameter efficiency vs interference). These three choices constitute the formal All Stages of Large-Model Training 17 

basis for the conclusion in Chapter 10 regarding "modality peripherals on a shared backbone", and they also show that multimodality does not depart from the five-stage framework of this paper but merely introduces an additional modality variable at each stage. 

# 4 Taxonomy 

## 4.1 Overview: Stage × Six Dimensions × Modality/Architecture 

Conclusion first: the taxonomy in this paper is a three-dimensional coordinate system— stage 

(five stages), six dimensions (data, objective, optimization, systems, evaluation, governance), and modality and architecture (text / multimodal; dense / sparse). Any method can be uniquely (or, at most, in a small number of places) positioned within this coordinate sys-tem, which makes heterogeneous work comparable across the board. ## 4.2 Definition of Taxonomy Dimensions and Notes on Orthogonality 

The stage dimension is a division by time / dependency order, answering "at which step of the training pipeline." The six dimensions are analytical perspectives internal to each stage, answering "which kind of choice was made at that step": data refers to the source and proportion of the training signal; objective refers to the loss / reward; optimization refers to the optimizer, schedule, and stability measures; systems refers to parallelism, GPU memory, and throughput constraints; evaluation refers to the means of measurement; and governance refers to safety, compliance, and fairness. The modality / architecture dimension characterizes the form of the model. Two places of non-strict orthogonality must be noted. First, "data" and "objective" are highly coupled in self-supervised stages (the masking strategy is both a data transformation and an objective design), and this paper explicitly points out the coupling points when compar-ing. Second, "optimization" and "systems" mutually constrain each other through batch size, precision (BF16/FP16/FP8), and parallelism granularity. Apart from these two places, the six dimensions may be regarded as orthogonal, so a "six-dimensional score" can be assigned to any method. 

Definition of the modality/architecture dimension. This dimension characterizes "what the model processes and with what structure": modality includes text, image, video, audio, speech, and interleaved multimodal; architecture includes dense and sparse (MoE), decoder-only and encoder-decoder. Both are continuous spectra rather than binary values—the interleaved-data ratio, the number of experts and routing sparsity, and whether visual/language parameters All Stages of Large-Model Training 18 

are shared all take continuous values. Therefore, when actually positioning a method, one should record the specific "modality combination + structural parameters" rather than merely attaching a "multimodal/sparse" label (Chapter 10 further subdivides multimodal architecture paradigms accordingly). 

An example of six-dimensional scoring. Taking some SFT method as an example, its scale may be: data (source and scale of the instruction set), objective (conditional NTP + mask-ing strategy), optimization (full-parameter or PEFT, learning rate), systems (GPU memory and multi-turn data layout), evaluation (instruction-following benchmarks), governance (synthetic-data risks). Scoring is not intended to "rate methods as high or low," but to expose which questions each work has actually answered when comparing—dimensions left unanswered are evidence gaps. 

## 4.3 Usage Notes: How to Compare Across Works 

When using this taxonomy to compare two works, the following order is recommended: first align the stage (avoid directly juxtaposing SFT results with RL results); then align the modality / architecture (text vs multimodal, dense vs sparse); then compare under the same six di-mensions (e.g., both look at the "objective function" or both look at "evaluation"); and finally verify whether the (N, D, C ) conventions are comparable. Wherever the conventions cannot be aligned, this paper uniformly refrains from giving numerical superiority/inferiority conclusions and instead states only the method and the evidence. Chapter 11 will use this procedure to sys-tematically compare three pairs of objects—"pretraining vs post-training," "text vs multimodal," and "LLM vs MLLM"—and provide an evidence-strength map (Figure 5). 

## 4.4 Relationship to Existing Taxonomy Frameworks 

Conclusion first: the taxonomy in this paper does not compete with existing frameworks, but rather provides a common coordinate system for them. The survey of five post-training paradigms [395] takes "fine-tuning/alignment/reasoning/efficiency/integration" as its axes, covering the in-terior of post-training but lacking cross-stage scope; the surveys of RL post-training [396][399] take algorithm families as their axes, going deep into a single stage; the data-centric multimodal survey [403] and the efficient multimodal survey [405] take modality and efficiency as their axes. The difference of this paper lies in explicitly introducing a stage axis and a six-dimensional axis , which allows all of the above frameworks to be "positioned"—for example, the "reasoning" paradigm in [395] can be positioned to the RLVR family in Chapter 8 of this paper, and the modality issues in [403] can be positioned to Chapter 10 and the data dimension. The practical value of this relationship is twofold: first, readers can use the coordinates of this paper to juxtapose the conclusions of several specialized surveys without confusing the stages; second, when different surveys give different conclusions about the same phenomenon, one can check whether they are genuinely comparable in terms of "stage/modality/compute conventions"—most apparent conflicts stem from this. 

## 4.5 Usage Boundaries and Common Misuses 

A taxonomy is an analytical tool, and misuse produces a spurious order. Three common misuses warrant vigilance: 

Misuse one: pushing orthogonality to an extreme. Section 4.2 has already noted two places of non-strict orthogonality (data–objective, optimization–systems); if one insists that the six dimensions do not affect one another, one arrives at the erroneous conclusion of "each independently optimal"—in reality, a change in data mixture alters the optimal vocabulary, and a change in parallelism granularity alters the usable batch size and thereby affects stability, both of which are cross-dimensional couplings. All Stages of Large-Model Training 19 

Misuse two: substituting stage division for causal attribution. Placing a method in the "pretraining" or "post-training" cell only indicates its position in the pipeline, not which variable its gains come from. Cross-stage gains are often jointly determined by data, objective, and compute, and attribution requires convention alignment and ablation following the procedure in Section 4.3. 

Misuse three: treating the modality/architecture dimension as a binary label. 

Text/multimodal and dense/sparse are not mutually exclusive binary values but continuous spec-tra (the interleaved-data ratio, the number of experts, and routing sparsity are all continuous). Binarizing continuous variables discards a great many real cases such as "partially multimodal" and "mildly sparse." 

## 4.6 From Taxonomy to Method-Family Map (Figure 3) 

Expanding the coordinates of the taxonomy into temporal evolution yields the method-family map (Figure 3), which annotates the maturity of method families in four colors: classic (foun-dational methods that have been widely validated), mainstream (the current default choice), 

frontier (promising but with newer evidence), and disputed (with unresolved disagreements). Examples by stage: • Data : heuristic filtering (classic) → DoReMi/DSIR data selection (mainstream) → data-mixture inference (frontier) → tokenizer saturation and synthetic-data collapse (disputed). • Pretraining : NTP (classic) → decoder-only + Chinchilla proportions (mainstream) →

MoE/FP8/long context (frontier) → Kaplan vs Chinchilla comparability (disputed). • SFT : human IFT (classic) → Self/Evol-Instruct (mainstream) → multimodal IFT (frontier). • Preference/RL : RLHF/PPO (classic) → the DPO family (mainstream) → GRPO/RLVR (frontier) → the debate over the Pass@K reasoning boundary (disputed). • Continual optimization : distillation/pruning (classic) → quantization-aware and model merging (mainstream) → model editing/machine unlearning (frontier/disputed). The continuation of Figure 3 is "from top-left to bottom-right, with disputed items always retaining a distinct color": it does not claim that the newest is the best, but rather that each family should be read together with its level of evidence. This map is used in tandem with the "evidence map (Figure 5)" in Chapter 11—the former gives the genealogy of methods, the latter gives the strength and gaps of the evidence. In summary, Chapters 2–4 together establish the analytical foundation of the entire book: terminology eliminates ambiguity, formalization provides notation and the boundaries of assump-tions, and the taxonomy supplies comparison coordinates. Without any one of the three, the cross-sectional comparisons in subsequent chapters would lose their basis; readers are therefore advised not to skip these three chapters even if they skim the method chapters. 

# 5 Data and Tokenization                    

> This chapter, together with Ch6, serves as a pilot for the dual-form "general framework + focused survey" format. Its full length unfolds according to the Stage 2 Section 8 tem-plate: definition and boundaries →inputs, outputs, and core problems →method fami-lies →subword tokenization →vocabulary and special tokens →multimodal tokenizer →
> representative-work table →key controversies →practical recommendations →cross-stage connections and open problems. Target length: 8,000–9,000 characters.

## 5.1 Definition and Boundaries 

Conclusion first: the data and tokenization stage addresses "what to feed, how to segment it, and in what proportions to feed it." It determines the information ceiling of pretraining and post-training, but does not directly determine optimization dynamics. Included : data col-lection and cleaning, exact/approximate/semantic deduplication, quality and safety filtering, All Stages of Large-Model Training 20 Continual Optimization

Figure 3: Method-family map—maturity annotated in four Morandi tones (sage = classic, misty blue = mainstream, clay = frontier, dusty rose = disputed) All Stages of Large-Model Training 21 

multilingual and domain mixing, data selection, curriculum and two-stage mixing, subword tok-enizers (BPE/WordPiece/Unigram/SentencePiece/tiktoken) and vocabulary design, and multi-modal tokenizers. Excluded : the human processes of annotation quality control (which belong to engineering management), and pure storage and retrieval systems. The boundary criterion is: anything that changes "the content of the training signal or the way it is segmented" is included; anything that only changes "how the signal is scheduled onto devices" belongs to the systems dimension (Chapter 6). 

Three categories of configuration for this stage (the minimal comparability stan-dard) : 1○ data sources and cleaning pipelines (composition of the raw corpus, deduplication strategy, filtering thresholds, whether synthetic data is included); 2○ mixing and curriculum (the weights of each source/domain, whether it is staged, whether it varies with scale); 3○ to-kenization and vocabulary (algorithm, vocabulary size, special tokens, multimodal placeholder scheme). If any one of these categories is missing, cross-work comparisons of data conclusions will fail—because a "dataset with the same name" differs enormously under different cleaning regimes (the crux of the controversy in Section 5.8). Each subsequent subsection of this chapter returns to these three configuration categories to annotate the evidence standard. 

Why the data dimension is the most easily underestimated. Models and optimization have clear mathematical forms and are convenient to publish; data pipelines, by contrast, are often engineering details that are hard to reproduce and are not disclosed. The result is a systematic bias: papers showcase models and objectives while concealing data recipes, making "performance improvements" difficult to attribute. This survey places data first among the six dimensions precisely because it is currently the largest and least transparent variable, and the place where the evidence gap is most concentrated (Appendix C/E). 

Interface with continual learning/domain adaptation : data and tokenization deter-mine not only general pretraining, but also the difficulty of "continued pretraining and domain adaptation" in Chapter 9. Domain corpora often need re-cleaning, deduplication, and re-mixing, and catastrophic forgetting must be prevented; this makes the data pipeline an activity that runs through the model’s entire lifecycle rather than a one-time upstream step (reverse empowerment, Figure 1). 

Calibration under the six-dimensional framework : the data dimension simultaneously touches the other five dimensions—objective (different objectives yield different marginal returns on different data, 6.2), optimization (data ordering affects the loss curve and stability, 6.6), systems (throughput bottlenecks in data loading and shuffling), evaluation (contamination and fairness audits, Chapter 12), and risk governance (PII/copyright/toxicity, Chapter 14). Data decisions are therefore inherently cross-dimensional decisions and cannot be optimized in isolation within the data dimension alone. 

Terminological clarification : in this chapter, "data" specifically refers to the training signal (tokens/samples) and does not include model parameters; "tokenization" refers to the process of mapping the raw signal into discrete/continuous tokens; what is called the "data recipe" downstream is precisely the complete combination of the three categories of configuration in Section 5.1. 

## 5.2 Inputs and Outputs, Core Problems 

The inputs are the raw corpus (web pages, books, code, dialogues, image-text pairs, video, etc.); the outputs are the cleaned dataset, the mixing scheme, and the tokenizer (including the vo-cabulary and merge rules). There are four core problems. First, how to trade off between scale and quality (the stricter the filtering, the cleaner the data but the lower the diversity) [71][87]. Second, how to remove overlap between the training set and the evaluation set (contamination) without erroneously deleting legitimate long-tail items [52]. Third, how to allocate vocabulary and mixing proportions for multiple languages and modalities, avoiding the systematic squeez-ing out of low-resource languages/modalities [51]. Fourth, whether the mixing should change All Stages of Large-Model Training 22 

dynamically with model scale and training stage [86][407]. 

Data-constrained vs. data-abundant is the premise that divides all data strategies. General English web corpora have been fully mined, and frontier training is mostly in the "data-constrained" regime (the growth rate of high-quality tokens lags behind the growth rate of compute); here the strategic focus shifts from "scaling up" to "improving quality, deduplicat-ing, synthesizing, and reusing." In low-resource languages, specialized domains, and multimodal settings, however, the situation remains "data-scarce," with the focus on collection and con-struction. The optimal strategies of the two are opposite, and mixing them leads to erroneous conclusions—this is the root of Section 5.8 and open problem 2. 

The data–evaluation loop : data decisions (filtering, mixing, deduplication) can only be validated through evaluation, and evaluation itself depends on data (benchmarks, contamination audits). Hence the "correctness" of the data dimension is relative and depends on the evaluation standard; without the contamination and unfairness analysis of Chapter 12, one cannot judge the true value of a data recipe. 

Two types of contamination : direct overlap between the training set and the evaluation set (the same text) and indirect contamination (question style/answer patterns are implicitly learned, or the evaluation set is generated by a model and then misused). Direct overlap can be detected by exact/approximate deduplication [52]; indirect contamination is difficult to audit and intensifies with model scale, making it the category with the weakest current evidence yet the most severe impact. Therefore, any conclusion that "a model improves on benchmark X" must pass a contamination audit before it can be accepted (Section 12.3). 

The priority of the four questions shifts with the stage : in the pretraining stage, "scale vs. quality" is the main line; in the post-training stage, "contamination and fairness" gain weight (because instruction data is closer to the evaluation distribution); in the multimodal stage, "vocabulary/modality mixing" becomes a newly added main line. Thus there is no one-time optimal data scheme, only local optima oriented toward a specific stage and evaluation objective. 

Realistic estimates of data volume : both the total order of magnitude of available high-quality public text and the actual number of tokens trained by various models suffer from a large number of inconsistent standards (whether duplicates are included, whether multiple languages are counted, whether code is counted), causing conclusions such as "A uses more data than B" to be frequently incomparable. When comparing data volume across works, one should standardize on the "deduplicated, vocabulary-based token count" standard and note whether multimodal tokens are included. 

The conflict between deduplication and mixing : aggressive deduplication reduces the usable volume of certain sources (forums, templated text, etc.), thereby forcing the mixing to tilt toward "less-duplicated but more official" sources, potentially losing colloquial and diverse styles. Therefore the deduplication threshold and the mixing weights should be evaluated jointly; tuning either in isolation sacrifices the other objective (yet another instance of six-dimensional coupling). 

The quantitative relationship between scale and quality : under data constraints, the marginal return of scaling up diminishes while the marginal return of improving quality rises; the crossover point moves with the compute budget ([87]), so "add data or clean data" should be treated as a continuous trade-off rather than a binary choice. 

## 5.3 Classification of Method Families 

Along the six dimensions, data and tokenization methods can be grouped into five families: • Cleaning and deduplication : heuristic filtering (language identification, perplexity, re-peated n-gram removal), exact deduplication (hashing), approximate deduplication (Min-Hash/LSH), and semantic deduplication. Representative evidence: the C4 documentation All Stages of Large-Model Training 23 

study reveals the commercial and bias costs of heuristic filtering [56]; exact/approximate dedu-plication is shown to reduce memorization and improve downstream performance [13]. • Quality and safety filtering : quality classifiers, toxicity filtering, and privacy (PII) and copyright handling. The datasheet work of Pile emphasizes the auditability of data sources and uses [61]; the quantification of the effects of quality, timeliness, domain, and toxicity on training is given in [71]. • Mixing and data selection : weighting by source/domain, importance resampling DSIR [67], learnability prioritization (such as the RHO-LOSS idea) [63], the minimax mixing optimization of DoReMi [70], data mixing laws [86], skill-driven mixing Skill-it [74], the compute-dependent scaling law of data filtering [87], and two-stage (general first, then high-quality) mixing [407]. • Tokenizers and vocabulary : BPE [1], WordPiece (BERT) [47], Unigram/SentencePiece [4][5], token-free (Canine [55], ByT5 [57]); and inferring the training data mixture from the merge table [406]. • Multimodal tokenizers : discretization of vision/audio (VQ-VAE [3], VQGAN [8]), visual encoders (CLIP [10], SigLIP [26], SigLIP2 [46], DINOv2 [27]), and multimodal placehold-ers/alignment (Flamingo [17], LLaVA [28], Qwen-VL [277]). 

The five families are not a pipeline but a coupling : the granularity of deduplication affects the number of usable samples, thereby changing the optimal mixing; the filtering intensity changes the data distribution, thereby changing the optimal vocabulary (subword frequencies); the vocabulary in turn affects the information per token, and hence the "effective token count" and the mixing budget. Treating data work as "one-time preprocessing" is therefore a com-mon misconception—in reality it is a closed loop repeatedly adjusted along with training and evaluation (the reverse-empowerment arrow in Figure 1). 

Safety, privacy, and copyright : quality filtering simultaneously performs a compliance function—removing PII, respecting robots/licensing, and handling copyrighted text. These pro-cesses affect the usable corpus volume and distribution, yet there is almost no standardized public reporting standard; differences across jurisdictions also blur the boundary of "trainable data." A datasheet such as Pile’s [61] provides an auditable starting point, but transparency in large-scale actual execution remains low (a governance issue, Chapter 14). 

Differences in evidence strength : deduplication [13], mixing laws [86][87], and tokenizer effects [51] have relatively strong Grade-A evidence; the "optimal thresholds" of quality classifiers and semantic deduplication mostly come from single-point studies (B/C); and the "largest-scale real data recipes" are almost entirely undisclosed (C or blank). This chapter can therefore draw mechanistic conclusions, but recipe-level conclusions are not transferable. 

The genealogy of deduplication techniques : exact deduplication uses document-level hashing (SHA on normalized text, etc.) to remove exact duplicates; approximate deduplication uses MinHash + LSH to find near-duplicates with high Jaccard similarity in sublinear time; semantic deduplication uses embedding similarity to capture samples that are worded differently but semantically duplicated. The granularity (document/passage/sentence) and thresholds of the three jointly determine "how much to delete and what to keep": too coarse misses passage-level duplication in long documents (a contamination risk), while too fine erroneously deletes legitimate templated text (code, formulas). The empirical practice is "multi-granularity com-bination + per-source thresholds," but public threshold–benefit curves are lacking, making this Grade-B/C evidence. 

Technical differences among mixing methods : DoReMi [70] uses a minimax (group DRO) idea to automatically adjust per-domain weights on a proxy model, minimizing the worst-domain loss; DSIR [67] resamples by the importance of hashed n-grams, tilting a general corpus toward its target distribution without training a model; Skill-it [74] builds a mixing order from the learning dependencies among skills, favoring curriculum learning; and data mixing laws [86] directly fit the "mixing–validation loss" relation as a predictor for extrapolation across scales. The four address different facets of the same problem (weights, direction, order, predictability) All Stages of Large-Model Training 24 

and are often combined in practice, but public evidence on the optimal combination is lacking. 

The composition of heuristic filtering : a typical pipeline performs, in sequence, language identification, boilerplate removal (navigation bars/disclaimers), removal of overly short/overly long samples, a repeated n-gram ratio threshold, and perplexity filtering (scored by a reference LM). Each item is simultaneously an operation of "purification" and "loss of diversity," and the thresholds are highly empirical; the C4 analysis [56] shows that such filtering systematically affects topic and group distributions, so the filtering configuration should be regarded as a design choice requiring a fairness audit rather than a neutral cleaning step. 

Curriculum learning and two-stage training : curriculum learning (easy first, then hard; ordered by skill dependencies) and two-stage mixing (first building a foundation with general corpora, then finishing with high-quality corpora) represent data strategies along the "time" axis. Skill-it [74] and the two-stage report [407] provide directional evidence, but "when to switch, and whether the benefit of switching is sensitive to model scale" lacks systematic study (mostly Grade B/C). One interpretable mechanism is that later high-quality data can "cleanse" the distribution bias introduced by earlier low-quality data, approximating a form of implicit fine-tuning. 

Why the optimal mixing is hard to reproduce : mixing experiments are highly sensitive to proxy model scale, training steps, evaluation set, and random seed, and the optimal mixing at small scale may reverse at large scale (data mixing laws [86] were proposed precisely to provide an extrapolation framework). Thus "we used an A:B:C mixing" is not itself a reusable conclusion; it must be reported together with the extrapolation model and the validation standard. 

How to evaluate a data decision : because of the data–evaluation loop (5.2), evaluating a data strategy requires three types of control: 1○ fixing the model and compute and changing only the data (ablation); 2○ fixing the data and sweeping the scale (testing the scale robustness of the conclusion); 3○ validating under multiple evaluation sets and contamination audits (to prevent overfitting to a single benchmark). If any one is missing, the data conclusion may be "accidentally valid under a specific setting"—this is the minimum threshold for advancing data work from experience to accumulable knowledge. 

## 5.4 Subword Tokenization Details 

BPE [1] builds the vocabulary by merging high-frequency byte/character pairs and is the default choice of the GPT series; it is fairly robust to multiple languages and unknown words, but its coverage is uneven for morphologically rich languages and languages sensitive to typography (no-space segmentation). WordPiece (BERT [47]) selects merge pairs by likelihood gain rather than frequency. Unigram / SentencePiece [4][5] model directly on raw text (including spaces), avoid-ing the unfairness of pre-tokenization toward CJK and multilingual text, and support subword regularization (randomly sampling tokenizations during training to mitigate vocabulary bias). Empirical studies show that the choice of tokenizer has a substantial and source-dependent ef-fect on the monolingual performance of multilingual models [51], indicating that the assumption "tokenization is neutral preprocessing" does not hold. 

Variants and engineering details of BPE : byte-level BPE (since GPT-2) uses 256 bytes as the initial alphabet and naturally handles arbitrary Unicode; the cost is that the same char-acter may produce multiple tokens due to encoding, which is especially uneconomical for CJK (a single Chinese character may occupy 2–3 bytes). Implementations such as tiktoken accelerate BPE to the point of handling massive corpora, allowing the vocabulary to be trained directly on large-scale data. There are also engineering conventions regarding merge order, case handling, and digit splitting; although these conventions do not change the algorithm, they significantly change token efficiency and downstream performance—yet another source of opacity. 

Per-token compression rate (fertility) : the number of tokens produced by the same sentence under different languages/tokenizers can differ by several times, directly determining the "effective context" and inference cost under an equal budget. Low-resource languages have All Stages of Large-Model Training 25 

higher fertility due to insufficient vocabulary coverage, so under an equal token budget they carry less actual information—a numerical manifestation of the multilingual fairness problem and the mechanistic explanation of the conclusion in [51]. When evaluating a tokenization scheme, the fertility of each language should be reported explicitly rather than only the global average. 

Theoretical differences among the three subword algorithms : BPE is a greedy fre-quency merge aiming at the shortest encoding (it does not explicitly model probabilities); Word-Piece uses the criterion of "largest likelihood gain after merging," approaching the local opti-mum of a language model; Unigram first posits a subword probability model, then prunes the vocabulary via EM, theoretically yielding the maximum a posteriori segmentation and naturally supporting subword regularization. The difference among the three is not one of algorithmic superiority, but of "whether the segmentation probability is explicitly modeled"—this explains why low-resource languages are more sensitive to the choice of algorithm ([51][4][5]). 

The segmentation challenge of CJK and morphologically rich languages : CJK has no spaces and blurry word boundaries, so byte-level BPE often splits common Chinese characters into multiple bytes and splits words into single characters; morphologically rich languages (such as Finnish and Turkish) suffer from overly long subwords that force fallback. Both raise fertil-ity and squeeze the effective context. The native-text modeling and subword regularization of SentencePiece/Unigram can mitigate this, but the optimal strategy still depends on the specific language and data volume, and no universal conclusion exists yet [51][4][5]. 

The token-free route. ByT5 [57] trains directly on UTF-8 bytes, and Canine [55] uses a character-level encoder; both avoid vocabulary bias and trade longer sequences for language neutrality, at the cost of increased sequence length and compute. This constitutes a long-standing axis of controversy—"discrete subwords vs. bytes/characters" (5.8). 

Inferring the data mixture from the merge table. Recent work shows that BPE’s merge rules and vocabulary carry a "fingerprint" of the training corpus distribution, from which the data mixture of an undisclosed model can be reverse-engineered [406]. This has a dual implication for data transparency auditing: it is both a tool for verifiability and a hint that the vocabulary itself may leak the composition of the training data. Its methodological significance extends beyond tokenization itself—it provides an operational entry point for "data auditing of closed-source models," and is directly related to the contamination issues of Chapter 12 and the governance issues of Chapter 14. 

## 5.5 Vocabulary Design, Multilingual Fairness, and Dialogue/Tool Tokens 

The size and composition of the vocabulary directly affect the information per token and the inference cost for multiple languages: too small a vocabulary leads to frequent fallback to bytes, while too large a vocabulary dilutes statistics and enlarges the output layer. Multilingual fairness is therefore partly determined by the vocabulary, not solely by the data mixing [51]. In dialogue and agent scenarios, additional conventions are needed for the role/separator control tokens in the chat template and for tool-call tokens (such as function names and argument boundary markers); the design of these special tokens affects the stability and generalization of the SFT and RL stages, and improper design can cause format compliance to fail when migrating across templates (see Chapters 7 and 12 for details). 

The trade-off in vocabulary size. Enlarging the vocabulary shortens the sequence length and reduces the pressure on attention and positional encoding, but the output projection layer (V × d) and the embedding table grow linearly in parameter count with the vocabulary, and rare subwords in data-constrained languages are insufficiently trained. There exists an implicit optimum related to data scale and language distribution, but public systematic evidence is limited (mostly Grade-B/C single-point reports). The common practice in multilingual settings is to "allocate a token budget per language and explicitly measure the token compression rate and monolingual performance of each language" [51], rather than pursuing a single global vocabulary optimum. All Stages of Large-Model Training 26 

Multilingual vocabulary construction practice : a common approach is to allocate vo-cabulary capacity according to the expected data proportion of each language/script, and after joint training to re-check using the per-language token compression rate and monolingual per-plexity; warm-starting (initializing from an existing vocabulary) reduces training cost but inherits its bias. The key risk is the "silent degradation of majority languages"—global average metrics mask the severe squeezing of low-resource languages, so evaluation must report per language (Chapter 12). 

Vocabulary and inference economics : a larger vocabulary improves the compression rate and reduces the number of generation steps, but drives up the cost of softmax and embeddings and may harm the sampling quality of rare subwords. On-device deployment is especially sen-sitive to vocabulary size (the embedding table consumes GPU memory/RAM). The vocabulary is therefore the intersection of "training efficiency—inference cost—multilingual fairness," and must be decided jointly under the target deployment conditions rather than by maximizing a single metric in isolation. 

The contractualization of special tokens. Chat templates and tool-call tokens are es-sentially "interfaces between the model and external protocols"; once fixed in the pretraining stage, they are difficult to change: changing them breaks already-trained patterns and causes cross-version incompatibility. They should therefore be treated as versioned contracts rather than ordinary configuration, maintaining a single source of truth across the data pipeline, SFT data, and the evaluation toolchain; cross-stage inconsistency is a common root cause of format-compliance failure and degraded instruction generalization (Chapters 7 and 12). 

The semantic burden of tool-call tokens : encoding function names and argument bound-aries as ordinary subwords (rather than dedicated tokens) leads to unstable parsing, especially when arguments contain arbitrary strings. A more robust approach is to use structured separa-tors + explicit schema constraints, and to treat the stability of the schema as part of the data contract (Chapters 7 and 13). 

## 5.6 Multimodal Tokenizer 

Multimodal training expands the tokenizer problem from "text segmentation" to "cross-modal discrete/continuous representation." In vision there are two routes: discrete tokens (VQ-VAE [3], VQGAN [8], which quantize images into a codebook) and continuous features + projec-tion (CLIP [10], SigLIP2 [46], DINOv2 [27] encoding followed by a projector into the LLM, as in LLaVA [28], Flamingo [17]). Audio/speech likewise has both discrete codec and continuous feature routes. Interleaved image-text data work such as OBELISC [273] shows that the "place-holder + cross-attention + alignment objective" design of multimodal tokens is strongly coupled with the data format. Unifying the optimization objectives of discrete tokens and continuous tokens remains an open problem (Chapter 10, open problem 3). 

Resolution and token budget. The "token economy" of images/video is a core constraint of multimodal training: higher resolution yields finer visual understanding but a surge in token count (roughly growing with area), and video even more so (adding linearly with the number of frames). This has given rise to schemes that trade token efficiency for capability, such as "arbitrary resolution + dynamic segmentation" (Qwen2-VL [288]) and lightweight compressors (Q-Former, BLIP-2 [265]). The trade-off of these schemes between "fine-grained recognition" and "long-video understanding" currently lacks unified evaluation, with evidence mostly at Grade C/B. 

Data format is the tokenizer. Multimodal token schemes cannot be optimized inde-pendently of the data format: the character equivalence of image-text pairs, the proportion of interleaved image-text, and the granularity of inter-modal alignment all change "how much in-formation one visual token represents." This again shows that data and tokenization are two sides of the same problem (Chapter 10). 

The token economy of video/audio is even more extreme : the token count of video All Stages of Large-Model Training 27 

grows in a coupled manner with the frame count and resolution, while audio faces a trade-off between "frame rate vs. semantic units" (continuous features vs. discrete codebooks). Current mainstream multimodal models are trained with "images as the mainstay + a small amount of video," and both long-range temporal modeling of video and fine-grained alignment of audio lack public scaling laws, constituting an evidence gap (Chapter 10). 

Attempts at a unified multimodal vocabulary : encoding images/audio into a token space isomorphic to text (a unified vocabulary) can simplify sequence modeling and reuse the optimization and system stack on the text side, but discretizing a continuous signal without losing detail has undetermined costs and benefits (the two lines of VQ-type [3][8] and continuous projection [10][28] coexist). This is one of the core open problems of Chapter 10. 

The quality dimension of multimodal alignment data : the quality of image-text pairs is not just clarity and resolution, but also the strength of image-text relevance (weakly related pairs teach the model that "image and text do not constrain each other"), annotation bias (regional/cultural bias is amplified), and safety content. Large-scale automatic filtering of images and text (CLIP scores, NSFW, caption quality) itself introduces bias, and combined with the multimodal evaluation sensitivity of Chapter 12, makes multimodal data problems harder to audit than pure text. 

The coupling between multimodal token schemes and objectives : the discrete-token route (VQ [3][8]) naturally fits the autoregressive objective of "predicting the next visual token" and can be unified with text NTP; the continuous-projection route ([10][28]) requires an additional alignment loss (contrastive/matching) in parallel with text NTP. Thus "which token to choose" is in fact choosing "which objective to train with," and the two cannot be optimized separately—again confirming the judgment that "data format is the tokenizer."
