Reading guide : the "Evidence" column of the table uses this survey’s unified grading; any "recipe-level" conclusion (a specific threshold, a specific mixing value) cannot be directly transferred across datasets even if it comes from a Grade-A paper (Section 3.4). The table should be used as a "mechanism index"—choose methods by mechanism, choose parameters by local experiments. 

## 5.8 Key Controversies: Tokenizer Neutrality, Data-Quality Transferability, and Synthetic Bootstrapping 

This chapter converges on three controversies, each annotated as "consensus vs. remaining disagreement." First, whether tokenization is merely lossless preprocessing . The consensus is that tokenization significantly changes token efficiency and multilingual performance ([51][406]). The disagreement concerns "whether tokenization can be decoupled": one side holds that the to-kenizer must be co-designed with the objective and data [51][406]; the other side’s token-free work [55][57] shows that byte/character-level modeling can achieve comparable or even better robustness, suggesting that the reliance on subwords is partly an engineering choice. There is currently insufficient evidence to determine "which route is generally better under a given compute budget," but it can be confirmed that for low-resource and morphologically complex languages, differences in vocabulary and tokenization are amplified into measurable performance gaps [51]. This is the direct source of open problem 4. Second, the transferability of data quality (echoing Section 6.4). The consensus is that quality affects convergence; the disagreement concerns "whether high-quality data allows reaching an equivalent loss with fewer tokens," i.e., whether the Chinchilla constant shifts globally with data quality. The filtering scaling law [87] and data mixing laws [86] support the idea that "optimality depends on compute/quality," but there is as yet no extrapolation formula that takes "data quality" as an explicit variable and covers multiple scales. Existing conclusions mostly come from single-point studies (Grade B/C), so any claim of the form "data quality can substitute for 2 × the token count" should be marked as pending verification. Third, the bootstrapping boundary of synthetic data . The consensus is that synthetic data can supplement scarce domains and "missing intermediate steps" (such as reasoning traces); the disagreement concerns whether long-term iteration leads to capability/diversity collapse All Stages of Large-Model Training 29 

(the model-collapse risk). Shumailov et al. [411] provide rigorous theoretical and empirical evidence showing that unconstrained recursive training on synthetic data inexorably triggers early loss of distribution tails and irreversible variance collapse (Model Collapse, Grade A evidence), establishing the indispensable role of real-data anchors; determining safe mixture ratios and quality filters in industrial pipelines remains an active challenge (Open Problem 11). Any claim that "purely synthetic data can losslessly replace real data" should therefore be treated with caution. 

The common root of the three controversies : tokenizer neutrality, quality transferabil-ity, and synthetic bootstrapping essentially all point to the same question— at what granu-larity data carries information, and how the value of that information is quantified .If "effective information content" could be treated as a first-class quantity alongside the token count (the interface of Section 5.10), all three controversies could be expressed within a unified framework as "the benefit curve per unit of effective information." 

The practical implications of the controversies : the three controversies are not purely academic—they directly determine budget allocation: if the tokenization effect is underestimated, insufficient investment is made in the vocabulary for multiple languages; if quality transferability is overestimated, one may over-filter and lose diversity; if synthetic bootstrapping is overesti-mated, one may fall into a self-loop of low-quality data. The "pending verification" annotation of this section is therefore not conservatism, but a guard against erroneous resource investment. 

## 5.9 Practical Recommendations 

1. Deduplication takes priority over adding data : when the budget is limited, the marginal return of approximate deduplication and contamination cleaning is usually higher than con-tinuing to expand homogeneous corpora [13][52]. 2. Filtering is not better the stricter it is : the optimal filtering intensity varies with the compute budget [87][71], and filtering should be treated as a tunable item on par with N, D .3. Mixing must be dynamic : a single static mixing will be defeated by differences in scale and stage; consider two-stage or mixing-law-guided mixing [86][407][74]. 4. Orient the vocabulary toward the target language/modality : in multilingual settings use SentencePiece/Unigram and explicitly evaluate the per-language token cost [51]; in multi-modal settings first decide "discrete vs. continuous," then design placeholders and alignment objectives [10][28]. 5. Contractualize special tokens : chat templates and tool-call tokens require version man-agement (a single source of truth) to avoid cross-stage inconsistency (Chapters 7 and 12). 6. Treat data as an auditable asset : register sources, filtering thresholds, deduplication methods, and mixing (Appendix D), and perform contamination audits on key benchmarks [61][52]. 7. When data-constrained, improve quality before synthesizing : under the data-constrained regime, prioritize deduplication, quality improvement, and domain rebalancing, and carefully assess the bootstrapping benefit and capability-collapse risk of synthetic data (open problem 11). 8. Publish a data card : use a structure similar to the Pile datasheet to register sources, licenses, filtering rules, deduplication methods, and known biases [61], making data decisions auditable, reusable, and accountable. 9. Isolate and version the vocabulary and data contract : bind the tokenizer, vocabulary, chat template, and data snapshot in a versioned way, so that any single training run can be traced back to the exact input contract (the reproducibility checklist in Appendix D). All Stages of Large-Model Training 30 

## 5.10 Cross-Stage Connections and Open Problems 

Connections : the quality, mixing, and vocabulary of data and tokenization directly determine the achievable loss of pretraining in Chapter 6 and the data foundation for alignment in Chapters 7–8; conversely, the contamination and unfairness exposed by evaluation in Chapter 12 flow back to correct this chapter’s methods (reverse empowerment). Open problems : how data quality changes the optimal token/parameter ratio (does Chinchilla shift with data quality, problem 2); whether synthetic-data bootstrapping has a capability-collapse critical point (problem 11); the quantifiable causality between vocabulary design and multilingual fairness (problem 4); whether a scaling law exists for the mixing of the four multimodal modalities (image/text/video/audio) (newly added, evidence gap). See Appendix F for details. 

An actionable research design example : for problem 2 (quality transferability), one can fix the token budget, pass a corpus through multiple levels of filtering intensity to generate multiple equal-token subsets, and measure whether the loss–compute curve shifts as a whole; for problem 11 (synthetic collapse), one can, at a controlled small scale, sweep the "real–synthetic" iteration ratio and examine when diversity decline and capability regression appear; for problem 4 (vocabulary causality), one can fix the data and compute, change only the tokenizer and vocabulary, and measure monolingual performance and per-token compression rate on several low-resource languages. All three can be completed within moderate compute, with reporting standards following Appendix D. 

Summary of the evidence distribution in this chapter : for Ch5, the method-type work (deduplication, mixing, tokenization) has relatively thick Grade-A/B evidence, but "the largest-scale real data recipes," "multimodal mixing laws," and "long-term effects of synthetic data" are almost entirely Grade C or blank. This distribution means that this chapter can give robust mechanistic conclusions (deduplication is effective, filtering depends on compute, tokenization affects fairness), but specific recipes are not transferable —any claim of the form "some model’s secret recipe" should return to evidence grading and standard re-verification. 

The quantitative interface with Chapter 6 : data quality enters the scaling law through the "effective token count"—under the same nominal tokens, deduplication and quality improve-ment raise the effective information density, equivalent to relaxing the data budget. The data recipe and the optimal (N, D ) mixing are therefore a joint optimization problem and cannot be optimized separately (Section 6.4). When evaluating any data strategy, the "effective token" standard should be reported rather than only nominal tokens; otherwise the data volume will be systematically overestimated and quality problems underestimated. 

The interface with post-training data in Chapters 7–8 : this chapter deals with self-supervised data; the instruction/preference data of Chapters 7–8 is superimposed on top of it. The two share the vocabulary, chat template, and contamination risk, and the distribution of post-training data (task type, difficulty, safety) should be constructed in a targeted way based on the gaps exposed by pretraining (reverse empowerment, Figure 1). The data pipeline should therefore run through "pretraining—SFT—alignment" rather than being optimized in isolated segments. This chapter, together with Ch6, forms the data foundation of the subsequent chapters, and its strength of conclusions (mechanistically robust, recipes non-transferable) also determines the prudence with which subsequent chapters should cite data-type claims. 

# 6 Pretraining  

> This chapter is a pilot of the "general framework + focused survey (sub-survey)" dual form: the full length unfolds according to the Phase 2, Section 8 template, so that Ch5/Ch8/Ch9 can directly reuse this writing style and length yardstick. Target length: 8,000–9,000 char-acters. All Stages of Large-Model Training 31

## 6.1 Definition and Boundaries 

Conclusion first: pretraining acquires general representations and knowledge on large-scale cor-pora via self-supervised objectives, producing a "base model" θ0; it determines the upper bound of post-training capability, rather than final alignment quality. Included : objective-function families (6.2), architecture and MoE (6.3), scaling laws (6.4), optimization and stability (6.5), distributed systems (6.6), long-context training and extrapolation (6.7), and multimodal pre-training (6.8). Not included : data cleaning and tokenization (Chapter 5), instruction and preference data (Chapters 7–8), and pre-deployment compression (Chapter 9). The boundary criterion is "whether it proceeds without task-label signals": whenever the loss comes from unlabeled data itself, it is pretraining. 

Inputs and outputs. The input is a token sequence (text) or multimodal tokens/features; the output is the base-model parameters θ0 together with the accompanying vocabulary, po-sitional encoding, and (optionally) expert routing. The commonly used proxies for evaluating pretraining quality are downstream zero/few-shot performance [121] and perplexity, but the cor-relation between the two is affected by evaluation contamination (Chapter 12). 

Core questions. This chapter answers four key questions: how the choice of objective function affects subsequent transferability (6.2); what the optimal ratio of parameters to tokens is under a given compute budget, and how data quality perturbs that ratio (6.4); whether sparsification (MoE), long context, and multimodality are merely "incremental engineering" or will change the scaling laws (6.3/6.7/6.8); and how the joint design of optimization and systems determines training stability (6.5/6.6). 

## 6.2 Objective-Function Families 

Autoregressive language modeling (NTP) is currently the absolute mainstream [121][140][141], defined in Section 3.1: token-by-token conditional maximum likelihood. NTP’s three advantages determine its dominance: it is fully consistent with autoregressive inference tasks and requires no additional objective adaptation; it can turn arbitrary text (code, dialogue, scripts) into training signals, so data scaling has no bottleneck; and the optimization itself is a stable maximum-likelihood procedure, facilitating scaling extrapolation. Its cost is unidirectional context and the short-sightedness of the "next token" objective, which partly gave rise to the competing lines of masked and denoising objectives. Masked language modeling (MLM) targets random masking and bidirectional prediction [47]; its advantage is obtaining bidirectional representations, which benefits understanding-type tasks, but its drawbacks are equally clear: the objective is inconsistent with generation, and the predicted-token ratio and masking strategy introduce hyperparameters. RoBERTa’s [48] system-atic experiments show that MLM’s performance depends heavily on training details (dynamic masking, longer training, removing NSP), which indicates both that MLM has a large tuning space and that its conclusions have higher "setting sensitivity" than NTP. The sequence-to-sequence (seq2seq) line, represented by T5 [49], unifies all tasks as "text-to-text"; it is essentially still conditional NTP but explicitly separates encoder and decoder. It still has structural advantages in scenarios requiring explicit conditioning of the input (summariza-tion, translation, controlled generation), but in general dialogue and tool-use scenarios it yields to decoder-only. 

Multimodal objectives : multimodal pretraining does not invent new losses but combines "contrastive + matching + NTP." CLIP [10] aligns the embedding space with an image-text con-trastive objective, establishing the "representation pretraining" paradigm; Flamingo [17] places vision into the language model via a pretrained vision encoder + cross-attention + gating; LLaVA [28], BLIP-2 [265], and Qwen-VL [277] map visual features into the LLM’s token space via a projector and then continue NTP. These three modes are essentially different ratios of "alignment signal to language loss" (Chapter 10), so discussing "NTP vs. contrastive" across modalities is All Stages of Large-Model Training 32 

a false dichotomy. 

Summary : the choice of objective function is not merely "what to predict"; it also deter-mines what kind of data yields the greatest marginal benefit and what kind of evaluation can reflect training quality. When comparing different objectives across works, one must fix the data scale and level of purification; otherwise the differences (mostly Grade-B evidence) will be drowned by confounding variables. 

Practical guidance for objective selection : when bidirectional understanding represen-tations are needed (retrieval, classification, embedding), contrastive or MLM-type objectives still have value; when the goal is to align with human instructions and support general generation and tool use, autoregressive NTP is the default and necessary foundation—because post-training (Chapters 7–8) is built on a generative conditional distribution. In other words, the choice of pre-training objective is partly constrained by the downstream alignment paradigm, another example of the "objective–evaluation–downstream" coupling. Beyond NTP, MLM, and seq2seq, several transitional objectives also exist. Prefix LM lets the prefix of a sequence be predicted with bidirectional context and the suffix autoregressively, lying between MLM and NTP; it was once used in multi-task pretraining to accommodate both generation and understanding. Denoising autoencoding takes "restoring text under arbitrary noise (span masking, text dropout, order shuffling)" as its objective, a general framework for the span-restoration objective of the T5 [49] family; it also shows that the actual implementation of the seq2seq objective depends heavily on the form of the corruption function. The significance of understanding these transitional objectives is that they all adjust two continuous variables— the visible range of context and the granularity of prediction —and these two variables directly rewrite the marginal benefit of data: this is precisely the mechanism coupling the "objective function" with "data mixture" in Chapter 5 (a coupling point of the six-dimensional framework). 

Objective–evaluation consistency is a hidden constraint when choosing an objective: whether an objective succeeds is ultimately still defined by downstream evaluation (Chapter 12). In the MLM era, "high scores on understanding benchmarks" defined success; in the NTP era, "few-shot and instruction following" defined success. This brings a methodological reminder: if one evaluates only on benchmarks that are "co-origin" with the objective, one will systemati-cally overestimate that objective. Cross-objective comparisons must use third-party evaluations independent of the objective; otherwise one falls into circular reasoning. 

## 6.3 Architecture: decoder-only, encoder-decoder, and MoE 

The decoder-only structure (the GPT family [121][140]) has become mainstream because of objective consistency and engineering simplicity; encoder-decoder (T5 [49]) retains advantages in explicit conditional generation. The trade-off between the two was discussed in 6.2. This section focuses on the sparsely activated MoE , which represents the key shift of "scaling" from dense to sparse. The core idea of MoE is to use gated routing to assign each token to a small number of "experts" (subnetworks), making the number of activated parameters far smaller than the total. 

Key variables : routing strategy (Top-K, soft routing), capacity factor and load-balancing loss, expert granularity and count, and the communication overhead of expert parallelism. Milestone works established MoE practice along five dimensions: Switch Transformer [9] proved that single-expert routing (Top-1) is stable and efficient at extreme scale and provided an engineering recipe for capacity factor and load balancing; GShard [122] proposed automatic sharding and a conditional-computation framework, coupling sparse scaling with system schedul-ing; GLaM [130] matched the performance of a large dense model with roughly 1/3 of the acti-vated compute, key evidence for "compute-efficiency-oriented scaling"; ST-MoE [131] systemat-ically examined the stability of sparse-expert training (regularization, router initialization) and the transferability of "sparse-to-dense" fine-tuning; Mixtral [151] demonstrated the trainability of an open-source 8-expert sparse MoE, freeing the sparse architecture from institution-specific All Stages of Large-Model Training 33 

engineering. A survey caveat: MoE comparisons must distinguish total parameters from ac-tivated parameters , otherwise the "parameter count" discussion loses meaning. 

Limitations and openness : the training stability of sparse models (loss spikes, expert collapse) is more sensitive than that of dense models; evidence on the interaction of MoE with long context and low-precision training is still dominated by internal reports (Grade C). Whether MoE routing is stable during the RL/alignment stage currently lacks systematic study (open problem, see 6.12). 

The essential trade-off between dense and sparse : a dense model uses all parameters in every forward pass, with strong interpretability and stable training, but diminishing returns per unit of compute; a sparse model trades additional total parameters for "higher capacity at the same activated compute," at the cost of system complexity for routing and load balancing, plus the risk of expert imbalance under long-tail distributions and unstable router convergence. Available evidence supports "at comparable activated compute, sparse MoE can match or exceed a large dense model" ([130][151]), but third-party replicable evidence for this conclusion under RL post-training and fine-grained instruction following is still scarce. 

Router details : the choice between Top-K routing (usually K=1 or 2) and soft routing, the capacity factor (the maximum multiple of tokens passed through), and the load-balancing auxiliary loss determine the stability and result quality of MoE training—too small a K loses information, too large degrades to dense; too small a capacity factor causes token dropping (loss fluctuation), too large introduces wasted computation. ST-MoE’s [131] comparison of these variables shows that MoE’s gains are not free: it shifts part of the "stability budget" to routing hyperparameters. Most consistent conclusions come from medium scale; recent frontier advances (such as MiMo-V2.6 [412], a 1.02T total / 42B active omni-modal MoE) disclose industrial recipes of freezing MoE routers and adopting hybrid sliding-window attention (hybrid-SWA) to stably scale context up to 1 million tokens under massive-batch asynchronous RL (Grade C), offering a viable reference for mitigating expert routing drift. 

Capacity and "memory vs. reasoning" : whether dense or sparse, the parameters of the feed-forward layers carry most of the factual memory. This explains why "scaling parameters" yields significant gains on knowledge-type tasks, and also why MoE gains are pronounced on knowledge-intensive tasks but limited on pure reasoning/format-type tasks—the latter depend more on data and objectives than on capacity. This observation is indirectly supported by several works (Grade B), but controlled experiments that cleanly separate "memory" from "reasoning" are lacking. 

## 6.4 Scaling Laws: The Kaplan vs. Chinchilla Controversy and Reconciliation 

The scaling law characterizes the empirical regularity of "loss with respect to parameter count, data amount, and compute," and is the quantitative basis for pretraining budget decisions. Ka-plan et al. [119] gave a power law under a relatively narrow setting, arguing that "a larger model is more cost-effective than more data." Chinchilla [16], through about 400 controlled training runs covering 70M–16B parameters and 5B–500B tokens, proposed that under a given compute budget the optimum should be trained at roughly ~20 tokens per parameter , revealing that models such as GPT-3 [121] before it were clearly "undertrained," and shifting the optimal im-plementation from "parameter-first" to "data-first." This conclusion directly rewrote the (N, D) selection of open-source models such as LLaMA [140] and formed the current mainstream. 

Why the two conflict : the divergence is not a mathematical contradiction but stems from differences in extrapolation settings—the learning-rate schedule and the allocation of the token budget, and whether extrapolation is to configurations outside the training range [119][16]. Thus "which scaling law is correct" is a false question; the real question is: under a given data quality and a given budget , where is the marginal return. Gopher’s [60] large-scale empirics support the "data-first" intuition, but likewise depend on specific tuning details. 

How data quality perturbs scale : recent work brings the data dimension into the scaling All Stages of Large-Model Training 34 

law. The scaling law of data filtering shows that the optimal filtering strength depends on the compute budget —when compute is limited, noise should be filtered more aggres-sively; when compute is ample, retaining more diversity is instead better [87]—which directly undermines the simplified understanding that "the Chinchilla ratio is a universal constant." The data-mixture law [86] models the interaction of mixture and scale with predictive loss, supporting dynamic rather than static fixed mixtures. The common implication of the two: treat data as a first-class citizen first, then discuss the (N, D) optimum —this is the interface between Chapter 5 and this chapter, and the source of open problem 2. 

Strength of evidence : among Grade A/B evidence, the scaling law is one of the lines with more controlled experiments ([119][16][60]), but the largest-scale extrapolations still rely on internal reports; whenever a work across the literature cites an "optimal-ratio constant," this paper requires that data quality and compute scope be stated (Section 3.4). 

Distinguishing two regimes : the scaling law is not a single-point optimum but a family of surfaces that move with (N, D, data quality). When data-limited (the total amount of obtainable high-quality data is fixed, as in vertical domains or low-resource languages), the optimal strategy is to "overtrain" on limited data—scaling parameters beyond the Chinchilla constant. This scenario is especially common in domain continued pretraining (Chapter 9), showing that "data-first" applies only to general corpora with abundant data. Moreover, the scaling law has not yet been jointly closed-formulated with the post-training budget: given a total budget, when is it more cost-effective to spend part of it on RL/alignment (Chapter 8) rather than continuing pretraining—an extrapolatable joint model is still lacking (open problem 1). 

Numerical anchors (scope-illustrative examples, not universal conclusions) : Chin-chilla [16] was trained with about 70B parameters and about 1.4T tokens (a data-to-parameter ratio of about 20:1), while LLaMA-65B [140] used about 65B parameters and about 1.4T tokens, adopting a similar ratio. These numbers are only anchors under a specific data quality ,not portable constants—the controversy in 6.10 is, to a large extent, precisely the mistaking of anchors for an optimal law. When citing such numbers, one should simultaneously give the premises of data cleaning and mixture (Chapter 5); otherwise it may mislead compute decisions. 

The practice of budget estimation : given a compute budget C (roughly estimable as 

≈ 6N D ), one can first use a scaling law to estimate an initial (N, D ) ratio, then perform a small-scale mixture sweep on the real data (e.g., at the 10 18 –10 20 FLOPs level) for local correction [16][86]. Engineering-wise, "data preparation (Chapter 5)—pretraining—post-training (Chapters 7/8)" should be regarded as a pipeline sharing a total budget: any single point’s "optimum," if it ignores the upstream and downstream, will be suboptimal along the entire pipeline. This is also the motivation for open problem 1. 

## 6.5 Optimization and Stability 

The optimizer is the first gate of pretraining stability. Adam [105] and AdamW [110] with de-coupled weight decay are the de facto standard, and their default recipe of "large learning rate + warmup + cosine decay" has been widely copied. Recent exploration unfolds along three lines: low-memory/high-efficiency —Lion [25], discovered by symbolic-optimizer search, uses a momentum-sign update to eliminate second moments, markedly reducing GPU memory and RAM in large-model training while performing comparably to AdamW; second-order approx-imation —Sophia [31] uses a diagonal Hessian approximation to accelerate pretraining conver-gence, but evidence for its robustness at extreme scale is limited; hyperparameter scaling —muP [132], via tensor-program theory, gives scaling rules for the learning rate and others with respect to width, enabling zero-shot transfer of hyperparameters from small to large models and reducing the cost of "tuning once" at scale. 

Stability engineering : besides the optimizer, key means for pretraining stability include: learning-rate warmup and scheduling (insufficient warmup easily triggers loss spikes); gradient All Stages of Large-Model Training 35 

clipping; appropriate weight initialization (including scaling of residual branches); and online monitoring of loss spikes with checkpoint rollback . Spikes often correlate with a too-high learning rate and abnormal data batches (e.g., duplicates or encoding errors); there is no unified theoretical explanation yet, and engineering practice relies mainly on detect-and-rollback. 

Precision and numerical boundaries : FP16/BF16 have become the default training precision, with BF16 being more robust to spikes due to its wider exponent range; FP8 and lower precisions improve throughput while significantly altering the numerical-stability boundary, and their interaction when combined with MoE, long sequences, and small batch sizes is unknown. 

Precision is both an optimization problem and a systems problem , a typical "coupling point" in the six-dimensional framework. 

Evidence and limitations : optimizer comparisons have relatively strong Grade A/B ev-idence at controlled medium scale ([25][31]), but at true frontier scale the vast majority of comparisons lack public, replicable third-party experiments, and the conclusions are mostly single-institution reports (Grade C). Comparing any "X% speedup" across works must state the precision, batch, sequence, and schedule scope. 

Rules of thumb for scheduling and monitoring : the sensitivity of scheduling details to scaling extrapolation is precisely one technical source of the Kaplan [119] vs. Chinchilla [16] divergence. The rules of thumb commonly used in practice (e.g., scaling the learning rate with the square root of the batch, adjusting the schedule according to the effective batch and token progress) mostly come from internal experience and are unpublicly verified (Grade C); when citing across works, one must state the original schedule and perform a small-scale re-test before transfer. For stability monitoring, the gradient norm (rather than only the loss) can expose numerical divergence earlier; its interaction with training precision (especially pronounced under FP8) and batch size is an active area of current stability research. 

Three grades of precision (scope reminder) : FP32 is the numerical safety baseline but incurs high GPU-memory and throughput overhead; BF16, with its 8-bit exponent, approaches FP32’s stability at medium scale and has become the training mainstream; FP16 has a narrow magnitude range and requires dynamic-scaling tricks, being unstable in some implementations; FP8 (E4M3/E5M2) has been used in some ultra-large-scale training, but gradient accumulation and master weights usually fall back to higher precision. Precision choice is thus an explicit trade of "throughput vs. stability," and must be reported together with batch size, sequence, and degree of parallelism; otherwise any "speedup" conclusion cannot be third-party replicated. 

Effective batch and gradient noise : batch size is not an isolated choice—it determines, together with gradient noise and learning rate, the "information efficiency per token." Empir-ically there exists a "critical batch size" beyond which training efficiency no longer improves linearly, and this critical value grows with model scale; but this is still an empirical regularity (Grade C). Many production recipes set the batch directly according to the GPU-memory limit, leaving a hidden coupling of "learning rate scaled accordingly"; when comparing optimization configurations across works, if the effective batch is not disclosed (the effective batch may differ from the nominal batch due to gradient accumulation), the conclusions are hard to transfer. 

## 6.6 Distribution and Systems 

Pretraining scalability is determined by the combination of parallelism strategies. Four types of parallelism constitute the mainstream toolbox: data parallelism (DP) shards data and all-reduces gradients; tensor parallelism (TP) splits intra-layer weight matrices (Megatron-LM [118] gives a reference implementation); pipeline parallelism (PP) segments layers and pipelines them with micro-batches (introduced by GPipe [115], including recomputation to reduce activation memory); expert parallelism (EP) works with MoE to distribute experts across devices (GShard [122]). ZeRO [6], by progressively sharding optimizer states, gradients, and parameters, eliminates the GPU-memory redundancy of data parallelism, enabling "very large batch + large model" to be trained without TP; FSDP [142] engineers this idea into a general All Stages of Large-Model Training 36 

framework and reports ultra-large-scale experience. 

The attention implementation determines the upper bound for long sequences. FlashAt-tention [135] uses IO-aware block-wise exact attention (tiling + recomputation) to significantly reduce GPU memory and improve throughput, turning long-context training from infeasible into routine; the batch of variants that followed basically follow the same IO-optimization route. 

The coupling of systems and optimization : the available batch, sequence length, and degree of parallelism are not free variables but are determined by GPU memory, bandwidth, and communication topology; they in turn constrain the learning-rate range, loss stability, and token throughput. Therefore, the "hyperparameter tuning" of 6.5 must be performed jointly within the "systems" of 6.6 ; otherwise the optimal learning rate and the optimal degree of parallelism will contradict each other. The compute-comparison table in Appendix C provides a template for registering such scope. 

Communication, memory, and fault tolerance : the second set of system-level variables concerns communication and memory optimization—bandwidth optimization of gradient all-reduce (bucketing, latency hiding), minimization of bubbles in pipeline parallelism, activation recomputation/checkpointing (the classic trade-off of trading compute for GPU memory), and periodic state checkpointing and fault recovery (long pretraining runs cannot be interrupted, so fault tolerance directly determines whether one can return to the schedule trajectory where the optimal learning rate lies). This forms a "throughput–GPU memory–wall-clock time" three-way budget game : for the same FLOPs budget, different parallelism and recomputation configurations give different wall-clock times and solvable sequence upper bounds. Cross-work reporting must give all three scopes simultaneously (the Appendix C template is designed for this purpose). 

Intuition for common combinations (not a recipe) : several-B to tens-of-B parameters commonly use DP + ZeRO/FSDP [6][142]; in medium-scale single-node multi-GPU scenarios, TP cuts intra-layer weights so that a single card can hold a larger layer [118]; hundreds-of-B to trillion-level scenarios require 3D parallelism (TP × PP × DP) combined with EP to handle MoE experts [122][151]. An auditable report should include: the total number of GPUs and the interconnect topology, each dimension of TP/PP/DP/EP, batch and sequence length, the layer granularity of activation recomputation, and measured throughput or MFU. Without these numbers, "cumulative GPU hours" by itself has almost no comparable value. 

Data order and shuffling : "sample order," which appears to belong to the data dimension (Chapter 5), is doubly constrained by systems and optimization in pretraining: the randomness of shuffling, whether epochs are reused, and whether the order of cross-device shards is consistent all affect the loss curve and the occurrence of spikes. Cross-work reports also rarely disclose data order (random seed, shuffle, number of epochs), a hidden source of replication failure that should be included in the Appendix D reproducibility checklist. 

Cost scope : a system comparison should give at least one of three: per-step wall-clock time, per-token throughput, or MLU/MFU (compute-utilization efficiency). These metrics are highly sensitive to precision, sequence length, and parallelism configuration, so any "we are faster" conclusion should be measured under the same software, hardware, and configuration. The compute table in Appendix C registers along the three dimensions of (N, D, C ) and configuration, precisely to avoid "throughputs under different scopes" being compared side by side. 

## 6.7 Long-Context Training and Length Extrapolation 

Long context has two distinct goals: training long context (requiring long-text data and cor-responding compute) and extrapolation (supporting longer sequences at inference with a short training sequence). Positional encoding is the key to achieving both. RoPE [11], with rotary positional encoding, preserves relative positional relationships and has become the mainstream choice; ALiBi [129] achieves zero-shot length extrapolation with a linear distance bias, a repre-sentative scheme predating the interpolation era; positional interpolation (PI) [143] extends the All Stages of Large-Model Training 37 

window by scaling positions and reusing pretrained weights, with low cost but requiring long-text continued training for calibration; YaRN [37] improves the frequency scaling and attention temperature of interpolation, enlarging the effective window and reducing perplexity loss under limited long-text data. Another line is linear-time sequence models : Mamba [149], with selective state spaces, achieves linear complexity in sequence length, avoiding the quadratic overhead of attention and offering a candidate path toward training-free ultra-long sequences, but its gap from attention on long-range retrieval-type tasks remains an open problem. 

Evaluation reminder : many measures of "successful extrapolation" decay on real long texts, so one must distinguish "can read" from "can use" (e.g., multi-hop reasoning and local-ized retrieval within long texts). Whether "training short, extrapolating long is universally feasible" remains unsettled (open problem 12): existing successes mostly rely on interpola-tion/transformation tricks [143][37] and a small amount of long-text calibration, without a general scheme that requires no long-text training at all. 

Long-context training recipes : the recipe has three input lines—long-text data (which must be extracted from massive corpora or reordered and concatenated), positional-encoding or interpolation transformations ([143][37]), and length-related loss weighting. The three are often used jointly and are difficult to attribute individually, one reason why long-context conclusions are mostly Grade-C reports. For evaluation, one should register both "can read" (perplexity, precise localized retrieval) and "can use" (long-text multi-hop reasoning, cross-paragraph information composition) metrics: the former is friendly to interpolation methods, while the latter is closer to real use; it suffices to register according to the evaluation matrix in Appendix E. 

The implementation spectrum of "train short, extrapolate long" (layered by cost– capability) : pure extrapolation—no change to training, relying on the inherent properties of the positional encoding (ALiBi’s linear bias [129], RoPE’s periodicity [11]); interpolation-style— scaling positional coordinates after training (PI [143]), requiring a small amount of calibration; transformation-enhanced—adjusting frequency and attention temperature (YaRN [37]), often with a very small amount of long-text calibration; direct long-text training—lengthening the training sequence, the most costly but closest to "true long-text capability." The four range from low to high cost and from "transposition-style extrapolation" to "persistent usable long-range," and reporting the "method–cost–capability" trio together avoids the misleading conclusion of "successful extrapolation." 

Contamination in long-text evaluation : the "longness" of long-text benchmarks may itself make contamination more covert—long documents in the pretraining corpus have a higher probability of overlapping with evaluation long texts, while existing deduplication tools are mostly sentence-level and struggle to cover paragraph-level semantic overlap. Thus, both be-fore and after long-context extension, a separate contamination audit should be performed on long-text benchmarks (Section 12.3); otherwise "successful extension" may be partly memoriza-tion rather than generalization. 

The cost structure of long context : the cost of long-text training includes not only the near-quadratic attention overhead of the sequence itself but also data construction (long documents are scarce and require reordering/concatenation), the long-text samples needed for calibration, and the linear storage growth of the KV cache at inference. Sequence length has different cost-curve shapes for training and inference (training near-quadratic, inference near-linear), so "supporting 128K during training" and "serving 128K at low cost" are two different things. In reporting, the training-sequence upper bound should be stated separately from the servable "effective context"—most claimed context windows are "can process," not "reliably usable." All Stages of Large-Model Training 38 

## 6.8 Multimodal Pretraining 

Multimodal pretraining can be architecturally divided into three types (detailed in Chapter 10): encoder-projector-LLM (LLaVA [28], BLIP-2 [265], Qwen-VL [277]), cross-attention fusion (Flamingo [17]), and unified arbitrary-resolution and multi-task (Qwen2-VL [288]). The data side relies on large-scale image-text pairs (LAION-5B [64]) and interleaved image-text (OBELISC [273]). The essential differences from pure-text pretraining (also the core of Chapter 10): first, 

token heterogeneity —visual tokens and text tokens are asymmetric in information density, quantity, and meaning; second, multiple alignment objectives —contrastive, matching, and NTP objectives may conflict; third, evaluation sensitivity —multimodal benchmarks are more sensitive to prompt, sampling, and evaluation protocol, and the overall strength of evidence is weaker than for pure text. Therefore, the correct way to approach multimodal pretraining is to "first make visual representations solid (encoders such as CLIP [10], SigLIP2 [46], DINOv2 [27]), then perform language-modeling alignment," rather than treating multimodality as a casual extension of pure text. 

The trade-off of freezing strategies : another decision dimension of the fusion approach is "to freeze or not." Flamingo [17] freezes the vision encoder and LLM and trains only the cross-attention module, giving high data efficiency but limited fusion capability; LLaVA [28] fine-tunes the projector and LLM (with different freezing strategies by version), giving stronger instruction following but requiring more image-text instruction data; BLIP-2 [265] uses a Q-Former to compress visual information and maintain loose coupling. A direct comparison of the three must fix the LLM backbone and the image-text data scale; otherwise the differences mainly stem from confounding variables (mostly Grade B/C single-point evidence). This dimension connects directly to multimodal instruction tuning in Chapter 7 and the hallucination discussion in Chapter 10. 

The multimodal specificity of data mixture : the data mixture for multimodal pretrain-ing is not a simple weighted sum of "image-text pairs + text"—images and text differ greatly in token consumption, cleanliness, and strength of semantic correspondence, and the model easily "locks onto" the weak correspondence of image-text pairs (image and text failing to constrain each other). Interleaved image-text [273] and multi-resolution training (Qwen2-VL [288]) attempt to alleviate this, but the mixture laws of the four modalities image/text/video/audio currently have no public scaling law, a clear evidence gap (registered in Chapter 12 and Appendix E). 

The "imprisonment" of conclusions by evaluation : multimodal pretraining research frequently uses specific VQA/image-text benchmark scores as success criteria, and these bench-marks are highly correlated with models’ promotional scores, forming "benchmark-guided recipes." To avoid circularity, cross-modal comparisons should at least report the contamina-tion audit of image-text pairs, the prompt and decoding settings, and the code/weight release status. This information is partially missing in most works, so the evidence strength of this chapter’s multimodal conclusions has been lowered accordingly.
