## 6.10 Key Controversies 

This chapter converges on three controversies, each annotated as "consensus vs. remaining disagreement": First, comparability of scaling laws . The consensus is that loss decreases as a power law with (N, D); the disagreement is about the optimal ratio. The difference between Kaplan [119] and Chinchilla [16] partly stems from settings, and the data-filtering scaling law [87] further shows that optimality is compute-dependent. Conclusion: any "optimal-ratio constant" should be interpreted under a stated data-quality and compute scope. Second, the effect of data quality on the optimal ratio . The consensus is that quality af-fects convergence speed; the disagreement is over whether it "transfers" the Chinchilla constant— whether high-quality data allows fewer tokens to reach the same loss. Existing evidence is mainly single-point studies (problem 2, evidence graded B/C), and no definitive conclusion can yet be given. Third, the open-source reproducibility deficit . Most of the largest-scale pretrainings (frontier MoE, FP8, ultra-long context) do not disclose data and compute details, causing system conclusions to be concentrated in a few institutions and impossible to third-party verify (see the many [to be verified] entries in Appendix C). This is one of the most serious "evidence gaps" in the field and the main source of Grade-C items in the Appendix E grading. Fourth, the absence of evaluation and governance during pretraining . Pretraining takes loss as its primary objective and rarely injects external evaluation or governance checks (toxicity, copyright, multilingual fairness) in mid-training, so problems surface only before de-ployment with extremely high rework costs. The pretraining stage has the largest investment, yet its "evaluation and governance" dimension is the weakest—the most imbalanced dimension un-der the six-dimensional framework and the main source of the governance problems in Chapters 12 and 14. All Stages of Large-Model Training 40 

## 6.11 Practical Recommendations 

1. First set the budget, then the ratio : given compute, use an extrapolatable scaling law to estimate the initial (N, D ) and conduct small-scale mixture experiments to calibrate the curve [16][86]; when the budget is insufficient, let "data-first" rather than "parameter-first" prevail. 2. Treat data as a first-class citizen : first perform data cleaning, deduplication, and mixture experiments (Chapter 5), then adjust the scaling-law budget according to data quality [87][86]. 3. Stability over aggressiveness : warmup, gradient clipping, loss-spike monitoring, and checkpoint rollback are low-cost, high-return [110][132]; the learning rate should be steady rather than aggressive. 4. Jointly tune systems and optimization : search for precision (BF16/FP8), batch, and degree of parallelism jointly as the "context" of the learning rate [6][142][135]. 5. Choose long context on demand : invest in long-text training only when the task requires it; otherwise prioritize interpolation/transformation-type extrapolation schemes [143][37]. 6. For multimodality, stabilize the encoder first : first fix the visual representation with CLIP [10]/SigLIP2 [46], then train projection and alignment, and finally perform language-modeling fine-tuning [28][288]. 7. Reserve budget for evaluation and governance : schedule mid-pretraining external eval-uation sampling (multilingual, toxicity, copyright, preliminary instruction tests) to move prob-lems earlier; register the data audit and compute scope upon release (Appendices D/E). 

## 6.12 Cross-Stage Connections and Open Problems 

Connections : pretraining determines the capability upper bound of θ0, directly constraining the achievable performance of SFT (Chapter 7) and preference alignment/RL (Chapter 8); the capability gaps exposed during alignment (long-range reasoning, instruction generalization, tool use) in turn drive design modifications to pretraining objectives, data mixture, and the tokenizer (reverse empowerment, Figure 1). Open problems : how scaling laws can be jointly optimized with the post-training budget (problem 1); how data quality transfers the optimal ratio (problem 2); the stability of MoE routing during the RL/alignment stage (newly added, Appendix F); whether "train short, extrapolate long" for long context is universally feasible (problem 12). 

Actionable research-design examples (turning open problems into executable experi-ments, for reference in expanding Appendix F): for problem 1 (scaling law × post-training bud-get), one can fix the total FLOPs and sweep the allocation of "pretraining steps vs. post-training (RM/RL) steps," measuring across multiple scales whether the downstream return curves con-verge; for problem 2 (data-quality transfer), one can perform a two-family "low-quality vs. high-quality" mixture sweep on similar corpora, testing whether the Chinchilla constant shifts as a whole; for MoE alignment stability, one can compare dense/sparse behavior in the same DPO/RLVR pipeline under the same backbone, and observe whether the router distribution drifts on degrading tasks. All three can be completed at small-to-medium scale, with reporting scope per Appendix D. 

Summary of this chapter’s evidence distribution : Chapter 6 cites 41 papers in total, predominantly Grade A/B, with Grade C concentrated in four categories: "frontier-scale MoE, FP8, ultra-long context, and multimodal mixture." This distribution is itself a conclusion: the core mechanisms of pretraining (objective, scaling law, optimization, systems) have relatively strong controlled evidence, but once one enters the combined region of "largest scale × newest hardware × multimodality," replicable evidence rapidly thins. Subsequent chapters (especially Ch7–Ch9) will repeat this pattern, allowing readers to quickly judge the credibility of any "best practice" claim. All Stages of Large-Model Training 41 

# 7 Post-Training: SFT 

> This chapter, together with Ch5/Ch6/Ch8, serves as a pilot of the dual-form "general frame-work + focused survey (sub-survey)". Evidence note: SFT’s mechanistic conclusions (tem-plate / loss masking / data form) rest on relatively robust evidence, whereas "optimal data scale / mixture" derives mostly from single-point reports (B/C). Target length: 8,000–9,000 characters.

## 7.1 Definition and Boundaries 

Conclusion first: supervised fine-tuning (SFT, also known as instruction tuning) uses "instruction–response" pairs to convert a base model into an interactive instruction model θ1; its core function is to activate and format existing capabilities rather than inject large amounts of new knowledge. In scope : instruction data construction (human/synthetic), multi-turn dialogue and templates, loss masking, full-parameter fine-tuning and PEFT, and multimodal instruction tuning. Out of scope : preference alignment and RL (Chapter 8), and continual pretraining and domain adaptation (Chapter 9). The boundary criterion is "whether the supervision signal is a token-by-token target response." 

Three categories of configuration at this stage : (1) data (construction method, scale, source model, domain distribution, number of turns); (2) training objective and loss (scope of loss masking, packing, whether system prompts are included); (3) fine-tuning method (full-parameter/PEFT, precision, learning rate and epoch). Together these determine the behavior of the "instruction model," and omitting any one of them compromises comparability—this is the fundamental reason why SFT recipes are difficult to compare across studies. 

Position of SFT in the pipeline : pretraining sets the upper bound on capability (Chap-ter 6), and SFT activates the ability to "predict the next token" into dialogue-capable, instruction-following behavior; subsequently, preference alignment (Chapter 8) optimizes "which response is better" on top of this. SFT is therefore the stage that shares a "starting point" with the alignment stage: capabilities that SFT fails to teach are difficult for the RL stage to learn from scratch via reward signals (although RLVR can partially compensate on verifiable tasks). 

Refinement of the boundary criteria for the three stages : pretraining is identified by "unlabeled self-supervision," SFT by "token-by-token supervision with a ground-truth answer," and alignment by "preference/reward signals" (Chapter 8). Accordingly, methods should be classified by supervision signal : data containing "reasoning traces" that are supervised token-by-token against ground-truth answers belongs to SFT; if a reward is given based on "whether the result is correct," it belongs to RLVR (Chapter 8). The same task can be trained with different signals, and classification depends on the signal rather than the data’s appearance. 

Why SFT cannot be skipped by pretraining or RL : the pretraining objective (NTP) does not distinguish "what should be learned" from "what should be answered," and RL requires scorable outputs in order to start. SFT provides three things that are difficult for the RL stage to obtain cheaply—a unified, parseable output format; executable exemplars of tasks; and an implicit demonstration of "what counts as a good response." Without it, the RL reward signal would be drowned by a large volume of unformed outputs. The engineering goal of SFT should therefore be "to prepare a viable starting point for RL," not merely to boost instruction benchmarks. 

Re-emphasis of the boundary with continual pretraining : if the goal is to inject new knowledge / new-domain corpora into the model (e.g., medical literature, legal statutes, a new language), the primary means should be continual pretraining (Chapter 9) or self-supervised adaptation on large-scale domain corpora, supplemented by a small amount of SFT to teach "how to answer using that knowledge." Placing the entire burden of knowledge injection on SFT is both inefficient and prone to inducing hallucinated citations. This is the main line of division of labor between this chapter and Chapter 9. All Stages of Large-Model Training 42 

Examining SFT through the six-dimensional framework : data (instruction construc-tion/mixture/decontamination), objective (cross-entropy over token-by-token ground-truth an-swers), optimization (small learning rate, few epochs, PEFT/full-parameter), system (packing, long-sequence GPU memory, parallelism), evaluation (instruction following + capability reten-tion + safety), and risk (forgetting, over-refusal, synthetic collapse). Among the six dimensions, SFT’s "system" constraints are far weaker than those of pretraining, while the "data" dimension dominates the outcome—this is precisely the key difference from Ch5/Ch6. 

## 7.2 Instruction Data Construction 

Human-written instructions : FLAN [163] templatizes existing NLP tasks into instructions; Super-NaturalInstructions [167] constructs declarative instructions for 1600+ tasks via crowd-sourcing; and the Flan Collection [172] systematizes the mixture of data and methods. The advantage of human-written data is high correctness and a trustworthy instruction distribution; the cost is that coverage is limited by existing tasks and by human labor. 

Synthetic instructions : Self-Instruct [24] uses model bootstrapping to generate instruc-tions and responses; Evol-Instruct (WizardLM [29]) uses "evolution" prompts to progressively increase instruction complexity (deepening, broadening, adding constraints). The two established the mainstream paradigm of "using strong models to create data, then fine-tuning." Synthetic data can rapidly expand scale and difficulty, but it also injects the generating model’s biases, formatting habits, and errors, and iterative use carries the risk of diversity collapse (Problem 11, sharing the same origin as the synthetic-data debate in Chapter 5). 

A third source: construction from real interactions and documents . Organizing "naturally occurring instruction–response" pairs—such as real user queries, document question answering, and code commits—into supervision data yields a distribution closest to the deploy-ment environment, but requires extensive cleaning and de-identification and struggles to cover long-tail tasks. In practice, the mixture is often "human + synthetic + real," and the mixing ratio significantly affects outcomes, yet public systematic ablations are scarce (C). 

Data for system prompts and tool schemas : modern SFT data contains not only user/assistant turns but also system instructions and tool-calling schemas. These structures must remain consistent between training data and inference time, otherwise tool calling and role adherence fail. SFT data should therefore be constructed and validated as "conversations with contracts," rather than as plain text pairs. 

Forms of dialogue structure : single-turn, multi-turn, and tool-augmented multi-turn dialogues differ greatly in data organization. Multi-turn data must ensure that roles alternate correctly across turns and that tool calls and returns appear in pairs; if tool returns are treated as ordinary assistant text, the model learns to "fabricate tool results itself." These structural details are frequent points of lost quality in SFT, yet they are rarely reported in papers. 

Sources of prompt diversity : instruction diversity can arise from task type, domain, language, length, number of constraints, and "openness" (deterministic answer vs open-ended). Expanding only by task name would miss the diversity of "constraint combinations." A prac-tical approach is to treat diversity as a measurable coverage metric (task/domain/constraint dimensions) rather than relying solely on subjective judgment. 

The "authenticity spectrum" of real versus synthetic data : data can be arranged by "source authenticity"—real user logs (most authentic, noisiest, privacy-heavy) > expert hu-man authoring (authentic but expensive) > strong model rewriting real queries (fairly authentic) > strong model generating queries (most synthetic, largest bias). Different tasks vary in their sensitivity to authenticity: safety and factual tasks depend heavily on authenticity and correct-ness, whereas format and reasoning-style tasks tolerate synthesis well. Treating "authenticity" as an explicit dimension when allocating budget is more instructive than a blanket "human vs synthetic" distinction (B/C). 

De-identification, privacy, and copyright : converting real interaction logs into SFT All Stages of Large-Model Training 43 

data requires first removing personally identifiable information (PII) and proprietary content, and clarifying copyright and usage licensing. Although synthetic data reduces privacy exposure, it may inherit the source model’s copyright and bias problems. The absence of data governance is the hidden reason why many "good recipes" cannot be made public, and it often makes full reproducibility of SFT unrealistic (Chapters 5 and 13). 

Automatic filtering of synthetic data : to control the quality of synthetic instructions, filtering is often applied after generation using rules (length, format, executability) or model-based judging (LLM-as-judge). Rules are fast but coarse; model-based judging can capture semantic issues but introduces the judging model’s own biases and may create circular bias when "using A’s preferences to filter B’s data." There is a trade-off between filtering intensity and preserving diversity—excessive filtering homogenizes the data (sharing the same origin as Problem 11, B/C). 

## 7.3 Data Quality, Scale, and Mixture 

The quality-versus-scale debate : LIMA [175] reports that good alignment can be achieved with only about 1,000 high-quality samples, suggesting that data quality and diversity may be more critical than scale; however, this conclusion is based on a single setting, and evidence for robustness across models and tasks is limited (B/C). By contrast, numerous reports show that for complex tasks (reasoning, tools), scale and correctness still significantly affect the upper bound. A more robust statement is: for "format and behavior" capabilities, a small amount of high-quality data suffices to activate them; for "knowledge and complex skills," data of corresponding scale and correctness is still required .

Diversity over sheer volume per task : coverage of task types, difficulty, domain, and language distribution generally improves generalization more than piling up volume on the same task. Empirical evidence for curriculum-style arrangement (simple to complex) and difficulty mixtures comes mostly from single-point reports (B/C), and no stable pattern across base models has emerged. 

Deduplication and contamination : instruction data likewise requires deduplication and contamination cleanup—overlap with evaluation sets’ instructions/answers inflates scores (Chap-ter 12). Approximate deduplication methods [13] and contamination detection [52] also apply at the instruction level, but determining "instruction semantic similarity" relies more on embed-dings and human review than text deduplication does. This area shares techniques with the data selection methods of Chapter 5 [392], but the target distributions differ (instruction distribution rather than pretraining distribution). 

The dynamism of mixtures : similar to pretraining, the optimal data mixture for SFT may vary with base-model capability and the subsequent alignment method—strong base models need more "formatting" data, whereas weak base models need more knowledge-type instructions. Treating the mixture as a one-time fixed choice is a common but unfounded simplification. 

Difficulty annotation and refusal samples : whether the data contains "I don’t know / refusal" samples determines the model’s uncertainty calibration and safety boundary. SFT with no refusal samples at all trains a model that "never refuses" (echoing the alignment tax in Chapter 8). Ideal data should explicitly include reasonable abstention/clarification behavior, but systematic research on how to proportion refusal samples is lacking (B/C). 

Contamination cleanup at the instruction level : overlap with evaluation sets may arise from identical items or from "synonymous rewordings." Three detection methods are com-monly used in practice: n-gram overlap (fast, prone to missing synonyms), embedding nearest neighbors (catches rewordings, requires a threshold), and manual spot-checking (accurate, expen-sive) [52][13]. Aggressive cleaning can mistakenly delete legitimate samples that are "reasonably similar" to the evaluation set (e.g., both asking "how to do X"), so a trade-off is needed be-tween "preventing inflation" and "preserving coverage"; when reporting SFT results, the specific contamination-detection method and threshold should be stated. All Stages of Large-Model Training 44 

A sense of scale : in public reports, SFT data volumes span several orders of magnitude— from the thousand-sample level (LIMA [175]) to million-scale mixed sets [172]. There is no universal answer to "how much is enough," because the upper bound is determined by effective information density rather than the number of items: the transferable signal contained in a single high-quality conversation may far exceed that of dozens of templated Q&A pairs. When comparing different works, one should therefore first normalize by "amount of information" rather than "number of items" (B/C). 

The distribution problem of multilingual SFT : instruction data is heavily skewed toward English. Fine-tuning directly on English-dominated data causes asymmetric improve-ment/degradation in non-English instruction following and generation quality. Multilingual SFT requires explicitly mixing languages and paying attention to the differences in token efficiency of templates and tokenizers across languages (echoing tokenization in Chapter 5 and multilingual pretraining in Chapter 6). Empirical research on language ratios and transfer remains scarce (B/C). 

Timeliness and knowledge cutoff : SFT data is typically collected at a certain point in time, introducing a knowledge cutoff; if the data contains outdated facts without annotation, the model will confidently repeat old information. If the alignment stage lacks supervision on "knowledge timeliness," the problem persists into deployment. This relates to the timeliness evaluation in Chapter 12 and the risk governance in Chapter 13, but it is under-discussed in the SFT literature (B/C). 

## 7.4 Training Details: Loss Masking, Packing, and Hyperparameters 

Loss masking is the core engineering concern of multi-turn dialogue SFT: loss is computed only on the tokens of the assistant’s response, while the system/user segments and template control tokens are excluded. If masking is done incorrectly, the model learns to "parrot the user’s question" or "predict template tokens," manifesting as repetition and format leakage. This problem is often overlooked, yet it is a frequent cause of failure when transferring across datasets (echoing the chat template contract in Chapter 5). 

Packing : concatenating multiple short samples into one fixed-length sequence can improve throughput, but cross-sample attention boundaries must be handled correctly (or document-level masks used); otherwise samples "contaminate each other" and form spurious context dependen-cies. Whether to pack and how to set boundaries significantly affect training stability and final quality, and should be noted when reporting. 

Hyperparameters and number of epochs : SFT typically uses a smaller learning rate and fewer epochs than pretraining (commonly 1–3 epochs); too many epochs lead to overfitting on the instruction data (loss of diversity, parroting training samples). The effects of learning rate, batch size, and schedule interact with model scale and data scale, and public ablations are mostly single-point (B/C). 

Sequence length and truncation : long instructions (containing long documents or long tool outputs) are easily truncated in SFT, causing the model to learn the erroneous behavior of "ending abruptly at the truncation point." Remedies include chunked supervision of long samples or aligning boundaries during packing. The impact of truncation strategy on long-context tasks is often ignored, and it is a source of distortion in SFT evaluation. 

Implementation pitfalls of loss masking : masking must not only correctly exclude user/system content but also handle the implicit role tokens and newlines in the template; different frameworks have different default behaviors (some count EOS, some do not). These implementation differences cause "identically named methods" to produce different results, a common source of replication failure—the specific template and masking configuration should be provided when reporting. 

Details of EOS and padding : whether EOS is appended to the end of each response and whether padding participates in the loss directly affect when the model "stops." If EOS is not All Stages of Large-Model Training 45 

added during training, the model may generate indefinitely at inference; if padding is counted in the loss, the model learns to "predict padding tokens." These seemingly trivial configurations are a common cause of SFT failure and should be made explicit in the method appendix. 

Long-context SFT and GPU memory : extending context to long documents requires including long samples in training, and long sequences significantly increase activation memory. Common measures include gradient checkpointing, sequence parallelism, and packing; but if long samples are scarce in the data, the model still struggles to remain stable over long contexts. Long-context capability is thus jointly determined by "pretraining positional-encoding/attention design" (Section 6.6) and "the supply of long samples in SFT," and neither can be omitted. 

The impact of parallelism and throughput on SFT : SFT commonly uses a combination of data parallelism plus tensor/pipeline parallelism, with packing and sequence length jointly determining GPU memory and throughput. As in pretraining, effective batch size is coupled with learning rate; on packed data, length differences among samples within the same micro-batch introduce additional gradient-noise variation that affects stability. 

Learning rate and scaling in SFT : the SFT learning rate is typically lower than the end-of-pretraining learning rate and is reduced as model scale increases; warmup still helps early stability. The larger the data volume, the higher the tolerable learning rate and epoch count. These relationships resemble those of pretraining but at smaller magnitudes, and public joint ablations are rare; in practice, one should scan learning rate × epoch combinations on a small-scale proxy and then transfer to the large model (B/C). 

Data order and curriculum : the order in which samples are presented affects the optimiza-tion trajectory—easy-to-hard (curriculum learning) or general-to-specialized has been beneficial in some reports, but the evidence is mostly single-point (B/C). As in pretraining, "shuffling" is the safest default; if a curriculum is used, one should report whether its gains come from the order itself or from the information content of harder data. The same applies to packing order: avoid over-clustering samples of the same distribution. 

## 7.5 Full-Parameter Fine-Tuning vs Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning 

Full-parameter fine-tuning updates all parameters; it is flexible but incurs high GPU-memory and storage costs and easily overfits small datasets. The PEFT family approaches full-parameter performance with very few trainable parameters: Adapter [159] inserts small modules between layers; Prefix-Tuning [160] and P-Tuning [161]/v2 [164] optimize continuous prompts; LoRA [12] injects low-rank updates into the weights and has become the de facto standard; and QLoRA [30] performs LoRA on a 4-bit quantized base, making single-GPU fine-tuning of large models possible. 

A precise statement of the trade-off : PEFT significantly lowers the resource barrier and facilitates multi-task switching (adapters for different tasks can be hot-swapped), but on tasks requiring substantial behavior change (introducing a new style, new-domain knowledge, or a new language), it often falls short of full-parameter fine-tuning or large-scale continual pretraining [12]. Whether "PEFT can replace full-parameter tuning" therefore depends on the magnitude of behavior change : PEFT is often sufficient for fine-tuning "activation formats," whereas injecting "new capabilities" requires a route with larger modifications. This judgment is supported by A/B-level evidence, but there is no quantitative boundary for "how large a change requires full-parameter tuning." 

Rank and target modules of LoRA : the performance of LoRA [12] is sensitive to the low rank r, the scaling factor α, and "which modules it is applied to" (attention only, or including MLP). The general rule is that the larger the magnitude of task change, the higher the effective rank required; applying it to both attention and MLP is usually better than attention alone but costs more. Public ablations are mostly single-point (B), so the optimal LoRA configuration should be searched per task rather than copied. 

Serving and switching costs of PEFT : hot-swappable adapters bring the convenience All Stages of Large-Model Training 46 

of "one base model serving multiple tasks," but they also introduce the engineering complexity and latency of switching at inference and mixing different adapters within a batch. The benefits of PEFT must therefore be evaluated together with the deployment form—the optimal choice differs between offline batch and online multi-tenant scenarios. 

Merging and inference equivalence of PEFT : LoRA updates can be merged into the base weights before inference, thereby adding no inference latency—this makes PEFT not only a training technique but also a "releasable product form" (one base model + multiple mergeable adapters). However, the precision after merging, as well as the numerical error after merging on a quantized base (such as QLoRA [30]), must be verified separately; the equivalence of activating multiple adapters simultaneously has no unified conclusion (B/C). 

Quantized fine-tuning and precision : QLoRA [30] performs LoRA on a 4-bit base, signif-icantly lowering the GPU-memory barrier, but quantization error and low-rank approximation compound and may lose precision on fine-grained tasks. Whether "QLoRA can replace full-parameter tuning" depends on the task’s sensitivity to numerical fidelity; it is usually sufficient for format and style tasks, while tasks requiring fine-grained knowledge or long-chain reasoning need careful verification (B/C). 

The relationship of Adapter/Prefix to LoRA : Adapter [159] and Prefix/P-Tuning [160][161][164] achieve parameter efficiency from the two respective angles of "adding modules" and "adding learnable prompts"; LoRA [12], through low-rank weight updates, achieves both simplicity and effectiveness and thus has become the modern mainstream. The three are the-oretically different parameterizations and are often interchangeable in practice, with the choice depending more on inference deployment and toolchain support than on absolute performance differences (B). 

## 7.6 Multimodal Instruction Fine-Tuning 

Multimodal IFT extends instruction fine-tuning to vision-language settings: LLaVA [28] uses visual instruction data to train a projector and an LLM, while BLIP-2 [265] and Qwen-VL [277] provide different fusion strategies (Section 6.8, Chapter 10). The key differences lie in the data form (image-instruction-response triplets), the loss handling of modality tokens, and the risk of hallucination—visual hallucinations introduced during the SFT stage often persist into deployment (Chapters 10 and 12). In practice, visual IFT data is more prone than text to mixing in samples with "weak image-text relevance," requiring dedicated pairing-quality filtering. 

Freezing strategies for multimodal SFT : which parts are trained (projector only, projec-tor + LLM, or including the vision encoder) significantly affects cost and effectiveness. Freezing the vision encoder saves compute but limits adaptation of visual representations; unfreezing it may harm general visual capabilities and exacerbate forgetting. This trade-off is in the same lineage as the pretraining freezing strategies in Section 6.8. 

Evaluation and hallucination measurement for multimodal IFT : the effectiveness of visual instruction fine-tuning is commonly measured with VQA/captioning benchmarks and the "hallucination rate." The hallucination rate can be estimated as "the proportion of men-tioned objects absent from the image" or via annotation-based judgments, but protocols vary across works, making cross-comparison difficult. More critically: if the SFT stage optimizes only "whether the response looks decent" without constraining "whether it is faithful to the image," it systematically fosters fluent but distorted descriptions—visual grounding supervision must be explicitly introduced at the data and loss levels (Chapters 10 and 12). 

Broader modalities (speech/video) : the "instruction–response" paradigm of SFT is ex-tending to speech and video: the data form becomes audio/video–instruction–response, loss han-dling must distinguish modality tokens, and the alignment objectives (such as lip-sync/temporal consistency) are more complex. The evidence density in these directions is far lower than for text and image-text, and public benchmarks are not yet mature (C), so this survey includes them only at the level of the methodological framework. All Stages of Large-Model Training 47 

Mixture in multimodal SFT and language-capability degradation : feeding only image-text instruction data degrades the model’s pure-text capability. The prudent approach is to mix a substantial proportion of pure-text instruction data into multimodal IFT to maintain language and general instruction following. The effect of the mixing ratio on "visual gains vs text regression" is structurally isomorphic to the replay problem, but public ablations are insufficient (B/C). 

Choice of vision-language connector : in multimodal IFT, a connector (linear projection, Q-Former [265], or cross-attention) is needed between the vision encoder and the LLM. The connector’s capacity determines the bandwidth at which visual information enters the language model—too narrow and details are lost, too wide and it easily overfits and increases cost. LLaVA [28] achieves considerable results with a simple projection, indicating that connector design is not the sole bottleneck; however, the differences among designs on fine-grained and OCR-type tasks still need task-by-task evaluation (B/C). 

## 7.7 Catastrophic Forgetting and Knowledge Retention 

A frequently underestimated risk of SFT is catastrophic forgetting : fine-tuning on narrow instruction data may impair the base model’s general capabilities (especially when the data is homogeneous and the number of epochs is excessive). Mitigation measures include mixing in a certain proportion of general/pretraining data (replay), limiting the learning rate and epoch count, and using PEFT to constrain the magnitude of updates. Evidence systematically com-paring the relative benefits of these measures is limited (B/C), and the measurement of "how much is forgotten" depends on the choice of evaluation set (Chapter 12). The forgetting problem is more prominent in continual pretraining (Chapter 9); SFT is a milder version of the same problem. 

The relationship between SFT and the alignment tax : the refusal/safety samples introduced during the SFT stage affect the model’s safety–helpfulness balance before RL does; if SFT is over-safety-tuned, the RL stage must "reverse" and restore helpfulness at higher cost. The trade-off between safety and helpfulness should therefore be planned jointly for SFT and alignment, rather than tuned independently for each (Chapters 8 and 12). 

Measuring forgetting and the replay ratio : forgetting can be measured with a "capa-bility matrix"—comparing score changes before and after SFT on the same set of general and target tasks, where forgetting appears as a systematic decline on general tasks. For mitigation, the optimal ratio of replayed general data varies with the "target vs general" trade-off, typically between 1% and several tens of percent (single-point reports, B/C); if too large, target-capability improvement slows. The rational approach is to first plot the Pareto frontier of "target gain– general regression" at proxy scale and then choose the ratio accordingly, rather than fixing an empirical value. 

Parameter-space versus function-space explanations of forgetting : one view at-tributes forgetting to the magnitude of parameter updates (parameter space), while another attributes it to changes in function/representation (function space). The former leads to "con-straining updates" (small learning rate, PEFT, regularization), whereas the latter leads to "pro-tecting key representations/replaying data." Both explanations are effective in practice but under different conditions, and a unified theory of forgetting is still lacking (B/C). 

The tension between forgetting and capability "emergence" : while aligning new behaviors, SFT may make some base capabilities "appear to degrade"—sometimes genuine for-getting, sometimes merely a change in trigger (the capability remains but requires a different prompt). Distinguishing the two requires evaluation with multiple triggering methods (Chapter 12). Interpreting every "score drop" uniformly as a loss of capability overestimates the cost of SFT and may also mask genuine degradation. 

The boundary between SFT and reasoning distillation : training token-by-token with a strong model’s chain-of-thought (CoT) as the supervision target belongs to SFT (using long CoT All Stages of Large-Model Training 48 

data to teach the model "how to reason"), whereas using its outputs as preference/reward signals belongs to alignment. CoT-SFT can teach format and reasoning style, but whether it "genuinely improves reasoning capability" is structurally isomorphic to the RLVR controversy (the precursor of Problem 5 in Chapter 8): it may merely learn to reproduce high-quality trajectories rather than acquire a new reasoning mechanism. The gains of CoT distillation should therefore be distinguished between "format acquisition" and "capability extension."
