Reading note for the table : this table is indexed by "mechanism" rather than "score"— the high configurability of SFT-type work makes scores nearly incomparable across papers (a conclusion sharing the same origin as Ch5). 

Ecosystem and uses of public instruction datasets : public resources such as the FLAN series [163][172], Super-NaturalInstructions [167], and Self-Instruct [24] can be used directly for training and are also often used as preset data for "format alignment." Their value lies in re-producibility and a low barrier to entry; their limitations lie in domain bias (mostly English, task-ified natural language), possible evaluation contamination, and inconsistent licensing. Be-fore use, one should verify the license and contamination status and supplement incrementally according to the target domain rather than copying wholesale. 

## 7.9 Key Controversies 

First, quality vs scale . The LIMA [175] camp argues that a small amount of high-quality data suffices for alignment; large-scale practice on the other side shows that complex capabilities (reasoning, tools, multilingual) still require corresponding scale. The consensus is that "for-mat/behavior can be few but well-chosen, while knowledge/skills require scale for support"; the All Stages of Large-Model Training 49 

disagreement concerns the boundary of "few" and "well-chosen" and how that boundary shifts with base-model capability (no systematic pattern yet). Second, whether SFT can inject new knowledge . The mainstream view (A/B) holds that SFT mainly activates and formats , that knowledge injection is inefficient and easily leads to hallucinated "endorsement"; however, if the instruction data itself is high-density, large-scale, and high-quality, SFT can indeed introduce a certain amount of domain knowledge (mostly B/C single-point evidence). The prudent conclusion is: assign the main responsibility for knowledge injection to pretraining/continual pretraining, and let SFT be responsible for "teaching how to use it." Third, the boundary of synthetic bootstrapping . Sharing the same origin as Chapter 5: using strong models to create instructions and responses can expand rapidly, but improper iteration and poor-quality filtering lead to diversity collapse and error accumulation (Problem 11, level C). The claim that "purely synthetic data can rival human data" should therefore be marked as pending verification. 

The common root of the three controversies : quality–scale, knowledge injection, and synthetic bootstrapping all point to "how the per-unit information content and diversity of supervision data change with scale." If "data utility" can be treated as a measurable quantity (rather than looking only at the number of items), all three become, within a unified framework, questions about the shape of the "utility–scale curve." 

How the three controversies affect engineering decisions : if one believes in "few but well-chosen," the budget should go to data curation (human labor/denoising/decontamination) rather than scaling up; if one emphasizes that "knowledge requires scale," one should scale up on knowledge and complex skills while bearing the noise; if one relies on synthetic bootstrapping, one must establish monitoring of quality and diversity to prevent collapse. These choices determine "where the money is spent," so interpreting the controversies is not academic fastidiousness but a basis for resource allocation. 

Does SFT also have a scaling law? Pretraining has a relatively stable loss–compute scaling law (Section 6.3), but SFT’s object is "alignment quality" rather than a measurable loss, and it depends heavily on data quality and evaluation definitions; thus there is as yet no widely accepted scaling law for SFT (an open problem). The three-dimensional relationship of "data scale–quality–capability" is mostly presented through single-point reports, which is the deep reason why several controversies in this chapter are difficult to converge. 

Turning controversies into testable hypotheses : all three controversies in this chapter can be written as falsifiable propositions—H1: on a fixed base model, "a small amount of high-quality" data is not inferior to "a large amount of mixed" data on format-type tasks; H2: the injection efficiency of SFT for "facts not present in the training corpus" is significantly lower than that of continual pretraining; H3: the performance of purely synthetic data first rises and then falls with the number of bootstrapping rounds (a collapse point exists). All three can be tested at moderate compute and are the gaps most worthy of prioritizing to fill with evidence. 

## 7.10 Practical Recommendations and Cross-Stage Connections 

Recommendations: prioritize ensuring the diversity, correctness, and template consistency 

of instructions; use PEFT (LoRA [12]/QLoRA [30]) with small data and perform strict dedupli-cation and decontamination; always perform loss masking in multi-turn training and explicitly handle boundaries during packing; control the epoch count and mix in general data to suppress forgetting; do not expect SFT to inject large amounts of new knowledge—that belongs to pre-training or continual pretraining. Cross-stage connections : SFT determines the "quality of the starting point" for the RL/preference-alignment stage—if SFT fails to teach basic formats and tool calling, the RL reward signal will be diluted by a large number of format errors, so SFT and preference alignment should jointly design data and evaluation [15][175]; conversely, instruction blind spots exposed during the alignment stage feed back into SFT data construction All Stages of Large-Model Training 50 

(reverse empowerment, Figure 1). Open problems : the quality-diversity-collapse critical point of synthetic instruction bootstrapping (Problem 11); whether the optimal scale of SFT varies with base-model capability; and the boundary of "behavior-change magnitude" between PEFT and full-parameter tuning. See Appendix F and [182] for details. 

Actionable research design examples : for the quality–scale controversy, one can fix the base model and evaluation, vary SFT data over a two-dimensional grid of "scale × quality (denoised/human vs synthetic)," and measure the divergent responses of format-type and knowledge-type tasks; for forgetting, one can scan the general-data mixing ratio during fine-tuning and measure the Pareto frontier of "target-capability gain vs general-capability regres-sion." Both can be completed within moderate compute. 

How to evaluate an SFT run : it should cover at least: (1) instruction following (format, constraints, multi-turn consistency); (2) capability retention (regression on general tasks relative to the base model); (3) improvement in the target domain; (4) safety and reasonableness of refusals. Reporting only "instruction benchmark scores" masks capability regression and over-refusal, leading to underestimation of the true cost of SFT (Chapter 12). 

The data interface between SFT and RL : before entering RL, the format of the SFT model’s outputs (separation of chain-of-thought from answer, tool-calling syntax, system-prompt adherence) must be consistent with the RL reward parser; otherwise the reward function gives erroneous signals due to parsing failures. The "format contract" is therefore a key artifact of the SFT →RL handoff: it should be versioned and its parse success rate explicitly verified in SFT evaluation (Chapter 8). 

The cost reality of SFT : compared with pretraining, SFT’s compute is usually smaller by several orders of magnitude, and the bottleneck is often data (construction, cleaning, annotation, and copyright) rather than GPUs. This explains why SFT progress comes mostly from data engineering rather than algorithms—it also means that the reproducibility of SFT depends more on data transparency (Chapter 5) than on training code. The checklist in Appendix D should list data sources and licensing as required items for SFT reproducibility. 

Safety data and red-teaming : safety behaviors (refusal, debiasing, refusing over-privileged requests) can already be injected during the SFT stage, with data sources including human red-teaming, policy rewriting, and synthetic adversarial examples. Their effectiveness depends heavily on the covered attack surface, and omitted categories will be exploited in deployment; moreover, over-injection triggers over-refusal (the alignment tax). Safety SFT should therefore be accompanied by quantifiable evaluation sets and a red-teaming loop, rather than one-off data injection (Chapters 8 and 12). 

The SFT checklist delivered downstream : an SFT artifact usable as an RL start-ing point should come with: template and special-token definitions, masking configuration, EOS/padding conventions, training-data sources and licensing, and baseline scores for each ca-pability dimension. This "contract package" enables the RL stage to correctly parse outputs and judge the quality of the starting point, and it also constitutes a prerequisite for reproducibility (Appendices D and G). 

Summary of the evidence distribution in this chapter : SFT’s mechanistic conclu-sions (templates, loss masking, PEFT feasibility) have relatively strong A/B evidence; "optimal data scale/mixture/curriculum" is almost entirely B/C single-point reports, consistent with the pattern in Ch5. This chapter therefore provides an engineering contract and interpretive framework (how to split data, how to mask loss, the trade-off between PEFT and full-parameter tuning), rather than portable recipe numbers. 

# 8 Preference Alignment / RL   

> This chapter, like Ch5/Ch6, is a pilot of the dual form "general framework + focused survey".
> Special note on evidence : most methods in this chapter (RLHF / RLVR / RLAIF) draw All Stages of Large-Model Training 51 their key evidence from technical reports and model cards, and the amount of Grade-A third-party reproducible evidence is markedly lower than in Ch5/Ch6. Consequently, this chapter uses a two-tier notation of "consensus vs. single report", and any conclusion that has not been independently reproduced is marked C. Target length: 8,000–9,000 Chinese characters.

## 8.1 Definition and Boundaries 

Conclusion first: preference alignment/RL uses human or AI preference signals (and verifiable rewards) to further optimize behavior on top of an SFT model, producing an aligned model θ2; it chiefly changes "how to answer", and on verifiable tasks it may also extend the boundary of rea-soning capability. Included : reward modeling, RLHF/RLAIF, RL-free preference optimization, reasoning RL (RLVR), tool and multimodal RL, safety alignment and red-teaming. Excluded :SFT (Chapter 7), pre-deployment efficiency optimization (Chapter 9). The boundary criterion is "whether the supervision signal is a preference/reward rather than a standard answer". 

The essential problem : pretraining and SFT yield a "conditional distribution", and the alignment stage imposes a target preference on that distribution—maximizing the probability of behavior that humans/AI deem good while suppressing bad behavior. Alignment is therefore not a problem of "training a better model" but of "defining and aligning a target function", and its core risk accordingly becomes misdefined objectives (the reward is a wrong proxy) rather than optimization capability alone. 

Three classes of configuration for this stage (the minimal comparability standard) :

> 1

○ source of preference/reward data (volume of human annotation, AI feedback, verifiable rules, mixing ratios); 2○ reward construction (implicit BT model, explicit generative RM, rule-based, process vs. outcome); 3○ optimization algorithm (PPO / GRPO / DPO family) together with its KL constraint and hyperparameters. If any one class is missing, cross-work comparisons of "alignment effectiveness" break down—this is the root reason why evidence in this chapter is generally marked C, because these three configurations are almost never disclosed in full. 

Three levels of alignment : the behavioral level (format, refusal, tone), the capability level (reasoning, tools, multimodality), and the value level (honesty, harmlessness, fairness). Preference data and RL have different reach across the three levels—the behavioral level is the easiest to characterize with preferences/rules, the capability level is effective only on verifiable tasks, and the value level is the hardest because the definition of "good" is itself contested. Conflating the three levels is one root of the "does alignment work vs. capability extension" debate (8.8). 

Coupling between alignment and the other stages : the target definition of alignment depends on what evaluation (Chapter 12) deems "good"; its data comes from the SFT starting point and the data pipeline (Chapters 5 and 7); its feasibility is bounded above by the base model’s capabilities (Chapter 6); and its results in turn affect continual optimization and de-ployment (Chapters 9 and 13). Alignment is thus not an endpoint, but a link in which "target definition—capability—evaluation" are continuously negotiated. 

The boundary between alignment and "fine-tuning" : in everyday usage, "fine-tuning" is often generalized to refer to both SFT and alignment, but in this survey’s taxonomy the two differ in supervision signal (standard answer vs. preference/reward), in objective (imitation vs. preference optimization), and in failure mode (catastrophic forgetting/format overfitting vs. reward hacking/alignment tax). Conflating them leads to misdiagnosis—for example, attribut-ing "sycophantic answers" produced by alignment to data quality, or attributing the repetition phenomenon of SFT to RL. 

## 8.2 Reward Modeling 

A reward model (RM) turns preferences into a scalar signal and is the "target" of RL post-training. Its forms fall into four groups: explicit vs. implicit (whether an independent RM All Stages of Large-Model Training 52 

is trained), pointwise vs. preference-based (absolute scoring vs. pairwise comparison), 

outcome reward (ORM) vs. process reward (PRM) (looking only at the final answer vs. scoring reasoning steps), and, more recently, generative rewards (using an LLM to directly generate judgment text/scores). The classic approach to preference-based RMs derives from the Bradley–Terry model: given a preference pair (yw, y l), train rϕ so that P (yw ≻ yl) = σ(rϕ(yw) − rϕ(yl)) . Its virtues are a simple objective and scalability; its drawbacks are that it can only measure "relative preference" and is sensitive to annotation noise and distribution shift—the RM’s out-of-distribution gen-eralization directly determines the upper bound of RL (once the RM is "gamed" by the policy, training deviates from the true objective, see 8.8). The motivation for process rewards is "lucky correctness": a correct final answer does not imply correct reasoning, and an ORM will reward a wrong process. Let’s Verify Step by Step [33] and Math-Shepherd [219], which constructs step-level signals via automatic verification, are representatives; both show that a PRM can improve the reliability of reasoning, at the cost of high annotation/construction cost and the possibility that the judgment of "correct step" is itself unreliable. The quality and transferability of the reward model set the upper bound for the entire RL post-training process; for a survey see [408]. 

Engineering of RM training : preference-based RMs are usually trained on hundreds of thousands to millions of pairwise annotations, with the output layer taking the scalar at the last position of the sequence; in practice reward normalization (shifting by a baseline) and reward ensembling (averaging or taking the minimum over multiple RMs/multiple random seeds) are commonly used to suppress over-optimization. The BT assumption requires comparisons to be transitive and noise-free, whereas real annotations are both noisy and not fully transitive (A>B, B>C but C>A), which opens a gap between the RM’s ranking ability and its suitability "as an optimization objective"— high accuracy on a validation set does not guarantee that an RM is safe as an RL objective .

Generative rewards and LLM-as-judge : using a strong LLM to directly give judgments or scores can significantly lower annotation cost, but introduces three known biases: position bias (preference for whichever appears first/last), length/style bias, and self-preference (models of the same family rate each other higher). Existing mitigations (randomizing option order, multi-judge ensembling, reference examples) can reduce but not eliminate these. Because the judging model itself keeps evolving, conclusions that use LLM-as-judge should be regarded as dependent on the judge version and time point (Grade C), and the judge model and prompt must be reported. 

Pointwise vs. preference-based vs. ranking-based : a pointwise RM assigns an absolute score to a single response; it is cheap but lacks relative calibration and is prone to annotation-scale drift. Preference-based (pairwise) RMs are more stable and are the mainstream of RLHF; ranking-based RMs (ranking multiple responses to one prompt) carry the most information but impose the heaviest annotation burden. Each has its own "data efficiency—stability—cost" trade-off, and the choice should be based on annotation budget and task rather than on pursuing a single fixed formulation. 

Scale and generalization of RMs : the capacity of an RM must match the complexity of the task it assesses—too small and it cannot express fine-grained preferences, too large and it easily overfits annotation noise. The RM’s ability to generalize to out-of-distribution prompts 

is the key to whether RL can generalize safely, yet public research mostly reports accuracy on in-distribution evaluation, and genuine OOD stress tests are scarce (Grade C). 

Reward shaping and online updating of the RM : decomposing the final objective into additive intermediate rewards (format score, retrieval-hit score, unit-test-pass score) can significantly alleviate sparsity, but improper shaping introduces "sacrificing the final objective to collect intermediate scores" behavior (shaping bias). A more robust practice is to let the RM update in step with the policy (online/iterative RM), so that the reward issue tracks the policy All Stages of Large-Model Training 53 

distribution, at the cost of engineering complexity and instability. 

Uncertainty and calibration of rewards : the score given by an RM should be understood as an estimate with uncertainty—on out-of-distribution prompts, the score has high variance and low confidence. Incorporating the RM’s uncertainty into optimization (conservative rewards, variance penalties, abstention) can suppress reward hacking in low-confidence regions, but how to calibrate uncertainty, and whether it is truly a reliable signal, remain open directions (C). 

Mixing of RM evaluation dimensions : the same RM often assesses factuality, style, safety, and instruction-following simultaneously, and the relative weights of these dimensions determine the slant of alignment—if the style weight is too high, the model "talks beautifully but more emptily"; if the safety weight is too high, refusals increase. Most papers report only a single "win rate", masking the trade-offs among dimensions, so the "good" of alignment should be reported dimension by dimension (Chapter 12). 

Evidence strength : the "effectiveness" of PRMs has relatively strong evidence on several benchmarks (A/B); but "PRMs can transfer to open domains and scale up" is mostly single-report (C). The reliability assessment of generative rewards and LLM-as-judge has no stable conclusion of its own and is severely affected by the choice of evaluation set—any RM conclu-sion should be reported together with whether its training distribution and evaluation distribution are from the same source .

## 8.3 Human and AI Feedback 

The classic pipeline of RLHF is "collect preferences → train an RM → optimize the policy with PPO [196]", established and popularized by InstructGPT [15]; the key engineering point is the KL penalty constraining the policy not to drift too far from the base model. Its value is relatively stable in Grade A/B evidence (InstructGPT has a public preference evaluation), but it is highly sensitive to annotation cost, annotator distribution, and consistency, and it is difficult to replicate its "secret recipe" (annotation protocol, prompt distribution), so "effect transferability" remains C. 

RLAIF uses AI feedback to replace part of the human annotation. RLAIF vs RLHF [38] reports that the two achieve comparable results; Constitutional AI [23] uses a set of principles to guide AI self-critique and revision, lowering human cost but potentially introducing the model’s own biases and turning "who defines the principles" into a governance problem. Self-rewarding 

(Self-Rewarding [223]) further lets the model iteratively generate preferences, but one must be wary of bootstrapping collapse : the model may converge to its own preferences, leading to reduced diversity and drift away from real humans (a manifestation of Problem 11 on the preference side). 

Trade-offs among feedback sources (consensus) : human annotation is high-quality but expensive, AI feedback is cheap but may amplify bias, and verifiable rules are precise but cover only decidable tasks. The three are not substitutes but a combination covering different task regions; when comparing RLAIF/RLHF across works, if the evaluation set and prompt distribution are not fixed, conclusions of "comparable" or "better" cannot be trusted. 

Sources of bias in preference data : the annotator distribution (culture, language, ex-pertise) determines the definition of "good", and misalignment occurs if it disagrees with the target users; the prompt distribution determines the situations being optimized, and biasing to-ward one task type crowds out other capabilities; and the annotation interface itself (pairwise comparison vs. scoring, whether the source model is shown) changes behavior. These biases are rarely disclosed in full in papers and are one of the main reasons why "RLHF effects are not reproducible". 

Rejection sampling and Best-of-N : once an RM is available, one can sample N candidates for each prompt, select the best with the RM, and then run SFT on the best—this is a "brute-force alignment" route without RL, simple to implement and with better reproducibility than PPO, and it is the default of many practical systems. Its cost is that inference cost grows with All Stages of Large-Model Training 54 

N and it is subject to the same constraints on RM quality; when the RM has a systematic bias, Best-of-N amplifies that bias (same origin as reward hacking). 

Scale–quality empirics of preference data : most public reports indicate that the qual-ity and diversity of preference data matter more than sheer quantity (denoising, difficulty stratification, coverage of multiple tasks), but the conclusion "how many pairs is enough" de-pends heavily on the base model and task (C). A recurring phenomenon is that a small amount of high-quality, debiased preference pairs can outperform a large amount of noisy data—isomorphic to Ch5’s experience that "deduplication takes precedence over adding data". 

The cost structure of RLHF : annotation (preference pairs) is usually the largest cost item, followed by the compute of RM training and the RL loop, and the engineering investment for repeatedly iterating the reward model. This means "algorithm choice" often yields to "whether enough high-quality preference data can be obtained"—when data is expensive, DPO/offline methods, RLAIF, and Best-of-N are more cost-effective; full RL pays off only when verifiable rewards or a sufficient annotation budget exist. 

## 8.4 RL Algorithms and Verifiable Rewards 

PPO [196] is the classic algorithm of RLHF; its strength is stability and maturity, and its weak-nesses are the need to train a value network, a heavy burden on GPU memory and hyperparame-ter tuning, and sensitivity to hyperparameters. GRPO (DeepSeekMath [224]) removes the value network and replaces it with a within-group relative advantage estimate, reducing the burden on GPU memory and tuning, and has become one of the mainstreams of reasoning RL. Other routes include REINFORCE-style variants without a value function, and "RL-like" schemes based on rejection sampling plus filtering; their common goal is to lower the engineering threshold of RL .

RLVR (reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards) performs RL on tasks such as mathematics and code where the answer can be automatically judged, using rule-based rewards. DeepSeek-R1 [45] uses an RL pipeline dominated by verifiable rewards to significantly improve reasoning performance, and at the technical-report level (not independently reproduced) notes that reasoning length and spontaneous self-verification behavior grow with RL. The appeal of RLVR is that the reward is noise-free and can be scaled up at large scale; its boundary is that a hard-to-bridge reward-design gap exists between verifiable tasks (with determinate answers) and subjective/open tasks (without determinate answers) (Problem 6)—generalizing RLVR’s "reasoning improvement" to "general capability improvement" requires evidence, and evidence is currently insufficient. 

Key variables : the KL constraint coefficient (too loose and it drifts from the base model, too tight and it cannot learn), reward normalization and length penalty (to prevent "the longer the better"), sampling temperature and group size (GRPO), and whether "cold-start" SFT data is mixed in. The optimal values of these variables are highly task-dependent, and cross-work reports must give the full configuration, otherwise one cannot tell whether it is "the algorithm is better" or "the tuning is cleverer". 

Implementation details of PPO : PPO in RLHF typically uses GAE to estimate advan-tages and applies clipping to the policy and value function; the key stabilization comes from a per-token KL penalty (relative to the SFT/reference model) and batch normalization of advantages. The reward is usually "RM terminal score + per-token KL penalty term", so the KL coefficient simultaneously controls "how much is learned" and "how far it drifts from the base model", making it one of the most sensitive hyperparameters. The value network must be the same scale as the policy or smaller, and its training is itself an extra optimization problem—this is precisely the motivation for GRPO’s removal of the value network. 

Reward design for RLVR : the advantage of verifiable tasks is that the reward is "hard" (correct/incorrect, test pass/fail), but one must handle: format reward (whether it contains a separator between the reasoning process and the final answer), partial correctness (sub-goal All Stages of Large-Model Training 55 

achievement), and reward scale (a 0/1 sparse reward is nearly unlearnable for long-chain reasoning and often needs shaping). These design choices are given in reports such as DeepSeek-R1 [45] but are not universal; mistaking "performance improvement induced by format reward" for improvement of "reasoning capability" is a common extrapolation error of Grade C conclusions. 

The relationship between RL and DPO/SFT : the three are not mutually exclusive but cover different needs—SFT provides format and basic behavior, DPO performs offline fitting on a fixed preference distribution, and RL optimizes within an explorable space (including verifiable tasks). In practice "staged mixing" is common: first SFT, then DPO to stabilize behavior, and finally RL on verifiable capabilities such as reasoning/tools. The weights and order of the mix significantly affect the result, but public systematic ablations are scarce (C). 

GRPO’s within-group advantage mechanism : GRPO samples a group of responses for each prompt and obtains the advantage by normalizing within the group by the mean/standard deviation of rewards, thereby eliminating the value network [224]. Its cost is that the advantage estimate depends on group size and within-group diversity—too small a group makes the estimate noisy, too large a group makes it costly; and the normalization is sensitive to reward scale and must be coordinated with reward design. These details are given in the report but have not yet been systematically ablated, so the conclusion "GRPO is better than PPO" should be marked C. Industrial efforts have pushed GRPO scaling further (e.g., MiMo-V2.6 [412]), utilizing fully asynchronous pipelines across massive batches (updating 2.7B–3.7B tokens per step) and introducing groupwise agentic grading—combining Groupwise Reward Synthesis (GRS) for dynamic contrastive rubrics and Groupwise Advantage Redistribution (GAR) to steer concise exploration—achieving unified multi-task RL across coding, reasoning, and multimodal agent environments (Grade C). 

Online vs. offline preference optimization : offline methods (the DPO family, rejection sampling) use only a fixed preference dataset, simple and stable but limited by data coverage; online methods (PPO/GRPO) sample with the current policy and score in real time by an RM/judge, able to explore out-of-distribution behavior but with complex and unstable engineer-ing. The choice depends on whether a reliable and real-time queryable reward signal is available: on verifiable tasks the benefit of online exploration is clear, and on subjective tasks offline is more robust (B/C). 

## 8.5 RL-Free Preference Optimization 

DPO [32] proves that one can directly optimize the policy from preference pairs without an ex-plicit RM or RL loop: its closed-form solution turns "preference maximization + KL constraint" into a differentiable classification-style loss, greatly lowering the engineering threshold. This reduces preference alignment from "large-scale RL engineering" to "a single supervised training run", one of the most important accessibility improvements in recent years. 

The derivation intuition and failure modes of DPO : the preference-maximization problem under a KL constraint has a closed-form optimal policy, and its log-ratio log πθ (yw )   

> πref (yw)

−

log πθ (yl)  

> πref (yl)

can be wrapped by a sigmoid into a binary classification loss, so the "implicit reward" 

r = β log( πθ/π ref ) needs no separate modeling. This explains why DPO is simple and also foreshadows its limitation: it is effective only on the distribution covered by the preference pairs , lacking RL’s active exploration; moreover, the loss is sensitive to sequence length and the choice of reference model [227][229]. DPO is therefore more like "offline preference fitting" than "online objective optimization". 

Synthesis and distillation of preference data : using a strong model to generate pref-erence pairs or directly generate "preferred answers" has become a low-cost route, at the risk of distilling the strong model’s biases and errors into the target model and accumulating them under iterative use. This makes "whose data to use for alignment" a choice problem constrained in both capability and diversity (same origin as the synthetic-data debate in 5.8). All Stages of Large-Model Training 56 

Its derivatives target specific weaknesses: IPO [214] corrects DPO’s overfitting under deter-ministic preferences; KTO [40] addresses the setting with only good/bad one-sided labels (no pairwise data needed); ORPO [41] merges SFT and preference optimization and needs no refer-ence model; SimPO [43] uses a reference-free length-normalized objective. Known issues include sensitivity to length bias [227] (preferring longer answers that are mistakenly learned as "bet-ter") and neglect of "token-level" signals [229] (sequence-level preference masks the contribution of key tokens). Overall, the DPO family is known for simplicity and stability, but on complex reasoning tasks it often needs to be paired with RL (such as GRPO/RLVR); for a survey see [397][398]. 

Evidence strength : the DPO family’s "can align, low cost" is a relatively strong Grade A consensus (multiple independent evaluations agree); but "which variant is better on which task" is Grade B/C—differences in evaluation sets, base models, and data across papers make horizontal comparison almost non-transferable. 

Why horizontal comparison within the DPO family is difficult : the base models, preference-data scale and source, evaluation sets, decoding, and length correction differ across variant papers, so "A scores a few points above B" is almost entirely non-transferable. Only 

mechanistic differences are transferable (whether a reference model is needed, whether one-sided labels are supported, whether length is normalized), not scores. This is consistent with Ch5’s conclusion that "recipes are not transferable, mechanisms are reusable". 

The role of the reference model and β: the reference model and the temperature coefficient β in DPO/RLHF jointly constrain how far the policy deviates from the base model. A larger β is more conservative (closer to SFT), a smaller β is more aggressive (may deviate from the distribution and cause degeneration). The choice of β is therefore essentially a trade-off between "alignment strength" and "capability preservation"; β values across papers are not directly comparable unless the reference model and preference data are reported as well. 

The boundary of the equivalence between DPO and RLHF : theoretically DPO’s closed-form solution corresponds to a specific form of KL-constrained RLHF; but this equivalence relies on "the preference data comes from the reference policy distribution" and on the BT assumption holding. Once the policy deviates from the data distribution (iterative training, multi-round preferences), the equivalence breaks down, and DPO may "overfit old data" while RLHF can still explore. This explains why multi-round/online scenarios often return to RL, and also explains DPO’s strong dependence on the reference model and β.

Applicable scenarios of the variants : KTO [40] suits production scenarios where only good/bad one-sided labels exist and pairwise data is hard to construct; ORPO [41] suits scenarios that want to complete SFT and alignment in one training run and do not want to maintain a reference model; SimPO [43] suits scenarios concerned with length controllability and the overhead of a reference model; IPO [214] suits scenarios with strong preference determinism where DPO overfitting is a concern. The choice should be based on data form and engineering constraints , not leaderboard scores. 

## 8.6 Tool Use and Multimodal RL 

Tool-use RL incorporates "call a tool—observe the result—continue reasoning" into a trajectory-level reward, and is the training foundation of agent capabilities; its essence is multi-turn sequential decision-making with external feedback , where rewards are often sparse and delayed. Multimodal RL, by contrast, optimizes vision-language tasks with preference or verifi-able signals and requires additional handling of modality alignment and hallucination. The common challenge of the three is reward sparsity and credit assignment (which decision step led to the good result). Countermeasures include: process rewards/intermediate supervision, decomposing long tasks into verifiable subtasks, and constructing dense signals with rules or tool results (such as code execution, retrieval hits). Evidence status : public system experiments in tool RL and multimodal RL are scarce, conclusions are mostly single-All Stages of Large-Model Training 57 

party reports (C), and evaluation protocols (tool set, environment, budget) differ enormously, making horizontal comparison nearly impossible—this is the weakest part of evidence in this chapter. 

The evaluation gap of agent RL : the effectiveness of tool RL depends on the environment (tool set, sandbox, budget), and the environment is usually not public, so the conclusion that "a certain model is better at using tools" is nearly impossible to verify independently (C). Reproducible progress mostly comes from open-source environments and narrow settings with fixed tasks; when transferring to real systems, tool reliability, error recovery, and long-horizon consistency become the new dominant factors, and these are rarely modeled explicitly in the training objective. 

The hallucination dimension of multimodal RL : the "hallucination" of vision-language models (describing content that does not exist in the image) can be partially suppressed under preference alignment, but if the preference data itself rewards "detailed description", hallucina-tion is exacerbated. Multimodal alignment must therefore explicitly distinguish "more detailed" from "more accurate" in reward design, and this requires fine-grained visual grounding evalua-tion; current evidence is mostly Grade C (Chapter 10). 

Countering reward sparsity : besides process rewards and shaping, curricula (expanding task difficulty from easy to hard), self-play/self-sampling (using the current policy to generate verifiable intermediate tasks), and decomposing long tasks into independently verifiable subtasks are all common means of alleviating sparsity and credit assignment. Their common cost is shifting the burden of "task design" onto data and reward engineering; effectiveness is highly task-dependent and public comparable evidence is limited (C). 

Reward design for tool RL : commonly used signals include "final task success" (sparse), "correct tool-call format" (dense but weak), and "usable retrieval/execution result" (semi-dense). Their combination can provide a learning signal early on, but too high a weight on weak signals induces "abusing tools to get the format right". A more robust practice is to stratify format and outcome rewards and, as training progresses, shift the weight from format to outcome. 

Data and pipeline for multimodal RL : multimodal preference data (ranking multiple responses to the same image) is more expensive than pure text and more prone to mixing in irrel-evant factors (image quality, resolution, watermarks), so that the preference signal is polluted by visual noise. Multimodal alignment therefore imposes higher requirements on data cleaning and pairing quality control than text, and its evaluation protocol is also less standardized (Chapters 10 and 12). 

## 8.7 Safety Alignment and Red-Teaming 

Safety alignment aims to suppress harmful outputs, improve honesty, and reduce bias; methods include refusal training, constitutional self-critique [23], red-teaming and jailbreak defenses, and machine unlearning (unlearning, e.g. TOFU [which provides an evaluable setting for large-scale knowledge unlearning]). The core difficulty is the alignment tax : over-refusal harms usefulness ("over-refusal"), while insufficient safety training brings misuse risk; how to quantify the trade-off between the two and establish empirical laws with respect to model scale and data mixture remains an open problem (Problem 8). 

The role of red-teaming : red-teaming discovers jailbreaks and failure modes, and its findings should flow back into training data and reward design rather than serving only as a pre-release gate. But red-teaming results are mostly qualitative evidence of "report upon discovery" (C), lacking comparable coverage and intensity metrics, making the "sufficiency" of safety alignment hard to judge objectively. Moreover , RL optimization itself can be exploited by "reward hacking" (the policy exploits loopholes in the RM, see 8.8), so safety cannot rely on reward shaping alone and must be suppressed at the level of the training mechanism. 

The measurement difficulty of the alignment tax : an ideal metric needs to measure both "refused what should be refused" and "answered what should be answered", but "whether All Stages of Large-Model Training 58 

it should be refused" itself depends on contested value judgments and varies by culture/policy. The "safety–usefulness" curves of different institutions therefore cannot be directly overlaid and compared; when reporting the alignment tax, the policy definition, evaluation set, and decision protocol should be given together, otherwise the numbers are uninterpretable. 

Machine unlearning and model editing : to satisfy data-deletion or safety requirements, models need "unlearning" or "model editing" (e.g. the ROME family). The difficulty is local deletion without harming generalization , and the measurement of "forgetting cleanly" is itself contested—there are two dimensions: "whether the knowledge can still be elicited" (robustness) and "whether related capabilities are collateral damage" (side effects). Existing evaluation settings (such as TOFU’s synthetic unlearning benchmark) provide a reproducible starting point, but real-world unlearning remains an open problem. 

Levels of safety alignment : it can be divided into 1○ training-time (re-fusal/preference/constitutional); 2○ inference-time (system prompts, classifiers, decoding con-straints); 3○ release-time (red-team gating, usage policy). None of the three can be omitted: training-time alignment alone is easily bypassed by jailbreaks, while inference-time filtering alone cannot prevent the model from "internalizing" harmful capabilities. A systematic comparison should report the strength of each of the three levels, rather than looking at a single level only. 

## 8.8 Key Controversy: Does RL Extend the Reasoning Boundary 

The controversy centers on the Pass@1 vs. Pass@K debate . One camp argues that RL (especially RLVR) extends the upper limit of the model’s reasoning capability, manifested as a continuous improvement in Pass@1 [45][224]; the other camp holds that RL mainly reweights the existing solution distribution and improves sampling efficiency , manifested as a possible narrowing of distribution coverage (Pass@K) [400][401]—that is, the model becomes better at "saying the right answer" but the variety of solvable paths it masters does not increase, and may even decrease. 

How to interpret : both conclusions may be correct, depending on the task and scale— if solvable paths already exist in the training data, RL is more likely to "reweight"; if the search guided by the reward signal can lead the model to discover new combinations outside the training distribution, it may "extend". Existing evidence is not yet sufficient to give a universal conclusion, and it depends heavily on the training setup (Problem 5). The judgment requires reporting both Pass@1 and Pass@K and testing generalization on out-of-distribution tasks; looking at Pass@1 alone cannot distinguish the two explanations. 

How to use Pass@K to decide "reweighting vs. extension" : if RL only reweights, then as K increases and sampling is sufficient, the Pass@K of the model before and after RL should converge (similar coverage); if it extends, then the post-RL model still exceeds at larger K (correct paths appear that the base model almost never produces). The judgment therefore requires comparing the Pass@K curves at sufficiently large K and with sufficient sampling; a single-point Pass@K or Pass@1 alone is insufficient to distinguish them [400][401]. 

Another boundary view: test-time compute : some reports attribute the reasoning improvement to longer chains-of-thought and spontaneous self-checking behavior after RL [45] (single report, Grade C). If so, then "capability extension" may partly come from an increase in test-time compute (longer generation means more sampling), rather than new capabilities within the parameters—Snell et al. [409] systematically demonstrate that guiding search via process reward models (PRMs) and adaptively allocating test-time compute allows smaller mod-els to match or exceed models 14 × larger under compute-matched budgets (Grade B evidence), formalizing test-time scaling laws. Regarding autonomous self-correction, Kumar et al. [410] (SCoRe) reveal that offline SFT on correction traces suffers from distribution shift and behavior collapse, whereas multi-turn on-policy RL achieves genuine self-correction gains without external oracle assistance (a 15.6% relative improvement on MATH, Grade B evidence). This makes the Pass@K judgment all the more necessary: comparison must be made under matched compute All Stages of Large-Model Training 59 

budgets , otherwise the long chain itself manufactures the illusion of "stronger". 

Related risks : reward hacking —the policy learns to satisfy the reward proxy rather than the true objective (e.g. longer answers, a more confident tone, exploiting rule loopholes). Countermeasures must act on the training-time mechanism (diversity of reward ensembling, periodically retraining the RM with new data, constraints on policy deviation) rather than inference-time filtering alone (Problem 13). This forms three facets of the same problem together with the RM’s out-of-distribution generalization in 8.2 and the safety alignment in 8.7. 

Common forms of reward hacking : 1○ length/format drift (more verbose, more punc-tuation or bullet points); 2○ sycophantic answers (catering to the asker’s presuppositions rather than seeking truth); 3○ rule loopholes (matching the shallow pattern of test cases on verifiable tasks rather than solving the problem); 4○ exploitation of the judge model’s preferences (target-ing the wording/structure favored by LLM-as-judge). All four stem from the structural fact that 

the reward is a proxy , so defense must act on the objective itself (reward diversity, genuine live benchmarks, continuously updated RMs), not merely on output filtering.
