## 8.10 Practical Recommendations and Cross-Stage Connections 

Recommendation: first achieve format and basic capability with SFT, then perform preference alignment ; when data is limited, prefer the DPO family ([32][43]) and explicitly All Stages of Large-Model Training 60 

correct length bias [227]; when strong reasoning is needed, consider GRPO/RLVR together with process rewards [224][45][33], but report both Pass@1 and Pass@K to determine whether it is "reweighting" or "extension"; safety and usefulness must be evaluated jointly to avoid the alignment tax, and red-teaming findings should flow back into training rather than serving only as a release gate. Cross-stage connections : the quality of alignment depends on the SFT starting point (Chapter 7) and the base model’s capabilities (Chapter 6); the reward hacking and hallucination problems exposed by alignment in turn drive the design of the data side and the pretraining objective (reverse empowerment, Figure 1). Open problems : Pass@1/Pass@K (Problem 5), extending verifiable rewards to subjective tasks (Problem 6), the robust boundary between process vs. outcome rewards (Problem 7), suppressing reward hacking at training time (Problem 13). 

An actionable research-design example : for Problem 5, one can fix the data and compute on the same base model and change only the RL algorithm (PPO/GRPO/DPO), while reporting both Pass@1 and Pass@K and testing generalization on out-of-distribution tasks; for Problem 7, one can make a PRM and an ORM compare under the same annotation budget to test "whether step-level signals persistently dominate on long-horizon reasoning"; for Problem 13, one can construct an experiment of "deliberate misalignment between the reward proxy and the true objective" to measure the emergence threshold of hacking behavior under different defenses (reward ensembling, periodically retraining the RM). All three can be completed within a moderate compute budget. 

How to evaluate alignment effectiveness : alignment evaluation should cover at least four categories: 1○ behavioral compliance (format/refusal/tool-call success rate); 2○ capability preservation (the regression margin on general tasks before and after alignment); 3○ preference win rate (pairwise evaluation with length/style correction); 4○ safety (jailbreak and harmful-output rates). If any one is missing, "more pleasing to the evaluation" may be misread as "more aligned". An ideal report should give all four categories together with the judge and protocol used, otherwise the alignment conclusion is uninterpretable. 

Summary of the evidence distribution in this chapter : the method mechanisms of Ch8 (DPO can align, PPO/GRPO are usable, PRMs help reasoning) have Grade A/B consensus, but the three core questions "whether the capability boundary is extended", "whether general reasoning transfers", and "whether safety alignment is sufficient" fall almost entirely at Grade C or blank. This chapter therefore offers reusable tools and an interpretive framework ,not a transferable recipe—consistent with the expectation in Section 1.2 about the proportion of Grade C evidence (Appendix E). 

The implication of this controversy for training strategy : if RL only reweights, then the marginal return on investing compute in RL lies mainly in "raising the probability of known correct paths" (improving Pass@1, reducing sampling cost), and capability extension should be shifted to data and pretraining; if RL can extend, then new solutions can be discovered under limited data, and the investment in RL should be increased. Problem 5 is therefore not a purely academic debate but a strategic judgment that decides "where the compute should go"—which is also why it is listed as one of the core open problems of this survey. 

# 9 Continual Optimization  

> This chapter, together with Ch5–Ch8, serves as a pilot of the dual-form "general framework + focused survey" (sub-survey). Evidence note: the mechanistic evidence for compression-type methods (quantization/pruning/distillation/merging) is relatively stable, but how "post-compression capability and safety change jointly" is mostly reported as isolated single points (B/C). Target length: 8,000–9,000 characters. All Stages of Large-Model Training 61

## 9.1 Definition and Scope 

Conclusion first: continual optimization refers to the further training and compression performed after alignment and before deployment, producing the deployed model θ3; it aims to "maintain or improve capability at lower cost," and its central risk is the alignment tax (the degradation of general capability or safety behavior caused by changes made for efficiency/domain/safety). 

Included : continued pretraining and domain adaptation, long-context extension, model merg-ing, pruning, distillation, quantization-aware training, model editing, and machine unlearning, along with continual learning. Not included : inference-time acceleration (KV cache, specula-tive decoding) and serving engineering—these do not change the weights and belong to Chapter 13. 

Why classify this as "continual optimization" rather than "post-training" : SFT and alignment (Chapters 7 and 8) change the "behavioral policy," whereas continual optimiza-tion mostly changes the "carrier that bears the policy" (precision, size, structure, knowledge content)—it can be performed after alignment, or conversely may require realignment. What the two share is that the "capability cost" incurred must be measured; the difference lies in the object of optimization (policy vs. carrier). 

Three types of configuration in this stage : 1○ objective (preserve capability, expand domain, improve efficiency, change facts, remove information); 2○ means (continued training, weight transformation, structural transformation, knowledge editing); 3○ constraints (budget, latency, precision, compliance). The interleaving of these three makes "optimal compression" highly dependent on the deployment scenario: offline batch processing can tolerate larger models, whereas online serving is strictly constrained by latency and GPU memory. 

The unifying nature of failure modes : Nearly all methods in this chapter face the same tension— stability vs. plasticity (retaining old capability vs. acquiring new changes). Continued pretraining, editing, unlearning, and continual learning are the direct manifestations of this tension; quantization, pruning, and distillation touch the same problem in the form of "numerical/structural perturbations." Therefore the through-line for interpreting this chapter is: any change must simultaneously report both its "benefits" and the "dimensions it weakens." 

The stage order is not fixed : In real pipelines, the various steps of continual optimiza-tion may occur before or after alignment—domain continued pretraining can precede alignment (reducing subsequent conflicts), while quantization/pruning usually follows alignment (avoiding retraining). Thus a "stage" is a functional division rather than a strict timeline; the arrows in Figure 1 should be understood as "the usual order" rather than "the only order." 

Why inference-time optimization is not included : KV-cache reuse, speculative decod-ing, and batch scheduling reduce latency without changing the weights and belong to the system/serving layer (Chapter 13). In contrast, all methods in this chapter change the weights or structure, and thus change what the model "is" rather than "how it runs." This distinction avoids conflating "engineering acceleration" with "model compression." 

The three levels of weights, behavior, and knowledge : Continual optimization can act at three levels—the weight level (quantization/pruning/merging change numerical values and structure), the behavior level (distillation/alignment change the output distribution), and the 

knowledge level (editing/unlearning/continued pretraining change specific content). Different methods often act across levels (e.g., continued pretraining changes both knowledge and behav-ior), which is the fundamental reason side effects are hard to localize: a change rarely stops at a single level. 

## 9.2 Continued Pretraining and Domain Adaptation 

Continued pretraining further updates the base model on a domain corpus via NTP, then (op-tionally) redoes SFT/alignment to restore instructability. Its benefits and risks coexist: domain adaptation improves specialized tasks but may induce forgetting of general capability; the two-All Stages of Large-Model Training 62 

stage mixture ratio [407] and the data-mixture law [86] indicate that the choice of ratio is critical to success. In practice, a lower learning rate and a domain–general mixed corpus are commonly used to suppress catastrophic forgetting. 

The dispute over the ordering of the two stages : The mainstream approach is to perform domain continued pretraining first and then SFT/alignment ("domain post-training"); other work performs alignment first and then domain adaptation, at the cost of more easily damaging safety behavior. The choice of order depends on whether "domain knowledge" or "safety/instructed behavior" is more fragile: domain corpora often lack safety samples, so per-forming domain pretraining last would dilute safety alignment; hence safety alignment should usually be redone after domain pretraining (B/C). 

The magnitude of forgetting : Domain continued pretraining typically damages general capability more than SFT (Section 7.7), because the corpus distribution shift is larger and the number of update steps is greater. Mitigations include mixing in general replay data, constraining the learning rate and number of steps, or limiting updates in parameter space (LoRA-style con-tinued pretraining). The evidence for systematically comparing these measures remains mostly isolated single points (B/C). 

The substitutive relationship with retrieval : Domain knowledge can be written into the weights through continued pretraining, or attached externally at inference time through retrieval augmentation (RAG). The former changes the weights and is expensive but incurs zero inference overhead; the latter does not change the weights and is easy to update but is constrained by retrieval quality. The choice depends on the frequency of knowledge updates and the latency budget, rather than on a single notion of superiority or inferiority (Sections 9.10 and 13). 

The amount of domain corpus and the learning rate : Domain continued pretraining usually requires only a very small amount of domain tokens relative to the total pretraining volume to be effective, but too few are insufficient to change behavior and too many aggravate forgetting. The learning rate is significantly lower than in pretraining and is often paired with warmup and cosine annealing; the specific magnitude varies with the domain and model scale, and public ablations are limited (B/C). A prudent approach is to first determine, on a small-scale proxy, the window in which "domain gains appear and general regression is acceptable." 

Empirical rules and risks of the mixture ratio : Common domain:general ratios range from 1:1 to 1:10, depending on the domain scale and degree of shift; the larger the shift, the more general replay is needed. Ignoring the ratio yields the illusory gain of "domain surging while general collapses." Reports should present both in-domain and general benchmarks; otherwise the true cost cannot be judged. 

Pitfalls in domain evaluation : Domain benchmarks are often drawn from the same source as the training corpus, leading to inflation (contamination, Chapter 12); moreover, domain tasks are numerous but have few samples, giving high variance. Credible domain evaluation requires temporal hold-out (data from the period after training), cross-institution benchmarks, and man-ual spot checks; the absence of any of the three will overestimate the adaptation effect. 

Domain vocabulary and tokenizer extension : If a domain contains many new terms, new languages, or new symbols, extending the vocabulary (adding domain tokens and initializing their embeddings) can significantly improve token efficiency and representation learning for that domain. However, extending the vocabulary makes the embedding matrix incompatible with the original pretraining, requiring continued training of the new token embeddings on the domain corpus and attention to forgetting of old tokens. This is the direct interface between tokenization in Chapter 5 and domain adaptation in this chapter. 

The safety consequences of domain continued pretraining : Domain corpora typically lack alignment and safety samples, so continued pretraining dilutes the original safety behavior, potentially producing "improved capability but easier jailbreaking." Therefore safety alignment, or at least a safety regression, must be redone after domain pretraining; skipping this step is a common source of privacy/compliance incidents (Chapters 8 and 13). All Stages of Large-Model Training 63 

## 9.3 Long-Context Extension 

Long-context extension is a typical scenario of continued pretraining: it requires long-document data plus positional-encoding adjustments. Methods are described in Section 6.7 (RoPE [11], ALiBi [129], positional interpolation [143], YaRN [37]); after extension, a small amount of long-document continued training is usually needed to calibrate the attention distribution. Evaluation must distinguish "being able to read long" (locating information within long documents) from "being able to use long" (multi-hop reasoning across long documents), whose evidence strengths differ (Chapter 12). 

The trade-off between extrapolation and interpolation : Interpolation-type methods (compressing positional indices into the training range) are stable in the near domain but deterio-rate on very long extrapolation; frequency-adjustment-type methods ([37]) extend extrapolation capability but require parameter tuning. Either way, long-document fine-tuning is needed to eliminate the distribution gap of "trained short, tested long." Thus long-context capability is jointly determined by "positional-encoding design + long-document data provision," and neither can be omitted (echoing long-context SFT in Section 7.4). 

Cost structure : The longer the context, the larger the attention overhead and KV cache, and the higher the inference cost. This makes "extending context" and "improving efficiency" (9.7) often need joint trade-offs—in some scenarios, compressing the KV or sparse attention is more economical than simply widening the window (Chapter 13). 

The layers of long-context evaluation : One can distinguish "being able to read long" from "being able to use long" via three layers: "single-point retrieval (needle in a haystack)," "multi-hop aggregation," and "global summarization." The former is easily passed by most ex-tension methods, whereas the latter often exposes genuine defects. When reporting long-context capability, the layer should be specified; otherwise the numbers are not comparable (Chapter 12). 

Training–inference inconsistency : The extended positional encoding may encounter po-sitional combinations not covered during training when inferring on long documents, causing drift in the attention distribution. Mitigation requires covering the target length already during the extension stage and performing long-document calibration; changing only the inference-side positional scaling (training-free) carries high risk on very long extrapolation and must have its extrapolation boundary clearly annotated. 

The synergy between long context and retrieval : Window extension and RAG are not mutually exclusive—a long window lets the model directly consume more documents, while retrieval selects relevant content from a massive corpus. The combination of the two can balance "breadth" and "depth," but it also introduces two new problems: "middle content being ignored" (uneven attention within the long window) and retrieval noise. When evaluating a long-context system, retrieval quality should be reported alongside it (Chapter 13). 

The training cost of window extension : Extending the window from the training length to the target length requires continued training on long sequences—compute grows superlinearly with sequence length, and GPU memory is also constrained. Thus "window extension" is often combined with "improving the attention mechanism (sparse/linear/chunked)"; extending the window purely through training is expensive, and in engineering it is often supplemented by inference-side long-document memory management (Chapter 13). 

## 9.4 Model Merging 

Model merging : Weight averaging (Model Soups [296]), task vectors [300], and TIES-Merging [35] merge multiple fine-tuned models through arithmetic operations or the resolution of sign conflicts; the cost is extremely low but it is constrained by parameter alignment and interference. 

Why merging is effective yet cheap : For models fine-tuned separately on the same base, their weight differences (task vectors) often lie in a low-dimensional, approximately shared All Stages of Large-Model Training 64 

subspace, so direct addition can yield multi-task capability simultaneously. But when task-vector directions conflict or their scales differ greatly, simple averaging cancels out; TIES [35] mitigates this by pruning small weights, electing by sign, and then merging. The cost of merging is that it requires almost no training compute, making it one of the most cost-effective means of achieving "multi-task/multi-capability." 

Limitations : Merging requires that all models share the same base and architecture; merging may fail or produce unintended compromises for "behavioral-style" differences (such as different alignment styles); and the calibration and safety behavior of merged models lack systematic evaluation (B/C). Thus merging suits scenarios with complementary capabilities and a consistent base, and not scenarios involving cross-architecture or strongly conflicting objectives. 

The root of merging instability: scale drift : Direct averaging lets high-variance task vectors dominate the result, and may even amplify the weight scale. TIES [35]’s pruning and sign election, and weighting by task importance, all aim to suppress this drift. Normalizing models that share the same base and the same initialization–fine-tuning path before merging is a prerequisite for stable merging (B). 

Combining merging with adapters : Merging multiple LoRA [12] adapters (or combining them via adapter arithmetic) is lighter and more controllable than merging full-parameter models, because adapters share a frozen base and are naturally aligned. However, interference and conflict among adapters still exist, and at inference time one must decide whether to "merge into one" or "dynamically route," whose cost–benefit differs. 

The evaluation gap for merging : Evaluation of merged models mostly focuses on target-task scores and lacks checks of merging’s "side effects" (such as behavioral consistency, cali-bration, and safety). Whether two individually safe models remain safe after merging is not guaranteed—this gap and "post-compression safety drift" are two faces of the same problem (B/C). 

Intuition for linear mode connectivity : Solutions obtained by fine-tuning the same base often lie in the same "basin" on the loss surface, so their weights can be linearly interpo-lated/merged without significantly increasing the loss—this is the theoretical intuition underlying the feasibility of model merging [296]. But when the fine-tuning shift is too large (basins sepa-rated), merging fails. Understanding this condition helps predict "which models can be merged." 

Using merging for lightweight domain adaptation : Rather than performing domain continued pretraining on a general model (expensive and prone to forgetting), one can separately fine-tune a "domain expert" and a "general/safety" model and then merge them, or apply a directional superposition with task vectors [300]. This is very attractive in terms of cost, but it requires the two models to share the same base, and the safety behavior after merging must be re-validated (B/C). 

## 9.5 Pruning 

Pruning : Structured pruning (LLM-Pruner [304], Sheared LLaMA [316]) and unstructured pruning (Wanda [309]) reduce scale; see the survey [312]; after pruning, lightweight recovery training is generally required. 

Structured vs. unstructured : Unstructured pruning (zeroing weights individually) has a high compression rate but requires sparse hardware to realize speed gains, and in practice often yields no inference acceleration; structured pruning (removing entire heads/layers/channels) di-rectly reduces matrix dimensions and achieves real acceleration, but is more sensitive to precision and usually requires recovery training. Thus "how much to prune" is tied to "what hardware is used." 

Pruning and "capability load-bearing" : Different layers/heads contribute unevenly to capability—certain structures are strongly correlated with specific capabilities (long-range de-pendencies, multilingual ability, particular safety heads). Blanket magnitude pruning randomly damages these "load-bearing structures," so importance measures [309][312] and layer-wise search All Stages of Large-Model Training 65 

are the mainstream approach. The safety regression after pruning (whether safety heads are weakened) is almost never systematically reported, which is a clear gap (B/C). 

The recovery-training budget for pruning : Recovery training after structured pruning often requires considerable compute (approaching a small continued pretraining run), so "the inference cost saved by pruning" should be accounted for together with "the recovery-training cost" in the total cost of ownership. Claims of "free pruning" that ignore recovery training are usually not reproducible (B/C). 

The practical conditions for sparse acceleration : To realize speed with unstructured sparsity, hardware and kernels supporting sparse/2:4 modes are required; otherwise only GPU memory is saved, not time. Therefore the choice of pruning scheme must be aligned with the target hardware; otherwise "high compression rate" does not equal "faster." 

Pruning and MoE/sparse structures : Unlike post-hoc pruning, MoE and the like intro-duce sparse activation already during pretraining (Section 6.6). The two share the same goal (operating with less computation), but MoE selects experts through routing while pruning re-moves redundancy; MoE deployment is more complex (load balancing, GPU memory usage), whereas pruning is more general. Understanding the trade-off between "training-time sparsity" and "post-hoc sparsity" helps in choosing a route. 

The complementarity of pruning and distillation : Pruning compresses by deleting structure, while distillation improves a small model by transferring knowledge; the two are often combined as "prune first, then distill" or "prune and distill simultaneously" to maximize retained capability at a given scale. But the combination order likewise affects the outcome, and unified evaluation is lacking (consistent with the compression-chain ordering problem, B/C). 

## 9.6 Distillation 

Distillation : From a large model or an ensemble to a small model, including sequence-level distillation [289], DistilBERT [290], on-policy distillation for LLMs (MiniLLM [308], [310]), and alignment distillation (Zephyr [185]); see the surveys [293][335]. 

Offline vs. on-policy : Offline distillation trains the student on fixed data generated by the teacher; it is simple but suffers from "training–inference distribution mismatch" (the erroneous states the student encounters are not covered by the teacher). On-policy distillation has the student generate while the teacher corrects, mitigating the mismatch but at higher cost [308]. For generation tasks, distribution mismatch is the main source of precision loss, so on-policy is usually superior but more expensive. 

Distillation of capability and alignment : Distillation transfers not only "capability" but also "behavior and preferences"—an aligned teacher can distill safety/style to the student ([185]). But distillation is ineffective when "the teacher also errs," and it amplifies the teacher’s systematic biases; how to distill "reliability/calibration" rather than merely "answers" remains an open problem (B/C). 

Teacher scale and data volume in distillation : The larger the teacher and the smaller the student, the more pronounced the distillation gains, but when the "capability gap" is too large the student struggles to absorb it, so there exists an optimal teacher–student ratio. The volume of distillation data must be sufficient to cover the teacher’s behavior distribution; otherwise the student learns only a local part; where the data comes from (teacher-generated vs. real) affects the degree of distribution mismatch (B/C). 

Self-distillation and iterative distillation : Training a model again on its own (curated) high-quality outputs can improve consistency without introducing an external teacher, but carries the risk of "self-reinforcing errors"; multiple iterations may collapse. This shares the same origin as the synthetic-data collapse problem in Chapters 5 and 7, and diversity metrics should be monitored. 

Distillation and the small-model ecosystem : Distillation makes it possible for "a small model to inherit the capabilities of a large model" and is the main path for on-device and low-All Stages of Large-Model Training 66 

cost deployment. But the distilled student typically inherits the teacher’s capability distribution rather than its full potential, and may be constrained in the space it could optimize on its own; thus the relative merits of "distillation" versus "training from scratch on a small-scale dataset" depend on data and budget (B/C). 

Distillation and the alignment tax : Distilling from an aligned teacher can "package" safe/helpful behavior to the student, but it also transfers the teacher’s alignment tax (e.g., excessive refusal); moreover, the student’s robustness to out-of-distribution inputs is typically weaker than the teacher’s, and its safety boundary is more easily breached. Therefore evaluation of distilled models must include probing their "spillover behavior." 

Key points for distillation evaluation : One should simultaneously report the student’s capability retention rate relative to the teacher, robustness to out-of-distribution inputs, and the width of the safety boundary (refusal rate and jailbreak rate). Reporting only the "retention rate" masks the decline in robustness and safety—this is consistent with the through-line of this chapter that "a change must report both its benefits and the dimensions it weakens" (B/C). 

## 9.7 Quantization and Quantization-Aware Training 

Quantization : Post-training quantization (PTQ, such as GPTQ [21], AWQ [34]) compresses weights to the 4-bit level, is training-free, and deploys quickly; quantization-aware training (QAT) simulates quantization error during training, which can further preserve precision but at higher cost. 

The differential impact of quantization on capability and safety : Quantization error is uneven across dimensions—it usually has little effect on perplexity, but is more sensitive in long-chain reasoning, low-resource languages, and safety-refusal boundaries, potentially producing "safety behavior drift" (Problem 9). Therefore quantization evaluation must be dimension-specific and include safety items, rather than looking only at average perplexity. This systematic impact still lacks a unified benchmark (B/C). 

Weights vs. activations : Most PTQ focuses on weights (W4), while activation quantiza-tion (KV cache, activations) yields greater GPU-memory and latency benefits but is harder (the outlier problem). Thus "4-bit" often refers only to weights; reports should distinguish weight quantization from activation quantization to avoid misreading the acceleration magnitude. 

Quantization granularity : Per-tensor, per-channel, and group-wise (e.g., groups of 128) quantization have successively lower error and successively higher implementation complexity and overhead. The choice of granularity is a direct precision–efficiency trade-off; when reporting "4-bit," the granularity and whether activations are included must be stated; otherwise the numbers are not comparable (B). 

Outlier handling : A small number of very large outliers in the activations significantly raises quantization error, so preserving outlier channels (mixed precision) or performing channel scaling is the mainstream approach. This explains why "4-bit weights" is relatively easy while "4-bit activations" is harder—the difficulty lies in the outliers, not the bit width itself. 

KV-cache quantization : The GPU-memory bottleneck in long-context inference often lies in the KV cache. Low-bit quantization of the KV can significantly save GPU memory, but it is sensitive to attention precision and easily harms long-range retrieval; therefore KV quantization must be reported jointly with long-context evaluation, rather than tested only on short-sequence perplexity (B/C). 

The calibration set for PTQ : Training-free quantization relies on a small batch of calibra-tion data to estimate the distributions of weights/activations. If the calibration set’s distribution differs greatly from the deployment inputs, quantization error rises; if the calibration set is too small, the estimation is unstable. Thus the representativeness of the calibration set is key to quantization quality, yet it is often missing from reports—this is also one of the reasons quanti-zation results are hard to reproduce (B). All Stages of Large-Model Training 67 

The trade-off between QAT and PTQ : PTQ is training-free and iterates quickly, suited to rapid deployment; when PTQ precision is insufficient (especially at low bit widths or for activation quantization), QAT recovers precision by simulating quantization error, at the cost of requiring a training pipeline and compute. The selection depends on the target bit width and precision requirements: 2–3 bit or activation quantization often leaves no choice but QAT, whereas 4-bit weight quantization is usually sufficient with PTQ (B). 

Joint compression : Quantization, pruning, distillation, and merging are often applied in combination (e.g., "pruning + quantization" or "distillation + quantization"). Joint compression is not simple addition—pruning changes the weight distribution and thus affects quantization error, and quantization in turn changes the distillable student capacity. Therefore "1+1"-style extrapolation of gains is unreliable and must be measured end-to-end along the whole chain (B/C). 

## 9.8 Model Editing and Machine Unlearning 

Model editing : Locate-then-edit methods represented by ROME [14] can quickly correct facts, but a small number of edits may cause model collapse (the "butterfly effect" [324]); batch editing and stability remain difficult problems [294][297]. The appeal of editing is that "changing one piece of knowledge takes only one forward pass," but it does not distinguish "fact storage" from "capability use," and thus easily produces side effects. 

Machine unlearning : Machine Unlearning [291][326], TOFU [39], and WMDP [328] target privacy/copyright and dangerous-knowledge removal, respectively; how to remove target infor-mation without harming general capability remains unsolved (Problem 14). Methodologically there are three classes—"exact retraining/approximate/constrained optimization": retraining is the most reliable but costly; approximate methods are fast but may leave residue or over-erase. Measuring unlearning is also difficult—"the information is truly gone" requires membership-inference-style probing, rather than merely looking at direct question-answering. 

The common difficulty of editing and unlearning : Both seek "pointwise modifica-tion" yet face entangled representations—the target information and adjacent capabilities share parameters, so deletions and changes implicate them. This is the sharpest form of the stability– plasticity tension (9.1). 

The order and batching of edits : Editing item by item accumulates side effects, and the editing order also affects the outcome; batch editing must trade off "mutual non-interference" against "changing many at once." In practice, one usually validates stability on a small batch first ([324]) and, after confirming no collapse, scales up, performing capability regressions before and after editing. 

Measuring and the ethics of unlearning : Verifying "whether information has truly been removed" requires membership-inference/probe-style attacks, rather than direct questioning (the latter can be evaded by "pretending not to know"). Meanwhile, unlearning involves copyright and privacy compliance, requiring a distinction between "technical removal" and "legal exemption"; the latter often also requires data provenance and audit records (Chapter 13). 

Evaluating edits and measuring side effects : Evaluating an edit involves not only whether "the target fact is corrected" but also whether "neighboring facts are mistakenly changed," "whether general capability declines," and "whether it collapses after many edits." These three correspond to local correctness, local side effects, and global stability, respectively, and none can be omitted; public evaluations mostly report only the first (B/C). 

Research design for editing and unlearning : The two can be compared on the same base—measuring the score changes on the target side and the neighborhood side after "editing N facts" and after "unlearning a class of information," as well as the collapse threshold as N grows. Such experiments can uniformly answer "the capacity limit of pointwise modification," and are an affordable and high-value gap to fill (Problem 14). All Stages of Large-Model Training 68 

An engineering workflow for the "right to data deletion" : For compliance, a user may request deletion of the influence of their data on the model. Since one cannot "delete item by item" from already-trained weights, industrial practice mostly adopts a combined workflow of "traceable—retrainable—approximate unlearning": recording data provenance and its scope of influence, retraining or performing unlearning when necessary, and verifying residue by probing. The cost and feasibility boundary of this workflow remain open problems (Problem 14, B/C). 

## 9.9 Continual Learning and Pre-Deployment Optimization for Multimodal Models 

Continual learning : Learning sequentially over a data stream without forgetting; see the surveys [332][334][337]. Its challenges are that task boundaries are unknown, data cannot be replayed, and one must simultaneously resist forgetting and overfitting; the methods overlap heavily with "domain continued pretraining + replay + parameter isolation," and it is a gener-alized form of the tension in this chapter. 

Multimodal post-training and pre-deployment optimization : Pre-deployment opti-mization of multimodal models superimposes modality-specific challenges—quantization/pruning of the vision projector and encoder, the preservation of cross-modal alignment after compression, and changes in hallucination after distillation/quantization. Because multimodal evaluation de-pends more on generation quality, the conclusion of "capability preservation" after compression is usually weaker than for pure text (Chapter 12). In particular, vision encoders are more sensitive to quantization, and the hallucination rate may rise rather than fall after compression. 

Evaluation protocols for continual learning : An ideal evaluation should simulate a real stream with "unknown task boundaries and non-replayable old data"; if replay is permitted or boundaries are known, the method’s capability is overstated. Mainstream benchmarks differ in their conventions on "how much replay is allowed," making conclusions incomparable (B/C). 

The ordering of multimodal compression : Whether to quantize the vision encoder first or compress the LLM first, and whether to prune them separately, affects hallucination and cross-modal alignment. Empirically the vision encoder is more fragile to quantization and should use more conservative bit widths; however, systematic ablations are scarce (B/C). 

The superposition of continual learning and the alignment tax : Continual learning on an already aligned model brings both "forgetting" and "safety drift," and the two may mask each other (changes in safety behavior are misread as capability changes). Therefore the evalua-tion of continual learning, like that of continual optimization, should include a safety regression (9.10). 

## 9.10 Representative Work Table, Controversies, and Practice                                 

> Table 7: Chapter 9 representative works—continual optimization
> Direction Representative work Core idea Evidence Notes Domain continued pretraining [86] / [407] Mixture ratio and two stages BForgetting risk Long-context extension RoPE [11] / PI [143] / YaRN [37] Positional-encoding adjustment A/B Requires long-document calibration Model merging Soups [296] / TIES [35] Weight arithmetic merging A/B Requires same base Structured pruning LLM-Pruner [304] / Sheared [316] Removing structural units BRequires recovery training All Stages of Large-Model Training 69 Direction Representative work Core idea Evidence Notes Unstructured pruning Wanda [309] Per-weight sparsity BRealization depends on hardware Distillation DistilBERT [290] / MiniLLM [308] Sequence-level/ on-policy A/B Distribution mismatch Alignment distillation Zephyr [185] Distilling alignment behavior BAmplifies teacher bias Post-training quantization GPTQ [21] / AWQ [34] 4-bit weights ADimension-specific evaluation Model editing ROME [14] / [324] Locate-then-edit BButterfly effect Machine unlearning TOFU [39] / WMDP [328] Removing target information BGeneral capability damaged Continual learning [332][334][337] Sequential learning without forgetting B/C Surveys predominate

How to read the table : This table is indexed by "means"; a single deployment objec-tive often combines several means (e.g., "domain continued pretraining + 4-bit quantization + merging"), and the combined effects lack systematic study, which is one of the main reasons for reproducibility difficulties. 

The ordering problem in joint optimization : Real deployment often chains several means in sequence (continued pretraining →merging →pruning →quantization). Different orders yield different cumulative damage and mutual amplification effects; there is currently almost no research systematically comparing the order of the "compression chain," which is an important source of reproducibility difficulties. 

Strong coupling with evaluation : Each step of continual optimization changes the object being evaluated, so the evaluation protocol must be versioned along with the optimization chain; if "before-and-after comparisons on the same benchmark" do not simultaneously fix the prompt, decoding, and evaluation script, the error will exceed the true difference brought by compression. This is the premise for the credibility of this chapter’s conclusions (Chapter 12). 

Complete accounting of cost–benefit : The "benefits" of any compression/adaptation means (GPU memory, latency, domain score) should be listed in the same table as the "costs" (recovery training, realignment, evaluation, engineering complexity, quality regression). Claims that report only benefits systematically overestimate value; converting "quality regression" into a cost is the starting point of rational selection. 

Connection with deployment : The endpoint of continual optimization is deployment (Chapter 13)—hardware, concurrency, latency SLA, and compliance requirements jointly de-termine the "acceptable" degree of compression and the knowledge-cutoff strategy. There-fore continual optimization should not be conducted in isolation from deployment constraints; front-loading the deployment profile into the optimization design can avoid the rework of "over-compression requiring rollback." 

Which open problems are most testable : 1○ "Is the order of the compression chain commutative?"; 2○ "The magnitude and predictability of safety regression after quantiza-tion/pruning"; 3○ "The pointwise capacity limit of unlearning." All three require only moderate compute and no new data, making them the most worthwhile directions to prioritize at this stage, and they also directly serve the deployment compliance of Chapter 13. 

Mapping to the six-dimensional framework : Data (domain/long-document corpus, cal-ibration set, replay set), objective (NTP / compression loss / editing objective / unlearning objec-tive), optimization (low-learning-rate continued training, training-free transformations), system (precision, hardware, latency), evaluation (joint capability + safety regression), risk (forgetting, alignment tax, safety drift, compliance). Among the six dimensions, the "system" dimension All Stages of Large-Model Training 70 

carries the highest weight in this chapter, which is exactly the opposite of pretraining (data-dominated). 

A minimal evaluation checklist for continual optimization : 1○ capability (general and target domain); 2○ safety (refusal boundary and jailbreak); 3○ calibration (whether confidence remains trustworthy); 4○ efficiency (GPU memory/latency/throughput); 5○ combined effects (the net change after chaining multiple means). If any of the five is missing, the conclusion is insufficient to support deployment decisions (Chapter 13). 

Key controversies : First, is compression "free"? Training-free quantization/merging claims near-losslessness, but the evidence is mostly on general perplexity and a single bench-mark; undocumented degradation may appear in safety, long-chain reasoning, and low-resource languages (B/C). Second, can editing/unlearning be pointwise? The optimists argue that parameter-level editing can precisely change knowledge; the pessimists rebut with the "butterfly effect" [324] and residue evidence [291]. The consensus is: pointwise modification is feasible for simple facts, but unreliable for entangled knowledge and safety behavior. Third, continued pretraining vs. retrieval . Writing weights and external retrieval are two routes, whose rela-tive merits depend on the frequency of knowledge updates and deployment constraints, rather than being absolute. 

Practical recommendations : Define evaluation before compression (clarify the ca-pabilities and safety items to be preserved); prioritize trying training-free quantization/merging to reduce cost ([21][34][35]); after domain adaptation and long-document extension, be sure to re-check alignment and safety (to guard against the alignment tax); validate the stability of editing and unlearning at small scale before scaling up ([324][14]); and perform a dedicated regression on the safety behavior after quantization/pruning. Cross-stage connection : Nearly every means in this chapter may "revert" the safety and instructed behavior of θ2, so each optimization should trigger a lightweight realignment or at least a safety regression—this is the key that distinguishes continual optimization from pure engineering compression (Figure 1). Open problems : How continual optimization can preserve alignment and safety behavior (Problem 9); how machine unlearning and copyright compliance can avoid harming general capability (Problem 14); and the systematic impact of quantization on safety behavior still lacks a unified benchmark. 

An actionable research design example : Fix an aligned model, construct several orders of a "compression chain" (e.g., quantization →pruning vs. pruning →quantization), measure the same set of capability and safety metrics after each stage, and plot a "cumulative-cost curve." This can answer the two currently unanswered questions of "which step harms safety the most" and "whether the order is commutative," with controllable compute. 

Summary of the evidence distribution in this chapter : The mechanisms of compres-sion methods and their gains on general benchmarks have relatively strong A/B evidence; but "how safety and capability change jointly after compression" is almost entirely isolated single-point reports at the B/C level, consistent with the pattern of Ch6–Ch8. Hence this chapter provides an evaluation framework of "change—cost" rather than a "lossless-compression recipe." 

# 10 Multimodal Training: A Cross-Cutting View  

> This chapter, together with Ch5–Ch9 and Ch12, serves as a pilot for the dual form of "general framework + focused survey." Note on evidence: the architecture and data pipeline (encoder–projector–LLM, interleaved data, contrastive alignment) have a fair amount of A/B-level literature, but the "multimodal scaling law," the "root causes of hallucination," and the "scalability of the unified token route" are almost entirely C-level or blank. Target length: 8,000–9,000 characters. All Stages of Large-Model Training 71

## 10.1 Architectural Paradigms 

Conclusion first: multimodal training is distributed across all stages, and its architectural paradigm determines the manner of data and alignment; hence this survey devotes a sepa-rate chapter to it from a "cross-cutting view" rather than treating it as yet another pipeline. According to how visual information is coupled with the language model, it can be grouped into three categories: encoder–projector–LLM (LLaVA [28], BLIP-2 [265], Qwen-VL [277], Qwen2-VL [288]): freeze or fine-tune the visual encoder and use a projector to map features into the LLM’s token space; cross-attention fusion (Flamingo [17]): incorporate visual features into the decoder via learnable gated cross-attention; unified discrete token / any-to-any : unify multimodality into discrete sequences (VQ codebook [3][8]) or end-to-end multimodal generation, a frontier route. For a survey see [402][404][405]; for a data perspective see [403]. 

The division of labor among the three paradigms : encoder–projector–LLM reuses mature LLM weights, trains stably, and has a mature ecosystem, and is currently the mainstay; cross-attention injects visual capability with a very small amount of training (Flamingo [17] freezes the LLM) and suits scenarios that "barely touch the language model"; unified discrete token pursues a "true multimodal foundation," but its training stability, token efficiency, and controllability remain open problems. Choosing a paradigm is choosing "how visual ev-idence enters the language model, and with how much bandwidth" —this choice runs through pretraining, SFT, and alignment (Chapter 5–9). 

The role of the encoder : the visual encoder (e.g., SigLIP [46], CLIP [10]) provides image semantics, while the projector/cross-attention performs the "features → language" translation. Whether the encoder is frozen and whether it continues to align on image-text pairs determines the ceiling of visual information and the engineering cost. The mainstream "frozen encoder + trained projector" is a cost-effectiveness compromise, not a theoretical optimum. 

Stage-wise training sequence : a typical multimodal recipe proceeds in stages—first train/align the projector (small data volume), then unfreeze and jointly fine-tune the LLM (the unfreezing timing is a major hyperparameter), and finally perform instruction tuning and preference alignment. "What to freeze, and when to unfreeze" is both a cost constraint and a regularization (preventing visual information from distorting and overwriting text capability); the temporal ordering is almost never shared across works, which makes recipes hard to compare directly (B). 

The capacity spectrum of the projector : from a single-layer linear projection (LLaVA [28]) to Q-Former (BLIP-2 [265]) and then to MLP/cross-attention, the projector capacity de-termines the bandwidth with which visual information enters the language model. Too small a capacity loses details, too large a capacity easily overfits and significantly increases computation; systematic comparisons under identical data are limited (B). 

The fusion trend of cross-attention and projectors : Flamingo’s [17] cross-attention injects images into attention, whereas LLaVA’s [28] projector stuffs images into the token stream. The two can be mixed (projection for global, cross-attention for dense), gradually blurring the boundaries of the three paradigms (B). 

Practical constraints on training stability : staged multimodal training is frequently interrupted by gradient explosion/divergence, and in practice it is stabilized by freezing, gradi-ent clipping, and segmented learning rates rather than by architecture alone. These engineering details are rarely disclosed, leading to the widespread phenomenon that "the same recipe fails when the implementation changes"—the transferability of stable recipes is a key practical prob-lem (Section 6.6, engineering convergence). 

Information redundancy across modalities : video frames are highly correlated with one another, and audio and ambient sound share a source, so the information density of multimodal data is far lower than that of "apparently equivalent" independent samples. Consequently, the "effective size of modality data" should be discounted: the higher the redundancy, the lower the benefit of an equal number of tokens, which partly explains the deviation of the multimodal All Stages of Large-Model Training 72 

scaling law (C). 

## 10.2 Essential Differences from Pure Text 

Multimodal training differs from pure text along five dimensions: first, data : image-text pairs contain "weak correspondence" noise, and the mixture of interleaved image-text (OBELISC [273]) and large-scale image-text sets (LAION-5B [64]) differs greatly from pure-text corpora; second, 

tokenizer : vision/audio require discrete or continuous representations, and the "per-modality token consumption" is not commensurate with text (Section 5.6; for a survey of discrete tokens see [394]); third, objective : it must combine NTP, contrastive (CLIP [10]), and matching ob-jectives, and the alignment objective may conflict with the language modeling objective; fourth, 

alignment : hallucination (errors in objects/attributes/counting) is more common in multimodal outputs and harder to evaluate automatically; fifth, evaluation : benchmarks (VQA, image cap-tioning) are more sensitive to prompt, sampling, and evaluation protocol, and the strength of evidence is usually lower than that of pure-text benchmarks. 

The commonality of the essential differences : all five dimensions can be reduced to "multimodal supervision signals are weaker and more mismatched "—image-text pairs are inherently weak annotation (one image corresponds to one sentence that does not necessarily describe the key information in the image), the semantic density of visual tokens is far lower than that of text tokens, and the contrastive/matching objective often competes with the NTP objective. Therefore the core problem of multimodal training is not "adding vision in," but "establishing reliable cross-modal correspondence under weak supervision." 

The fundamental nature of weak supervision : image-text pairs are "weak annotation"—a sentence usually describes only part of the content in the image, and may be un-related to the image. As a result, the "signal-to-noise ratio" of the multimodal training signal is lower than that of pure text: the model must generalize from a small amount of correspondence, requiring bridges across multiple samples. This explains why coarse alignment such as image-text contrast (CLIP [10]) can be effective yet insufficient to support fine-grained perception (Section 10.8). 

The asymmetry of semantic density : a text token often carries syntax and semantics, whereas an image patch carries only a local pixel distribution; compressing vision into a fixed number of tokens amounts to making the decision of "which pixels deserve attention" on the model’s behalf. The token allocation strategy is essentially a "priority ordering of visual atten-tion," which determines both cost and hallucination tendency (Sections 10.4 and 10.10). 

The protocol sensitivity of sampling : for multimodal text generation, the sampling temperature, repetition penalty, and image token order all have a large impact on metrics (Section 12.9). The same model can differ considerably in image-text captioning scores under different decoding settings, so any conclusion that "the description is better" must first fix the protocol. 

The "number" and "amount" of objectives : it is not that each of the five dimensions adds one objective, but rather the ratio between "data volume vs. number of objectives"—the more objectives, the relatively less data for each. Joint training with multiple losses requires determining the weight and sampling of each loss; the weights are almost entirely tuned by experience, and their sensitivity is itself an unknown (C). 

## 10.3 Data: Image-Text Pairs, Interleaved Documents, and Video 

Image-text pair data : the products of CLIP-style contrastive training [10] (LAION-5B [64], LAION-400M [59]) rely on "image-text correspondence" filtering; they are noisy but massive, and their quality directly affects downstream fine-tuning and hallucination. If the mixture of image-text pairs does not match the "real visual distribution" of deployment, it will systematically bias toward certain image styles and text styles. All Stages of Large-Model Training 73 

Interleaved image-text documents : inserting images into the text stream for training (OBELISC [273]) is closer to the real web distribution, but document segmentation, loss masking of modality tokens, and image-text correspondence all require dedicated design. The benefit of interleaved data is that "the context is the supervision" (the text surrounding an image constrains how it should be understood); the drawback is that automatic segmentation easily produces noise where image and text are unrelated. 

Video and 3D : video (Video-LLaVA [280]) and embodied settings (PaLM-E [267], RT-2 [276], OpenVLA [286]) introduce "spatial-temporal correspondence" into training; the data are more expensive and the alignment objectives are more complex (actions must be coupled with perception). Public data in these directions are scarce, and the evaluation protocols are far from stable (B/C). 

Cleaning of image-text pairs : LAION-family datasets [59][64] filter by CLIP similarity, but "related" does not equal "corresponding"—a high similarity score may reflect coarse-grained matching. Further cleaning (deblurring, deduplication, object-consistency checking) can improve downstream performance, but it also reduces data volume and may shift the distribution. The tradeoff between cleaning intensity and data scale is one of the core variables of a recipe (B/C). 

Resolution and token budget : higher resolution preserves details (OCR, small objects), but the token count and attention cost rise superlinearly. Qwen2-VL [288] and others balance detail against cost with a "dynamic resolution/pyramid" strategy. Hence "image quality" and "model scale–data scale" jointly determine the visual ceiling, rather than any single one deter-mining it alone (a multimodal variant of the scaling law of Chapter 6). 

The dynamics of image-text mixture during training : as training progresses, the proportions of visual instruction data, pretraining image-text pairs, and pure text may need to change—early on, alignment dominates; later, tasks dominate. Evidence for dynamic mixtures (e.g., curricula) mainly comes from single-point reports (B/C), but the default assumption that "the mixture is static" is likewise under-argued. 

Weakly supervised transformation of image-text correspondence : in interleaved documents, "the position where an image appears in the text" is not a reliable alignment signal— the image may be unrelated to the surrounding text. During training one may use only the text loss, impose no loss on image positions (image tokens do not enter the loss), or mask selectively; this connects with pure text on the "loss masking strategy" (Section 5.7) as the same problem (B). 

Deduplication of multimodal data : image duplication (similar images appearing multiple times) likewise causes overfitting and contamination (Section 5.4). Image deduplication requires visual hashing/nearest-neighbor clustering, which is more costly than text n-gram deduplication; yet the impact of duplicate images on hallucination and memorization is often neglected in the literature (B/C). 

Data sources and licensing : multimodal training data come in large part from crowd-sourced "works of others," and copyright and portrait-right disputes are prominent (LAION-family datasets have repeatedly faced litigation and takedown). In data governance (Section 12.3), multimodal training relies more than pure text on compliant channels for image copyright; otherwise the lifecycle of a dataset will be interrupted by legal terms (B/C). 

## 10.4 Tokenizer and Visual Representation 

The representation by which vision enters the language model is either continuous features (the projector route) or discrete tokens (the VQ codebook [3][8]; for a survey see [394]). Per-modality token consumption is a key engineering quantity: a 224px image typically produces 256–576 "image tokens" after projection, far more than the token count of the corresponding text, directly raising attention and GPU memory costs (Chapters 6 and 9). 

The tradeoff between discrete and continuous : continuous features have high fidelity and require no VQ training, but they mix "images" and "text" heterogeneously into the se-All Stages of Large-Model Training 74 

quence; discrete tokens make vision thoroughly textualized (Chameleon [285] and other any-to-any routes) and can be unified with NTP, but they must bear the VQ reconstruction loss and codebook degradation. The relation between quantization granularity (resolution, patch) and the number of tokens determines the cost curvature and is also a key leverage point of multimodal efficiency (Section 10.10). 

The significance of positional encoding for visual tokens : the arrangement of visual tokens (grid vs. sequence) requires suitable positional encoding; incorrect positional assumptions will cause the model to confuse spatial relations (up/down, left/right). The relative positions of image tokens and text tokens in interleaved documents must also be handled. Position handling is an implicit prerequisite for "spatial understanding," yet it is often unreported (B). 

VQ codebooks and reconstruction quality : the VQ codebook of the discrete route determines reconstruction quality and token semantics. Too small a codebook yields coarse reconstruction; too large a one has low utilization (codebook collapse); Chameleon [285] and others attempt to discretize first and then train uniformly, but the stability of cross-modal generation and conditional control remains a bottleneck (C). 

Dynamic resolution and token reallocation : the dynamic resolution of Qwen2-VL [288] splits a high-resolution image into multiple tiles, essentially allocating more tokens to "regions with more information." The direction of this sort of reallocation is correct, but the boundary problem of tile splitting (objects spanning tiles being cut off) and the connectivity of positional encoding still need verification (B). 

The relative position of tokens across modalities : in an interleaved image-text se-quence, the absolute numbering and relative order of image token positions affect adjacency relations; if image tokens are compressed into a single representative token, the spatial adja-cency information is lost. How to represent "the spatial relations among multiple regions and multiple texts" is a core weak point of serialization schemes (C). 

Grounding/scene graphs as intermediate representations : besides tokenization, pars-ing vision into a structured intermediate representation (bounding boxes, attribute graphs) and then feeding it to the LLM can significantly reduce hallucination about details; the cost is pipeline complexity and information loss. This "explicit structure" route coexists with the end-to-end route, and its boundary is undetermined (B). 

The attention cost of image tokens : the more visual tokens, the more self-attention grows quadratically (Section 9.7), and the coupling between image tokens and text tokens further amplifies computation. Hence "adding images" is not merely "adding a modality," but makes the KV cache and attention time expand nonlinearly with the token budget—this is precisely where the core leverage of efficient multimodality lies (Section 10.10). 

## 10.5 Pretraining and the Current State of the "Scaling Law" 

Multimodal pretraining starts from "first align the projector/encoder, then train the full model": BLIP [254] and BLIP-2 [265] pretrain on noisy image-text pairs via bootstrapping; BEiT [258] and CogVLM [279] explore stronger visual pretraining; Gemini [282] improves overall capability with large-scale mixed modalities. A key sharp question : text has a relatively stable scaling law (Section 6.3), but the relationship of "number of image tokens × data volume × model size" for multimodality still lacks a recognized law (C). 

The "images as a foreign language" metaphor : BEiT [258] and others advocate pre-training images as "a foreign language"—using self-supervision to turn vision into a sequence consumable by the LLM. The appeal of this perspective is that it reuses the entire mechanism of language modeling; the risk is that the semantic alignment of the "foreign language" and "language" still relies on weakly supervised image-text pairs, which cannot be compensated for by grammatical structure. 

The systemic consequences of freezing strategies : freezing the encoder saves compute and keeps the visual representation stable, but it limits the visual side’s ability to adapt to down-All Stages of Large-Model Training 75 

stream tasks; unfreezing may harm general visual capability and aggravate overfitting (Sections 6.8 and 7.6). "Freezing" is not a question of whether, but of "where to draw the line": whether each of the encoder, projector, and LLM is frozen constitutes a strategy grid that has hardly been systematically scanned. 

Synthesis of contrastive vs. generative objectives : CLIP-style contrast [10] learns text-image alignment, while NTP learns generation; the two objectives differ and may interfere with each other. Most recipes use "staging" (contrast first, then generation) rather than joint optimization to avoid the conflict, but staging itself may limit deep cross-modal alignment. The optimal weights and ordering for objective synthesis lack controlled study (B/C). 

The hypothesis of insufficient multimodal data scale : the capability ceiling of mul-timodal models is often viewed as "limited by the scale and quality of image-text data" rather than by architecture—this hypothesis is not yet settled, but if it holds, the multimodal scaling law would be closer to "data–architecture joint" than to a pure parameter law. This leaves the investment decision of "larger model vs. more image data" without a basis (C). 

The reverse influence of the pretraining objective on the encoder : when the en-coder is unfrozen and participates in contrastive/generative training, the visual representation drifts toward language semantics—this may improve downstream image-text tasks yet reduce the generality of pure visual retrieval/classification (forgetting, Chapter 9). "Visual-side early stopping" preserves generality at the expense of alignment, again an unscanned strategy grid (C). 

Data-dependence curve : even without a recognized scaling law, practical experience sup-ports a rough curve that "the benefit of modality data volume declines after a certain threshold"; the problem is that the threshold is coupled with model size and token budget, so no quantitative relation of the same order as the text scaling law can be given (C). 

Sharing of multimodal encoders : the same encoder (e.g., SigLIP [46]) is reused by mul-tiple multimodal models, meaning that "the latest progress in image semantics" can be shared across models, and model differences shift to the projector and alignment. This reuse lowers training cost, but it also causes "the true gain brought by new data/new encoders" to be con-founded with other changes in each recipe (B). 

## 10.6 Instruction Tuning and Alignment 

Multimodal SFT (IFT) trains on visual instruction data (image–instruction–answer triples): LLaVA [28], MiniGPT-4 [268], and InstructBLIP [270] each provide data and training recipes; Qwen-VL [277]/Qwen2-VL [288] bring dense perception such as grounding and OCR into in-struction tuning. The key difference in multimodal alignment : preference data (ranking multiple answers to the same image) is expensive and hard to correct, so progress in RLHF/DPO on multimodality lags behind text (Chapter 8). 

Joint mismatch of vision and text : if multimodal instruction tuning over-optimizes "visual instructions," it degrades pure-text capability (the mixture problem of Section 7.6); if visual constraints are ignored, it cultivates "fluent but distorted" descriptions. The two parts should be mixed and evaluated jointly, rather than optimized independently. 

The scarcity of multimodal preference data : visual preference ranking is expensive (one must look at the image to judge) and easily disturbed by irrelevant factors (clarity, style). Therefore multimodal DPO/RLHF is usually conducted on small-scale preference sets, and the evidence for its effectiveness and reliability mainly consists of single-point reports (C; a typical case of the evidence grading of Chapter 8). 

The interaction between hallucination and alignment : alignment optimizes "which answer is better," while the judgment of "better" may itself favor fluency over faithfulness— if the judge favors natural descriptions, the model is pushed toward "sounding pretty," even when untrue. Hence multimodal alignment needs to be coupled with hallucination evaluation; otherwise it will amplify hallucination in reverse during the RL stage. All Stages of Large-Model Training 76 

The absence of unified evaluation : compared with text, multimodality lacks a stable, consensus evaluation matrix (such as the MMLU+HELM combination for text), leading to frag-mented conclusions. Establishing standard "cross-modal–cross-task" evaluation (including hal-lucination, grounding, OCR, and text retention) is the public good most lacking in multimodal research today (echoing Ch12). 

Visual diversity of instruction data : if visual IFT data concentrate on a few templates and question styles, the model learns "template matching" rather than perception (instruction diversity of Chapter 7). Multimodality needs to expand diversity along four dimensions—object, scene, language, and task—otherwise hallucination and stereotyped descriptions will be solidified by the preferences of the IFT data (B). 

Outlook for multimodal alignment : scaling up visual preference data (human-supervised ranking + model-assisted filtering) is a direct extension of the text alignment path (Chapter 8), but it is constrained by "visual judgment being expensive and easily disturbed," and in the short term it is hard to catch up with the scale of text alignment. The gap in multimodal alignment is not a matter of method but of data infrastructure (C). 

## 10.7 Hallucination: Definition, Measurement, and Mitigation 

Hallucination refers to generating content inconsistent with the image (such as objects that do not exist, wrong attributes, wrong counting). Measurement requires protocols such as "the proportion of mentioned objects that do not exist in the image," but definitions vary across works, so they are not comparable across models (Problem 15; for evaluation see [271]). Factors triggering hallucination: insufficient image resolution, image-text pair noise, visual tokens being "drowned out" by text tokens in attention, and RL’s preference for "plausible-looking" answers. 

Mitigation and limitations : mitigation measures include better data quality, loss mask-ing (reinforcing visual supervision only on tokens that need grounding), and image-verification fallback at inference time. However, the phenomenon that "incorporating stronger visual su-pervision does not eliminate hallucination" indicates that hallucination is partly architectural 

(insufficient visual information bandwidth) rather than merely a data-level problem, so a single data fix is hard to eradicate. 

Fine granularity of hallucination evaluation : two types are commonly used—object existence (POPE-style discrimination) and self-generated statistics (the proportion of erroneous objects in generated descriptions). The former is cheap but suffers undersampling bias; the latter is close to actual use but depends on annotation. Cross-method comparison requires fixing the judgment mechanism (discrimination vs. self-generation); otherwise the numbers are incomparable [271]. 

Images as a second verification channel : at inference time, image embeddings can be used for a secondary check of "answer-image consistency," rejecting or correcting generations that conflict with the image. This sort of "test-time correction" can reduce hallucination, but it adds latency and complexity, and it fails when the image itself is low-resolution. It can serve as a supplement beyond SFT/RL (a variant of test-time compute of Section 6.6). 

The evaluation problem of hallucination mitigation : most mitigations report "a drop in POPE score," but POPE is a binary discrimination that deviates from the real generation distribution. Mitigation claims not re-verified on the generation side should be downgraded in interpretation; conversely, claims with only self-generated statistics also need discrimination-side re-checking. Mutual corroboration by both channels is the default standard of this chapter for hallucination evidence (B). 

The relation between hallucination and data scale : intuitively, "more and cleaner image-text pairs" should reduce hallucination, but the evidence shows more often that "pro-jector/encoder bandwidth" is more critical—under identical data, hallucination rates differ markedly across projector architectures. This casts doubt on the effectiveness of "buying more data" as a mitigation strategy (Problem 15, B/C). All Stages of Large-Model Training 77 

Catastrophic forgetting in multimodality : fine-tuning to add a new modality often degrades existing capabilities (pure text or existing modalities) (Section 7.5). Multimodality’s "new modality + re-mixture" amplifies the risk, and visual SFT forgetting is harder to detect— testing only pure-text benchmarks will miss it. Mitigation evidence (replay, joint objectives, LoRA) is mostly single-point, lacking cross-recipe comparison (B/C). 

## 10.8 Grounding, OCR, and Dense Perception 

Qwen-VL [277], Qwen2-VL [288], Kosmos-2 [272], and others bring grounding (bounding boxes/referring) and OCR into training, enabling the model to "see and say where." Such tasks refine visual evidence from "global semantics" to "spatial evidence," which directly constrains hallucination (an answer must be bound to a specific region). The cost is expensive data anno-tation (bounding boxes/masks), and evaluation depends on coordinate-matching protocols (IOU threshold, prompt). 

Tokenization of OCR : converting the text in an image into retrievable text tokens (OCR result + position) is the foundation of document understanding. OCR-token lets the model di-rectly "read characters" rather than guessing from pixels, significantly improving long-document and receipt understanding, but it also depends on OCR quality and layout analysis. Qwen2-VL [288] and others combine OCR with grounding, representing the mainstream direction of "fine-grained perception" (B). 

Protocol details of grounding evaluation : referring grounding is judged by an IoU threshold for a hit, and fine-grained tasks may require a high IoU; the prompt ("box out X" vs. a natural question) and the coordinate format (normalized vs. pixel) affect parsing and matching. When protocols are not unified, grounding scores are not comparable across models (B). 

Evaluation of OCR and document understanding : OCR metrics (character-level/word-level accuracy) and document question answering (requiring retrieval + reasoning) place different demands on the model; treating an OCR score as a document-understanding score is a common misreading. Document-type evaluation must also report layout-structure handling and long-text dependence (Chapter 12). 

Visual question answering vs. image captioning : VQA is a short-answer task, whereas captioning is open-ended long text; the former can be scored automatically, the latter almost only by humans (Chapter 12). Strong model performance on VQA does not guarantee caption quality; the decoupling of the two capabilities suggests that they should be reported separately in evaluation, avoiding generalizing from question-answering scores to captioning (B). 

## 10.9 Video, Embodiment, and Any-to-Any 

Video understanding : Video-LLaVA [280] explores a "align first, then project" spatiotemporal representation; video data are sparse, inter-frame redundancy is large, and evaluation protocols are still evolving (B/C). Embodied/vision-language-action (VLA) : RT-2 [276] and Open-VLA [286] transfer web knowledge to robot actions, and PaLM-E [267] argues that "large-scale language models can serve as embodied controllers"; their evaluation mostly relies on simula-tion and real environments, with high cost and high variance. Any-to-any : unified discrete routes such as Chameleon [285] hope to "train once and for all," but cross-modal generation and modality-conditional control are not yet stable (C). 

The audio modality : audio (speech/music/ambient sound) can likewise be integrated via "encoder–projector–LLM," and tokenization must handle sampling rate, duration, and speaker attributes. Joint training of speech and vision (video contains both) shares temporal-alignment supervision, but data are scarce and evaluation protocols are undetermined (C). 

The variance of embodied evaluation : VLA results in simulation and real environments differ greatly; the success rate of the same policy varies widely across initial states and noise. All Stages of Large-Model Training 78 

Hence VLA claims must be accompanied by environment settings, random seeds, and success-rate distributions; otherwise they have no comparative value (B/C). 

Long video and temporal alignment : timestamp grounding, action counting, and causal-order inference impose new requirements on inter-frame modeling; frame sampling density and spatiotemporal positional encoding determine the ceiling. Long-video training data are scarce and evaluation is not standardized, making this one of the sub-directions with the weakest evidence in multimodality today (C). 

The mirror problem of text-to-image/text-to-video : generating images/videos from text goes through the same stages (inverse token, diffusion conditioning) and faces the same data, objective, and alignment problems, but its evaluation (FID/CLIP scores) shares no unified framework with text-generation evaluation, making cross-method comparison difficult (C). This survey focuses on "understanding" and merely points out the existence of the mirror structure. 

## 10.10 Efficient Multimodality 

The efficiency of multimodality unfolds along three lines (for a survey see [405]): token compres-sion (reducing the number of image tokens, downsampling visual tokens); parameter efficiency 

(frozen encoder + light projection/adapter); compressed deployment (quantization/pruning of the visual encoder and cross-modal parts, Section 9.7). The common problem of the three lines is: visual information bandwidth and efficiency directly conflict, and the evidence lost to compression transforms into hallucination and perceptual degradation—"saving tokens is saving visual information" is a cost that must be accounted for. 

Means of visual token compression : these include downsampling (grid pooling), token merging/pruning (deleting visual tokens by importance), and cross-layer sparsity. The losses of these methods all fall on details and spatial relations, so they must be validated with fine-grained tasks (OCR, grounding [277][288]); validating with coarse-grained classification will mask the information loss. 

The interaction between compression and visual tasks : visual token compression harms OCR/grounding far more than image classification; therefore "efficient multimodality" reports must simultaneously give fine-grained perception regressions, rather than reporting only "a X-fold speedup in processing" (consistent with the compression of Chapter 9).
