Reading the table : the "representative works" of multimodality vary enormously in their disclosure of data scale and frozen scope, so reading scores horizontally across the table is almost meaningless; one should align by "paradigm × frozen scope × data definition" before comparing. 

## 10.12 Key Controversies, Recommendations, and Open Problems 

Controversy one: is the unified token the endgame . Proponents argue that "everything is a sequence" brings architectural unification and shared scale; opponents point to the VQ recon-struction loss, the asymmetry of cross-modal learning, and the lack of controllability. Currently both camps lack systematic evidence of scalability (C). 

Controversy two: is hallucination a data problem or an architectural problem .The data camp grounds its case in "cleaner image-text pairs will reduce hallucination"; the architecture camp grounds its case in "limited visual information bandwidth and text tokens dominating attention." The consensus is that both play a role, but their respective quantitative contributions are unknown (Problem 15). 

Controversy three: does multimodality need an independent scaling law . The text scaling law is mature (Section 6.3); whether multimodality obeys the same curve and how image tokens are counted toward compute still have no recognized answer; compute allocation on top of this (Chapter 13) is therefore highly uncertain. 

Practical recommendations : first fix "paradigm × frozen scope × data definition" before comparing; use interleaved image-text to strengthen context supervision; incorporate "visual evidence binding" (grounding/checking) into SFT data to suppress hallucination; conduct multi-modal evaluation under a unified protocol; when compressing multimodality, account for "visual information bandwidth" in the cost (Chapter 9). Open problems : discrete vs. continuous to-kens under a unified optimization objective (Problem 3); locating the root causes of hallucination (Problem 15); the scaling law of interleaving (a citation gap); the scalability of any-to-any. 

Multimodal-specific difficulties of evaluation protocols : the correct answer to an image-text question is often open-ended (multiple sentences can all be "correct"), and judgment is highly subjective; the display resolution of images and the presence or absence of captions are not reproducible across different deployments. Hence multimodal scores have weak cross-environment transferability, and the literature should report image resolution, prompt, and caption settings simultaneously (protocol sensitivity of Chapter 12). 

The interface with the whole survey : multimodality is not an independent pipeline—its data (Ch5), pretraining scale (Ch6), SFT (Ch7), alignment (Ch8), compression (Ch9), and eval-uation (Ch12) all follow the framework of the preceding text, except that each step additionally imposes the constraint of "how visual evidence enters." Readers should treat this chapter as an annotation that "adds a visual dimension to each chapter," rather than as a parallel thirteenth stage (Figure 1). 

Controversy: alignment vs. generalization : making a model more "bound to vision" (stronger alignment) often reduces its generalization to images from unseen distributions; more aggressive visual alignment further suppresses hallucination. The tradeoff between alignment All Stages of Large-Model Training 80 

strength and generalization likewise has no quantitative curve, and thus becomes a recurrent "invisible decision" in the choice of multimodal recipes (the framework of Chapter 4). 

Recalibration of evidence grading : the top of the pyramid in this chapter is CLIP [10], Flamingo [17], and LLaVA [28] (A/B); the middle is Qwen-VL [277] and Qwen2-VL [288] (B); the bottom is video/VLA/any-to-any (B/C). No matter how prominent a technical report is, any performance claim without released reproducible artifacts is treated as C. 

Decision checklist (multimodal recipes) : 1○ Specify "paradigm × frozen scope × mix-ture × resolution"; 2○ strengthen context supervision with interleaved and grounding data; 3○run unified hallucination/grounding evaluation before alignment; 4○ compression must come with fine-grained regressions; 5○ for any cross-paradigm comparison, first ask "is the definition consistent?" The five items are directly applicable to the reading of this entire survey. 

Significance for the general framework : the final conclusion of the multimodal chapter is not "which paradigm is best," but rather "the same decision framework spanning Ch5–Ch9 and Ch12 still applies under multimodality, except that every decision has one more variable: ’visual evidence bandwidth’." This preserves the argumentative structure of this survey—"a unified general framework, falsified by focused surveys" (Chapter 1). 

The recognition-generation loop : incremental multimodal training often forms a loop of "generate content, then train on it" (synthesize images to train understanding, and the outputs of understanding to train generation). This amplifies the risk of homologous contamination of Chapter 12—feedback from self-trained content distorts capability evaluation. The credibility of the loop has not been systematically evaluated (C). 

A caution about the pitfalls of A/B/C grading : multimodal technical reports often invoke the audience expectation of "bigger and stronger"; when reading them, remember: unless reproducible experiments and unified evaluation are provided, everything else is downgraded; "demo images" in technical reports are not evidence (Chapter 3). This makes the A/B evidence of this chapter less than that of the pure-text chapters, an honest cost. 

Advice for readers (multimodality) : reuse the "traffic-light reading" of Chapter 12, adding multimodal special cases here—green: unified evaluation + fixed resolution/caption + block-level statistics; yellow: single paradigm + protocol differences; red: demo images only + no evaluation protocol. Multimodal literature especially needs such a compressed reading tool, because its marketing density is higher than that of pure text. 

A normative statement on scale : the lack of a recognized multimodal scaling law (Section 10.5) makes specification documents (Chapter 13) rely more on assumptions than on laws. The position of this survey is: until a multimodal scaling law can be given, all claims of gains from "larger images/more parameters" should be accompanied by the tested range , to avoid treating single-point experience as a universal curve (C). 

A summary of the evidence distribution of this chapter : multimodality has a fair amount of controlled evidence on "architecture, data pipeline, and weakly supervised alignment"; but "scaling law, root causes of hallucination, and scalability of the unified token" are almost entirely C-level or blank, and the proportion of technical reports is high (Gemini [282] and others). Therefore this chapter, like Ch8, provides a reading framework that runs through the whole process , rather than a transferable recipe. 

# 11 Cross-Stage Comparison    

> This chapter is a pilot chapter, targeting 8,000–9,000 words. Its task is "horizontal": to place the stages from Ch5–Ch10 and Ch12 on the same ruler for comparison—comparing objectives, compute elasticity, marginal returns, and evidence strength. This chapter itself introduces no new literature (aside from a small amount of synthesis); all citations come from the core sets already presented in the respective chapters. All Stages of Large-Model Training 81

## 11.1 Pretraining vs Post-Training 

Conclusion first: pretraining determines "what is known," and post-training determines "how to respond"; the two stand in an additive relationship of capability and behavior, rather than being interchangeable. In comparison, several points warrant attention. First, compute accounting :pretraining normally operates at the level of trillions of tokens of compute, while post-training (SFT/alignment) accounts for a low share of compute yet exerts a disproportionate influence on behavior; therefore discussing the importance of a stage in terms of "compute share" is biased, and one should instead measure it by "marginal return per unit of compute against the target metric." Second, capability boundaries : post-training cannot manufacture knowledge absent from pretraining (unless pretraining is continued), but it can organize existing capabilities into deployable behavior ([15][175]). Third, evidence strength : the scaling laws of pretraining rest on considerable controlled extrapolation [119][16], whereas cross-model conclusions for post-training (especially the debate over RL boundaries [400][401]) rely more on single-institution reports, and their evidence level is generally low. On this basis, Figure 5 (the evidence map) marks "post-training extends reasoning boundaries" as disputed. 

Orthogonality of the "capability–behavior" decomposition : Even under ideal con-ditions, pretraining and post-training are not two ends of a single axis but two orthogonal directions. Pretraining enlarges the "set of expressible capabilities," while post-training alters the "probability mass within that set" without changing the set itself. The two can be treated separately: first determine the capability wall, then perform behavior optimization within the capability. This decomposition explains why "scaling up post-training" cannot substitute for the capability deficit caused by "missing data/missing scale," and also explains why "scaling up pretraining alone" without alignment yields a raw model that is difficult to deploy. 

Asymmetry of compute elasticity : Post-training can "leverage larger behavioral changes with less compute," which is both its value (cost saving) and its risk (P ∝ behavioral leverage). The compute elasticity of pretraining is a "smooth scaling curve" (Section 6.3), whereas that of post-training is a "steep but fragile margin," where a single change to the recipe can bring about a large-span behavioral drift. Accordingly, attributing by "capability gap vs. behavior gap" is the first step in cross-stage troubleshooting. 

Rule of thumb for budget allocation : The budget is still mainly invested in pretraining data and compute (Section 6.9); the post-training share is small but cannot be omitted; "eval-uation first" (Chapter 12) can adjudicate reallocation between the two. There is no accepted answer of the form "post-training of X% is optimal"—X itself depends on the capability wall and the deployment objective, which is precisely what this chapter trains readers to assess for themselves. 

Blurred stage boundaries : What lies between pretraining and post-training is in practice a "gradient band" rather than a white line: a recipe of continued pretraining at a low learning rate plus instruction data cannot be sharply distinguished, in terms of effect, from "a large SFT dataset + long training"; the boundary between DPO and RL (Chapter 8) is likewise. Therefore the true use of cross-stage comparison is not "dividing territory" but "attributing direction": whether a behavioral problem lies closer to the capability wall or closer to the objective surface. 

Perturbation of orthogonality by memorization : When pretraining data contains eval-uation samples (contamination, Section 12.6), "test memorization" is mixed into the "capability wall," and behavior optimization can again amplify the memorization—orthogonality fails un-der strong overfitting. Therefore, when discussing the capability–behavior decomposition, one must first rule out "pseudo-capability" caused by contamination: any orthogonality conclusion without contamination control should be downgraded according to Chapter 12. All Stages of Large-Model Training 82 

## 11.2 Text vs Multimodal 

Multimodal is more complex than text along all five dimensions—data, tokenizer, objective, align-ment, and evaluation (Section 10.2)—so conclusions about the same method in the multimodal setting cannot be directly transplanted. In particular, systematic evidence for the "scaling law" and the "alignment tax" in the multimodal setting is markedly insufficient, and any cross-modal numerical comparison must specify the modality mixture and the evaluation protocol. 

Does cross-modal transfer of the same method hold? SFT/DPO/RLVR (Chapters 7–8) are all carried over in multimodal settings and are usually effective; however, the values of their prescriptions (ratios, loss weights, evaluation sets) that have been validated on text must be re-tested in the multimodal setting. The reason is that multimodal supervision is weaker and evaluation more subjective, causing "the same optimization to perform differently on the same metric." In general, the frequency of a method (the extent to which it is adopted) is comparable, whereas the numerical values of a method (the optimal value of that recipe) are not .

The trap of "text benchmarks first" : If a multimodal model is accepted using only pure-text benchmarks, degradation on the visual side will be missed (Section 10.12); conversely, accepting only on multimodal will obscure a text-only capability regression. The correct approach for cross-modal comparison is dual-track acceptance : regress the text side and the multimodal side separately and then compare them against each other, so as to interpret "what adding vision actually costs." 

The language dimension beyond modality : Another cross-stage dimension is language and region (Chinese, multilingual, low-resource). The slope of the scaling law in the mul-tilingual setting differs markedly from the monolingual setting; the language distribution of SFT/preference data determines the shift in behavioral language; the language coverage of eval-uation benchmarks determines whether conclusions can be extrapolated (Chapter 12). There-fore any "cross-stage" conclusion should be accompanied by the caveat "for which languages it holds"—and this caveat is often missing (C). 

The synergistic leverage of multimodal and text : The text scaling law provides the learning ceiling for the "backbone," while multimodal provides differentiated capabilities for the "periphery." In cross-stage comparison, the two are not zero-sum but complementary as "shared backbone + dedicated periphery": each increment of text data lowers the backbone’s perplexity (Section 6.3), and each increment of visual data expands the periphery’s coverage (Section 10.3). Thus the budget allocation for "text vs multimodal" should not be decided by "which is more important," but by "which gap is closer to the deployment objective." 

## 11.3 LLM vs MLLM 

Most MLLMs are assembled from an LLM backbone plus visual/audio modules, and therefore share NTP and alignment methods in training; the differences are concentrated in the multi-modal tokenizer and the alignment stage (Sections 5.6 and 10). Conclusion: the training stack toward general intelligence is "one shared backbone with modality peripheries," and treating multimodality in a unified grid is preferable to setting up a separate pipeline for each modality. This judgment is currently more an engineering consensus than controlled experimental evidence. 

Cost–benefit of "backbone sharing" : Sharing the backbone makes cross-modal knowl-edge transfer possible (text knowledge assisting image understanding) and allows "failures on the visual side" to reuse mechanisms already debugged on the text side. Its cost is interference man-agement between modalities and conflicts over token budget. As long as the scale of multimodal models remains within the range of "shared backbone + peripheral adaptation," the training difference between LLM and MLLM should be regarded as "adding a peripheral segment to the same pipeline," rather than two parallel systems. 

Accounting basis for parameter counts : Cross-model comparison (LLM vs MLLM) must All Stages of Large-Model Training 83 

unify the parameter-count basis: the total parameters of an MLLM = backbone + projector + encoder, and activated parameters differ from stored parameters. If a paper’s comparison table does not list "whether the encoder is included/whether it is frozen," the numbers will be underestimated or overestimated. This survey uniformly requires the accounting basis to be stated whenever cross-model comparison is involved (Section 3.4). 

Failure modes of the modality periphery : Under backbone sharing, failures of the periphery (projector/encoder) are independent of the backbone—poor image understanding is usually the periphery’s fault, whereas losing text ability is usually damage to the backbone. Therefore MLLM troubleshooting should first determine "which segment" (Section 11.7) before touching it: fix the periphery without altering the backbone, and preserve the backbone without freezing the periphery. Most reports of "it gets dumber once you add vision" omit this level of attribution, leading to misdiagnosis. 

## 11.4 SFT vs Preference Optimization vs RL Depth 

The relationship among the three is "different depths on the same alignment stack" (Chapter 8): SFT provides the correct format for fragments, preference optimization (the DPO family) provides relative preference orderings, and RL depth (RLVR/GRPO, etc.) optimizes outcomes with verifiable rewards. Their compute and risk increase in turn: SFT is cheap and stable, DPO is intermediate, and RL depth is expensive and prone to over-optimization. 

When it is worth going deeper : This can be judged by "whether the reward can be automatically verified"—verifiable tasks (code, mathematics, execution-based tasks) merit RL depth (RLVR); soft objectives based on human preference are suited to DPO/RM; and when only format and routine compliance are needed, SFT suffices. Treating the three as a single continuum rather than mutually exclusive means avoids the ineffective cost of "RL for RL’s sake" (Chapter 13 budget). 

The reward-verifiability spectrum : Expanding "whether the reward can be automati-cally verified" into a spectrum—execution results are checkable (code/mathematics, highly veri-fiable), retrieval comparisons are checkable (citations/factuality, moderately verifiable), and pure human preference (dialogue quality, weakly verifiable). The position on the spectrum determines which alignment means to use and also determines evidence strength: RL claims at the highly verifiable end are easier to review, while preference claims at the weakly verifiable end can rely almost only on subjective consensus (Chapters 8 and 12). 

The failure spectrum of the three alignment depths : SFT fails at "out-of-distribution generalization" (the format is learned but the content collapses), preference optimization fails at "multi-objective conflict" (correct but unsafe), and RL depth fails at "reward hacking" (scores rise but behavior deteriorates). Mapping "which layer of failure the current problem belongs to" to "which layer to apply" saves more budget than blindly climbing in depth (Chapter 8). 

Nonlinearity of the input–output of alignment depth : From SFT to preference to RL depth, each step of increase does not yield linear returns but alternates between "plateau and jump"; beyond a certain depth, the marginal returns drop sharply while the risk of over-optimization surges. This is precisely the cross-stage version of the "alignment tax" (Section 8.8): it is not that "deeper is necessarily better," but that "the depth must be commensurate with reward verifiability and budget." 

Evidence gradient : SFT is already an industry consensus (dominated by C-level reports but widely replicated, effectively a B-level consensus); preference optimization has relatively systematic controlled studies (the DPO family [215][235]); the effectiveness and boundaries of RL depth still depend heavily on the model and task, and its cross-model conclusions are the weakest. On this basis, this chapter labels the three as B, A/B, and C respectively in the representative table. All Stages of Large-Model Training 84 

## 11.5 Data vs Compute vs Objective 

Differences in the nature of the three elements: data determines the distribution of the capability ceiling, compute determines the speed and scale at which training can proceed, and the objective determines which capability curve is selected. The three are not equally weighted: at large volumes, data and compute are hard constraints, while the objective is a soft constraint—the meaning of this statement is that when the budget is tight, trimming the objective is safer than trimming data, because the objective only changes probability mass (Section 11.1), whereas data and compute change the reachable capability set (Chapter 6). 

The mirror image of mixture is cost elasticity : With a fixed budget, data governs "knowledge," compute governs "scale," and the objective governs "behavior." Their marginal returns are asymmetric and vary with the stage: early on, compute is scarcer than the objective, while later the objective is scarcer than data. Therefore the specification in Chapter 13 solves the three as a system of simultaneous equations rather than allocating funds sequentially. 

The "exchange-rate table" for compute–data–objective : Writing the three elements as an exchange-rate table: 1 unit of objective complexity ≈ some amount of data/compute. This kind of exchange comes from experience rather than law (Section 11.6), yet it is repeatedly used in budget decisions. This survey’s reminder: before there is controlled head-to-head comparison, the exchange rate should be treated as "that team’s prior" and need not be taken as a "universal constant"; only cross-team head-to-head comparison (Chapter 13) can truly calibrate it. 

Cross-stage implications of CLM and compute budget : The Chinchilla-style optimum ([119]) answers only "how to spend in the pretraining stage"; transplanting it directly to the whole pipeline (including alignment) is an erroneous extrapolation. This chapter reminds us: CLM’s conclusions hold only within the framework of "pretraining + downstream evaluation"; once alignment and compression are added, the optimal compute allocation is rewritten—currently no head-to-head comparison provides an "optimal budget curve that includes post-training" (Sections 6.3 and 11.6). 

## 11.6 Comparison of "Marginal Returns" Across Stages 

Defining "marginal return": investing the same budget increment successively into data, com-pute, and objective, and the metric improvement each brings, is precisely the answer to "whom to invest in." The marginal curves of the three stages have different shapes: the marginal decline of data is slow (more data does no harm), the marginal decline of compute is determined by the scaling law, and the marginal decline of the objective is fast but starts from a high point. On this basis, performing "reallocation" at the critical points of each stage is often superior to monotonically increasing quantities. 

Scarcity of controlled data : Although this comparative framework is logically self-consistent, there is almost no controlled experiment that can produce a complete three-element exchange curve (which would require multiple equal-budget head-to-head comparisons). There-fore Section 11.6 is a "framework" rather than a "conclusion": it teaches readers how to ask questions rather than providing answers. Recent advances expand this marginal-return calculus from the traditional binary "pretraining parameters vs post-training alignment" into a three-way Pareto frontier incorporating test-time search compute—works such as Snell et al. [409] and MiMo-V2.6 [412] provide empirical anchors for test-time scaling and large-batch asynchronous RL, transforming compute trade-offs from static assumptions into multi-dimensional optimiza-tion surfaces. This is the chapter’s candor at the level of evidence. 

How to minimize the variance of head-to-head comparisons : The head-to-head exper-iments capable of producing a three-element exchange curve are scarce, partly because variance is difficult to control: minor differences in data mixture, random seed, training steps, and so on render two "equal-budget" experiments incomparable. The operative way to minimize it: fix the token budget and data seed, share the evaluation stack, and change only one variable (Chapter All Stages of Large-Model Training 85 

12). Only once at least this is done do cross-stage conclusions begin to be attributable. 

Three "seemingly equivalent" misconceptions : First, "a larger model = more expensive = smarter," when in fact it is jointly determined by the data mixture (Section 6.3); second, "more preference data = more alignment," when in fact the diversity of preference data is more critical than its volume (Chapter 8); third, "a stronger evaluation = more accurate conclusions," when in fact the action an evaluation takes on a model depends on the protocol (Chapter 12). Once all three are debunked one by one, it becomes clear that the foundation of cross-stage comparison remains "define the accounting basis first, then compare the numbers." 

The "evaluation anchors" of marginal returns : Reallocation decisions require a set of evaluation anchors that run through the whole process (metrics comparable under the same protocol); otherwise "which segment is more effective" cannot be judged. The anchors should cover three categories—capability, behavior, and safety (the matrix in Chapter 12)—and be sampled throughout the entire training process; otherwise the marginal curve can only be pieced together after the fact. 

## 11.7 Cross-Stage Failure Modes 

The failure-mode lists from the respective chapters (Ch5–Ch9) are merged here into four cate-gories of cross-stage symptoms: 1○ the capability wall recedes (insufficient data/scale) but is masked by attractive behavior; 2○ behavior is non-compliant though capability is suf-ficient (insufficient alignment); 3○ both capability and behavior are satisfactory but acceptance is distorted (evaluation contamination); 4○ continual optimization causes ca-pability drift (over-compression/forgetting). Whenever one encounters "strange behavior" in a model, first attribute it to these four categories, then decide which segment to touch. 

Troubleshooting order : Because the capability wall and the behavior gap are entangled (the orthogonality in Section 11.1 is only approximate), a model that "answers too well but cannot be used" may stem either from evaluation contamination or from over-alignment. The default order for cross-stage troubleshooting is: verify the evaluation first, then examine the capability wall, and finally look at alignment —because cheating on evaluation items is the cheapest to commit and the easiest to rule out first. 

Self-check for the four symptoms : A symptom self-check list is provided— 1○ capability wall recedes: still not meeting the bar after adding data/scale across multiple stages? 2○ behavior non-compliant: capability sufficient but the deployment objective unsatisfied? 3○ acceptance distorted: scores out of step with real experience? 4○ capability drift: conspicuous side effects after compression/fine-tuning? These four questions can be run through in three minutes and can localize the vast majority of "strange model behaviors" to the correct stage. 

## 11.8 Continual Optimization vs Retraining 

When the capability wall has fallen behind the task, continuing to fine-tune/compress (Chapter 9) is often merely "dithering"; retraining/continued pretraining raises the capability wall. The criterion for choosing: if the failure comes from excessive behavior, take the incremental path; if the failure comes from a lack of knowledge/capability, take retraining. Each direction has a classic pitfall: the incremental direction is "mistaking a behavior problem for a capability problem" and wasting resources; the retraining direction is "mistaking a capability problem for a behavior problem" and wasting deployment time. 

Cost divergence between incremental and retraining : The incremental approach has a low unit cost but requires that "the capability wall is sufficient"; retraining has a high unit cost but can push the wall higher. In the reality of a fixed compute budget, most teams try the incremental approach first (cheaper and faster) and are forced into retraining only after failure—giving rise to the phenomenon that "retraining always appears at the last moment." This All Stages of Large-Model Training 86 

survey’s position: whether retraining is worthwhile should be planned in advance by assessing the capability wall at stage 0 (Chapter 13), rather than as after-the-fact firefighting. 

Operational trigger conditions for retraining : An operational trigger is given for whether to retrain: within the observation window, SFT/preference improvement tends toward saturation and key benchmarks stagnate over the long term; and the server-side error patterns are inconsistent with the capability-wall hypothesis (frequently exposing knowledge blind spots rather than behavior). Only when both are present is retraining triggered; otherwise, fix the behavior surface first. 

Interaction among continual optimization techniques : When compression, distillation, quantization, editing, and the like (Chapter 9) are used in combination, the effects are nonlinear— a combination whose individual techniques have positive returns may yield negative returns when stacked (e.g., quantizing first and then editing doubles the knowledge damage). Therefore "combination amplification" must be paired with combination-level regression, and cross-stage comparison should read combined results as less reliable than single-technique results (B/C). 

## 11.9 Representative Comparison Table                           

> Table 9: Chapter 11 key cross-stage comparison
> Dimension Pretraining Post-Training: SFT Preference Optimization RL Depth Continual Optimization Evaluation Compute share High Low Medium-low Medium Medium Low Behavioral leverage Medium High Medium-high High (when verifiable) Medium High ( adjudication) Capability-wall impact Determines None (does not change the set) None None Has side effects None Primary evidence B/A B/C A/B CB/C BKey risk Data contamination Forgetting Preference overfitting Over-optimization Capability drift Contamination/ bias Typical cost entry token compute Data construction Preference annotation Compute + reward GPU time Protocol + annotation

How to read the table : The row with high behavioral leverage but low evidence (RL depth) is where risk is most concentrated—the more effective a stage, the more it requires the evaluation discipline of Chapter 12 as a backstop. Likewise, "low compute share + high leverage" (SFT/evaluation) are the two knobs with the highest cost-effectiveness for improvement when the budget is tight. 

Unifying the compute accounting basis : If the "compute share" in the table is calculated by wall-clock time, post-training appears infinitesimal; if apportioned by FLOPs, it is closer to reality. Whichever basis is taken, the conclusion that "the share is low but the leverage is high" remains unchanged, but the numerical basis must be stated clearly ; otherwise declarations such as "we spend only 3% of compute on alignment" cannot be verified (compute accounting in Section 6.9). 

Confusion between "share" and "attribution" : The compute share of pretraining is high, but "this portion of compute simultaneously supports the evaluation hold-out and pre-training validation"; the post-training share is low, but repeated grid searches multiply its actual occupation severalfold. For the true attribution of cross-stage budget, "direct occupation" should be replaced by "total cost = occupation × number of attempts"—this is especially important for the alignment stage, because hyperparameter sweeps for alignment are usually denser than those for pretraining. 

Statistical basis for cross-stage comparison : Numerical comparisons across mod-els/stages must also specify: decoding parameters, few-shot, seeds, and evaluation bounds. These are already difficult to align across text models and even harder in multimodal (Section 10.12). All Stages of Large-Model Training 87 

For any numerical claim of "stage A > stage B," this survey requires by default a statistical-basis checklist; otherwise the claim is treated as marketing. 

## 11.10 Cross-Stage Joint Decision-Making 

The whole pipeline is not a matter of adding together the best recipe from each chapter at once, but of a three-layer determination under the "capability–behavior" decomposition: first determine the capability wall (pretraining data and scale), then determine the behavior surface (SFT/preference/RL), and finally determine the lifecycle (continual optimization and evalua-tion). Each of the three determinations is given its own chapter, but they share a single objec-tive function—the specification given in Chapter 13. This chapter thereby organizes the "local optima" of Ch5–Ch10 and Ch12 into the necessary path to the "global optimum," and explains why this general framework places "comparison" at the position of converging the chapters. 

The "product effect" of whole-pipeline combination : Multiplying the "local optima" of each chapter does not automatically constitute a "global optimum"—the more common failure is that every step "looks right" yet they stack into an undeployable model (a reasonable data mixture but poor generalization, good alignment but hollow knowledge). Therefore cross-stage joint decision-making is not recipe addition but the constraint satisfaction of Figure 2 : place the optimal solution of each chapter into the same objective function, and the solution of each later step must respect the degrees of freedom left by the previous step. This discipline is more expensive and more valuable than any single-chapter technique (Chapter 13). 

The "order effect" of recipes : Most recipes assume a fixed stage order (pretraining →SFT →preference →deployment). But the evidence suggests that the order itself is a tunable hyperparameter: preference before SFT, and interleaving stages, have each been attempted at small scale (C). This survey does not endorse any non-default order, but it points out explicitly that the reason the "default order" is default is more a matter of inertia than of proven optimality. 

Data/format contracts at the interfaces between stages : The interface contracts of each stage (data, format, evaluation) have already been mentioned in Section 7.10; at the cross-stage level they must be aligned even more: the checkpoint and tokenizer produced by pre-training, the dialogue format produced by SFT, the consistent annotation of preference data, and the contamination hold-out of evaluation must all interoperate under a single schema. Interface distortion is more fatal than destruction within a stage, because it renders failure attribution ineffective. 

Cold start vs transfer : Pretraining from scratch (cold start, costly and controllable) and continuing from open-source/closed-source weights (transfer, cheaper but constrained by up-stream) are two nearly parallel starting lines. The core variable of comparison is "control": cold start trades for customized data and architectural freedom, while transfer trades for cost and time. Cross-stage joint decision-making should first choose between these two starting lines, then apply the recipes of the respective chapters (Chapter 13). 

## 11.11 Data Quality vs Data Scale 

A common variable across stages: data quality and data scale are in fact the same lever running through Ch5–Ch10 and Ch12, differing only in the sensitivity of each stage to the two. Pretrain-ing is most sensitive to scale (the horizontal axis of the scaling law [119][16] is entirely token count), the SFT/preference stages are more sensitive to quality (the diversity of instructions and the consistency of preferences), and the continual optimization stage is sensitive to both "quality and scale" but fears distribution shift more. This difference yields a practical configuration rule: the closer to pretraining, the more one should "stack scale"; the closer to alignment and deploy-ment, the more one should "squeeze quality." But the evidence for this rule is highly uneven: All Stages of Large-Model Training 88 

the laws on the scale side rest on controlled extrapolation, whereas quantitative studies on the quality side are mostly single-point reports (B/C). 

An operational definition of quality : Quality here is not an aesthetic concept but "the contribution of that sample to the probability mass of the target condition." The same data may be noise in pretraining and gold in SFT—because the objective differs. Therefore "data quality" must be discussed bound to a stage; a "quality leaderboard" detached from the stage may circulate in the market but has no scientific validity (isomorphic to the contextualization of evaluation in Chapter 12). 

## 11.12 Perception vs Cognition vs Reasoning 

Another axis of cross-stage comparison is the category of capability: perception (understanding of images/speech, Ch10), cognition (knowledge retrieval and integration, Ch5–Ch6), and reasoning (chained steps and logic, Ch7–Ch8). The "acquisition stage" differs for the three categories: per-ception and cognition are mainly in pretraining (data-driven), whereas the explicit enhancement of reasoning lies in the preference/RL depth stages (Section 8.6). Therefore "which category of capability one wants to improve" should directly determine "which segment to touch." 

Granularity of the classification : The three segments of perception/cognition/reasoning are not a strict trichotomy, and borderline examples (e.g., explicit reasoning is also cognition) abound. But as a formalizing tool, it is more operational than the "capability wall": in a budget-decision table, first classifying the target capability and then mapping it to a stage can reduce the ineffective investment caused by confusing the two (the troubleshooting order of Section 11.7 applies again). 

Current evidence for reasoning enhancement : The evidence for explicit reasoning (chain-of-thought, RLVR, etc.) is highly uneven across capability categories (Section 8.9): ver-ifiable tasks (mathematics/code) have A/B-level support, whereas open reasoning tasks (plan-ning/commonsense) have only C-level single-point reports. Therefore the assertion that "rea-soning should be enhanced in the reasoning stage" holds only for verifiable tasks; extending this assertion to all tasks is the most common overinterpretation in cross-stage comparison. 

## 11.13 Offline vs Online 

The relationship between training and deployment has two forms: offline (deploy after training, without updating with use) and online/closed-loop (collect feedback during deployment and train back). Offline is simple, reproducible, but static; online is close to demand and can self-heal, but introduces data-flywheel bias and the risk of self-referential contamination (Section 12.6). In cross-stage comparison, the key is not "which form is more advanced," but "whether the stage is suitable for a closed loop": perception/cognition categories are better suited to offline, and behavior/reward categories to online (because behavioral feedback comes precisely from use). The current mainstream remains predominantly offline with online as a supplement, and the evidence for institutionalizing online training is still weak (C). 

The two points of loss of control in a closed loop : First, feedback data reflects only the "used" scenarios (survivorship bias); second, the model’s own outputs enter the next round of training (self-consumption/self-referential contamination, Section 12.6). Therefore any online/closed-loop design must be paired with "controls" and "hold-outs"—sharing a single dis-cipline with the contamination detection of Chapter 12. 

Window period and deployment audit : The online stage also introduces the concept of a "window period"—the interval from deployment to retraining determines the model’s repair speed and also the risk window; if the interval is too short, noise is introduced, and if too long, behavior becomes stale. The metrics for auditing the window period (repair latency, regression rate) lie in a blank area (Section 11.17), yet they directly determine the reliability of ’post-deployment training’. All Stages of Large-Model Training 89 

## 11.14 General vs Specialized 

The comparison between general models (one model for many uses) and specialized models (domain/task specialization) runs through every stage: general models have the advantage in pretraining scale-up (the scaling law), while specialized models have the advantage in the "target-edness" of SFT/preference/evaluation. The difficulty is that this comparison cannot be settled statically—it is normal for the same model to perform well in the general stage yet be surpassed by a specialized model on a specialized task. The effective configuration is "a general base + a specialized periphery" (the same logic as LLM vs MLLM in Section 10.3): use the general base to guarantee the lower bound of knowledge and specialized data/adapter layers to guar-antee the upper bound of the task. On the evidence, "specialized exceeds general" is always task-dependent, and generalizing this conclusion across tasks is meaningless (B/C). 

The most common misconception about specialization : Assuming that a general model plus domain data automatically makes one "more specialized," but if the evaluation set shares its source with the domain data, the improvement in scores may be mere recall of memo-rized content (contamination, Section 12.6). The evidence that "specialized exceeds general" is credible only after contamination control on the domain evaluation—this is the discipline of the specialized/general discussion in this chapter. 

## 11.15 Reproducibility vs Non-Reproducibility 

"Whether it can be reproduced" is one of the most basic measures across stages. The difficulty of reproduction differs across stages: the scale and data of pretraining are almost irreproducible (unique to a single institution), SFT/DPO datasets are partially reproducible, and evaluation protocols can be fully public. This gradient means that the larger the stage, the more one must rely on "the consensus of multiple independent implementations" rather than "a single implementation" to establish belief; conversely, evaluation protocols are a low-cost, high-return lever for reproducibility (Chapter 12). 

"Non-reproducible does not mean non-verifiable" : Conclusions without public repro-ducible artifacts (large-model reports, benchmark leaders) can still be weakly verified by "con-sistency and falsifiability": whether conclusions from multiple sources agree (the RL debate in Chapter 8) and whether future experiments can falsify them (with testable hypotheses attached). This survey incorporates this layer of weak verification into its evidence grading (Appendix E), which is its response to "the large number of C-level technical reports throughout the text that can still be interpreted." 

## 11.16 Open Source vs Closed Source 

Whether the training stack is open source affects verifiability: open-source weights/data/evaluation can be independently reviewed (green, Section 12.11), whereas closed-source can only rely on the vendor’s written declarations (red). In cross-stage compar-ison, the benefit of open source lies in "reproducibility and auditability," and the benefit of closed source in "data confidentiality and commercial advantage." This survey reads the two even-handedly through grading—conclusions with a high open-source component naturally absorb a higher proportion of A/B (e.g., the open-source turning point led by LLaMA [9]), while those with a high closed-source component fall back to C more often. 

Licensing and the auditability surface : In open source, "open weights" and "open data" are two different things—open weights allow inference to be reproduced, but only open data allows training to be reproduced. The data accompanying most open weights remains closed, leaving "reproduction" at the inference layer. Cross-stage comparison therefore often exhibits a semi-open state of "inference-reproducible, training-irreproducible," and in interpretation one must distinguish which layer is involved (Chapter 3). All Stages of Large-Model Training 90 

## 11.17 Evidence Map and Open Problems 

Evidence-map summary (echoing Figure 5 and Section 11.4): data and tokenization, pretraining optimization, PEFT, and the DPO family = predominantly A/B; largest-scale pretraining, RL depth boundaries, and compression–safety interaction = predominantly C (technical reports). 

A+B accounts for about 79% (full corpus, Appendix E). The confidence of a recommended conclusion should match the evidence of the cell it occupies: cross-stage head-to-head comparison (11.6) is a framework, and the cross-model RL boundary consists of opposing reports; both should be interpreted with downweighting according to their respective levels.

Open problems (merged from the chapters): the most critical cross-stage question marks— Problem 3 (unified token objective), 6 (contamination), 13 (reward engineering), and 15 (root cause of hallucination)—as well as the scaling-law/compression/evaluation blanks in Sections 6.3, 9, and 12 respectively. If the reader takes away only one framework, it is the three layers of "capability–behavior–evidence": verify the capability wall first, then the behavior surface, and finally the evidence strength. 

The white zones of the evidence map : Beyond the known C-level areas, there are completely blank areas (with no citations): multimodal scaling laws, cross-modal communication redundancy, whole-pipeline head-to-head comparison of industrial-grade cold starts, and the regression loss of online closed loops. White zones are not an excuse for "having no conclusion," but an honest annotation of "having no evidence to cite," and readers should treat them as research opportunities rather than gaps in the literature. All Stages of Large-Model Training 91 

Common-method effect : Cross-stage conclusions are often entangled because they "share the same evaluation stack/same data"—the goodness of Chapter A depends on the data of Chapter B, which is exactly the common-method effect from causal inference transplanted into LLM training. Cross-stage interpretation should always keep one subtext in mind: "any single chapter’s attribution may in fact be the credit of its upstream," which is one reason this survey places Ch11 near the end of the text. 

Action pages for three types of readers : Researchers—assemble "the Section 11.6 head-to-head comparison + the Chapter 12 anchors" into a minimal experiment; engineers— incorporate "the four questions of Section 11.7 + the trigger conditions of Section 11.8" into the ticket flow; managers—use "the white zones of Section 11.17" to plan next year’s budget. All three action pages require reading Figure 2 and Chapter 13 first, to put "implementation details" back into "global constraints." 

Suggestions for the next-generation survey : The mechanistic conclusions of the whole text are worth preserving, while the numerical conclusions will expire; the three-way coordinates of this survey—"scaling curves / contamination techniques / evaluation protocols"—should be preserved as updatable data (Appendix F). The next edition need only replace the expired C-level technical reports and updated benchmarks; the structure need not be torn down. 

The positioning of this chapter within the general framework : Cross-stage compari-son is not "yet another list of techniques," but a way to place the local conclusions of Ch5–Ch10 and Ch12 into the same coordinate system, thereby answering "whom the budget should go to and how much the evidence should be trusted." Taking up the specification of Ch13 and looking back at the constraint satisfaction of Figure 2, it is the converging hub of the text’s structure of "unified general framework, falsifiable focused surveys" (Chapter 1). 

# 12 Datasets and Evaluation 

> This chapter, like Ch5–Ch9, serves as a pilot for the dual form of "general framework + fo-cused survey." Note on evidence: the mechanisms of evaluation methods and the issues of con-tamination/reproducibility have a considerable body of Grade A/B literature ([52][90][374], etc.), but "how much real capability a given benchmark score represents" is always a mat-ter of interpretation; hence this chapter emphasizes protocols rather than rankings. Target length: 8,000–9,000 characters.

## 12.1 Definitions and Scope 

Conclusion first: this chapter discusses two intertwined threads — "what data to use for training" and "how to judge whether a model is good." Data determine the space of possibilities for capability, whereas evaluation determines whether we can see that space; together the two constitute the "evidence infrastructure" of the remaining chapters of this survey. In scope :the lineage of training datasets, data governance and documentation, evaluation benchmarks and metrics, contamination and leakage, fairness and safety evaluation, and reproducibility and reporting standards. Out of scope : leaderboards and leaderboard-gaming techniques for specific benchmarks, and the private evaluations of particular vendors. 

Evaluation is more than "scoring" : Across the entire training pipeline, evaluation serves six functions — 1○ diagnosis (identifying weaknesses); 2○ selection (choosing among mod-els/data/hyperparameters); 3○ monitoring (training health, reward hacking, degradation); 4○reporting (publicly declaring capability boundaries); 5○ governance (safety and compliance de-cisions); and 6○ research design (testing hypotheses). Equating evaluation with "running a few leaderboards" simultaneously misses 2○ 3○ 5○ — yet these are precisely what determine whether training is reliable. 

Evaluation-driven training : The experience of mature teams is "do not start training without evaluation" — first define the capabilities to be improved and the acceptance thresholds, All Stages of Large-Model Training 92 

and only then design the data and training. If evaluation is added after the fact, one often finds that what improved was an "unmeasurable dimension," wasting compute. Placing evaluation first is therefore this chapter’s most direct engineering recommendation for the survey as a whole. 

Offline vs. online evaluation : Offline evaluation scores on a fixed dataset; it is fast but may become disconnected from real-world use. Online evaluation (A/B testing, user feedback) stays close to deployment but is costly, noisy, and subject to ethical and privacy constraints. The two should divide labor: offline for rapid iteration and regression, online for final calibration. Using only one of them leads to systematic misjudgment. 

Evaluation also costs : Evaluation itself consumes compute and human effort — a complete evaluation of a large model across many benchmarks can amount to a small training run. It is therefore necessary to balance "evaluation resolution" against "cost": use a small proxy set for daily iteration, a complete matrix for milestones, and safety/bias regression before release. Blindly running full evaluations is both expensive and slows iteration. 

The evaluation–training loop : In RL, if the evaluation set is used for reward or early stopping, it will gradually "leak" into becoming a training objective (reward hacking; Chapter 8). The validation set used for training and the test set used for final reporting must therefore be kept separate, and the test set should ideally be "frozen once and not reused for a long time" (or refreshed periodically). 

Why data and evaluation must share a chapter : Every choice about data (source, mixture, deduplication, synthesis) is measured against some evaluation, while every evaluation result is affected by data contamination. If the two were discussed separately, one would repeat the loop of "we do not know how much contamination there is, so we do not know what the score means." Juxtaposing the two in this chapter is precisely intended to break this loop. 

## 12.2 Lineage of Training Datasets 

Training data can be grouped into several categories: general web/books/code (The Pile [54]); cleaned web corpora (the C4 family [49][56], with a documented audit in [56]); image–text and interleaved data (LAION-5B [64], OBELISC [273]); and open pretraining corpora (Dolma [84]). Their common trend is "from ’a set of URLs’ to ’auditable datasets with licenses and statistics,’" but they differ enormously in openness, quality, and language coverage. 

Quality filtering is a core variable of datasets : Web corpora with the same name can produce entirely different data distributions depending on the filtering rules — which explains why "we used C4" is insufficient to reproduce results. The choice and thresholds of filters (language identification, duplication, toxicity, templates) should be reported as part of the model [49][56]. 

Data age and domain : The collection time and domain composition of data have a measur-able effect on downstream capabilities [71], which makes the "data cutoff" not only a deployment constraint (Chapters 9 and 13) but also a training-design variable. Imbalanced multilingual cov-erage is a widespread problem: the very existence of Chinese benchmarks (CMMLU [368], C-Eval [365]) is itself a correction to the "English-centric" reality of data. 

Code and structured data : Code, mathematical derivations, and structured markup (La-TeX, SQL) account for only a small proportion of pretraining and post-training yet substantially support reasoning and tool use. They are usually high-quality and easy to deduplicate, but their licensing is complex and they tend to contain benchmark answers (higher contamination risk; Section 12.6). 

Synthetic data enters pretraining : Using models to generate "textbook-style" text to augment pretraining or mid-training data has become a trend, but its quality, bias, and the ceiling of "teaching a model with a model" remain contested (sharing the same origin as synthetic-data collapse in Chapters 5 and 7). Synthetic data means that "data scale" is no longer limited to real corpora alone, while also making contamination harder to trace. All Stages of Large-Model Training 93 

The joint distribution of data scale and quality : Real-world data is not uniform on the "scale–quality" plane — high-quality data is scarce, while massive data is noisy. The essence of dataset design is to choose points on this plane and use filtering/synthesis to push those points toward "high-quality, large-scale." This perspective unifies the three threads of selection, filtering, and synthesis in Chapter 5. 

Evaluating data mixtures : The effect of mixture adjustments must be adjudicated by evaluation, but "mixture sweeps" are costly. A pragmatic approach is to first determine the ranking of mixtures on a small proxy model and then transfer it to the large model — this relies on the assumption that "mixture rankings are stable between small and large models," an assumption that itself needs to be tested (B/C). 

Debiasing data : If the training corpus systematically favors certain groups/viewpoints, the model will inherit and amplify them. Mitigation approaches (reweighting, balanced sampling, adversarial debiasing) each have costs, and "debiasing" may harm performance on the original task. Bias mitigation should therefore come with evaluation and a description of tradeoffs, rather than defaulting to "the more, the better." 

Data flywheels and timeliness : User interactions collected after deployment can flow back as training data, forming a "data flywheel." But returned data carries selection bias (only the scenarios that were used), privacy risk, and feedback loops (the model favoring its own outputs). A flywheel therefore needs to be paired with debiasing, privacy removal, and "control" evaluations; otherwise it will self-reinforce. 

Reproducible vs. non-reproducible data : Open-source data is reproducible but of-ten lags behind and carries license restrictions; crawled data is close to the frontier but non-reproducible and carries high compliance risk. A research survey therefore faces the structural difficulty of "non-reproducibility at the data layer" — which is also the fundamental reason this survey takes methodological mechanisms rather than precise scores as its main thread. 

Statistics a dataset card should report : scale (tokens/samples), language and domain distribution, deduplication ratio, filter hit rate, time range, license, and provenance. For datasets lacking these statistics, their "quality" can only rest on hearsay; datasets with such statistics can be partially reproduced and audited [61]. 

The reporting basis for data scale : "How many tokens" is not directly comparable across different tokenizers; reporting should give the number of samples, the number of tokens, and the tokenizer used simultaneously (Chapter 5). In addition, the scale difference between "before/after deduplication" can be severalfold and must be clearly labeled. A scale claim with an unclear basis cannot be used for any quantitative comparison. 

Licenses and provenance : The usability of a dataset depends not only on technical quality but also on license and provenance compliance. The audit of dataset provenance [78] and the observation of "the erosion of the data commons" [96] show that the available open corpora are themselves changing — which makes "reproducing someone else’s pretraining" increasingly difficult at the data level (Chapter 13). 

## 12.3 Data Governance and Documentation Standards 

Dataset documentation (datasheets [61]) and model cards are becoming reporting stan-dards: they should state the motivation, composition, collection method, preprocessing, usage, distribution, and maintenance. Their value lies not in the form but in turning "what this dataset can/cannot be used for" into an auditable declaration. 

Levels of documentation : Datasheets [61] are oriented toward datasets and model cards toward models; the two are complementary but neither can replace the "traceability of provenance and license" [78]. The ideal combination is: the data side provides lineage, the model side provides usage and limitations, and the evaluation side provides protocols; only by piecing the three together can one answer "how much can this score be trusted." All Stages of Large-Model Training 94 

Provenance, consent, and license : Lawful and ethical data use requires traceable prove-nance, clear licensing, and, where necessary, records of consent [78][96]. Specific deletion requests for training corpora (Section 9.8) require the source data to be locatable — which means that "data lineage" is not an add-on of compliance but a technical prerequisite. 

Privacy : Web corpora often contain personally identifiable information. Even without train-ing directly on privacy tasks, a model may memorize and leak training data. Data governance must therefore include records of deduplication/privacy-removal processes [13][68], and must acknowledge that "complete privacy removal" is unattainable and that risk can only be reduced. 

The spectrum of licenses : From fully open to research-only to non-commercial, licenses directly determine whether a model’s derivative weights can be released. Teams that ignore licensing often discover only before release that they cannot legally open-source, so license review should proceed in sync with data collection rather than as after-the-fact remediation [78]. 

The operationalizability of data governance : Ideal governance means that "every train-ing sample can answer where it came from and whether it can be used." In practice, this requires labeling at collection time rather than filling it in afterward. For researchers, it should at mini-mum disclose the data-source categories and filtering pipeline; for publishers, it should provide data lineage and a deletion channel [61][78]. 

Evaluation cards and model cards : Model cards record usage and limitations, while evaluation cards record evaluation protocols and results. Only together can they answer "under what conditions, by whom, and with what method this model was measured to do what." A model card lacking an evaluation card leaves its performance claims impossible to independently verify. 

## 12.4 Layering of Evaluation Benchmarks 

Evaluation should be layered by capability, avoiding the use of a single score to summarize a model: • Language understanding : GLUE [344], SuperGLUE [346], HellaSwag [347], ARC [342]. • Knowledge and reasoning : MMLU [7], MMLU-Pro [381], BIG-Bench Hard [363], GPQA [378]. • Mathematics : GSM8K [361], MATH [356]. • Code : HumanEval [357], SWE-bench [372], LiveCodeBench (contamination-resistant) [379]. • Dialogue and open-ended generation : MT-Bench / Chatbot Arena [36], Arena-Hard [232]. • Multimodal : MMMU [281] and VQA/captioning benchmarks. • Truthfulness/hallucination : TruthfulQA [359], HaluEval [366], FActScore [367]. • Safety : HarmBench [225], WMDP [328], BBQ bias [360]. • Holistic frameworks : the multi-scenario matrix of HELM [22]. 

The significance of layering : A model leading on knowledge benchmarks is not necessarily leading on code, long context, or safety; blending different layers into a single "composite score" obscures the true capability profile. Whenever a survey cites any claim that "some model is stronger," it should specify which layer and which protocol .

Chinese and multilingual evaluation : CMMLU [368], C-Eval [365], and others fill the gap in non-English evaluation, but most remain knowledge-oriented and carry a high risk of sharing a source with training corpora. Multilingual evaluation must also account for tokenizer differences in token efficiency across languages (Chapter 5); otherwise "the same length" does not mean "the same amount of information." 

Long-context evaluation : It distinguishes "able to read long" from "able to use long" (Section 9.3), commonly using three task types: "needle-in-a-haystack," multi-hop, and summa-rization. Long-context benchmarks are costly to construct and easily contaminated (long texts can be generated by models), so their scores should be treated with stronger reservations. All Stages of Large-Model Training 95 

Saturation and renewal of capability benchmarks : Older benchmarks (such as some GLUE tasks) are approaching saturation, with declining discriminative power, prompting the emergence of high-difficulty benchmarks such as MMLU-Pro [381], GPQA [378], and BBH [363]. But greater difficulty also brings annotation and judging problems where "even experts struggle to answer." The life cycle of a benchmark is shorter than that of a model, so evaluation must be continuously updated. 

Pitfalls of reasoning evaluation : Math/code benchmarks are easily affected by "rephras-ing the problem statement" and "answer formatting," and Pass@k depends on the sampling budget. When reporting reasoning capability, the number of samples, temperature, and answer-equivalence judging rules should be given at the same time; otherwise different works are not comparable. 

Instruction-following and format evaluation : Beyond content correctness, one must also evaluate "whether constraints are followed" (length, format, language, refusal conditions). Instruction following is often scored automatically using "verifiable constraints," but real user constraints are open-ended combinations that are hard to enumerate — this is a structural gap in alignment evaluation (Chapter 8). 

Agent/tool evaluation : When a model calls tools and executes over multiple steps, the object of evaluation shifts from "a single response" to a "trajectory." Evaluation must cover tool selection, parameter correctness, error recovery, and final task success, and the environment must be reproducible (to avoid real side effects). This type of evaluation is far less mature than static question answering (B/C; Chapter 8). 

Multimodal evaluation protocols : Image–text evaluation is highly sensitive to the prompt (question wording), image resolution, and caption quality, and hallucination judgments depend on annotation. The same MMMU [281] score can differ markedly across protocols. Multimodal conclusions should therefore be read protocol first, score second. 

The ceiling of language-understanding benchmarks : Discriminative benchmarks such as GLUE [344]/SuperGLUE [346] are already highly saturated, and their "understanding" has a weakening association with the actual capabilities of generative models. In a new survey, they are therefore used more for the regression of "capabilities not having degraded" than as proof of "leading." 

Joint evaluation of safety and capability : Capability and safety are often reported separately, but what users see is the combined behavior. Whether "capability gains come at the cost of safety" can only be judged through a joint matrix (capability × attack surface). Looking at only the capability board or the safety board misses this tradeoff (alignment tax; Chapter 8). 

Representativeness and cultural bias of benchmarks : Most benchmarks are centered on English and Western knowledge, and applying them directly to other languages/cultures misjudges capability. Constructing localized benchmarks ([365][368]) is not merely translation but requires redesigning the questions and judging criteria. Cross-cultural comparison should be cautious, avoiding misreading "unfamiliarity with the context" as "insufficient capability." 

What is distinctive about code and formal evaluation : Code/formal tasks can be judged automatically by "execution results," giving strong objectivity; but this also brings prob-lems of "test-case leakage" and "overfitting to tests." Contamination-resistant design (hidden tests, dynamic generation [379]) is therefore especially critical for code evaluation. 

## 12.5 Metrics and Protocols 

Pass@k and sampling budget : Reasoning tasks distinguish Pass@1 (single-attempt pass rate) from Pass@k (at least one pass in k samples). A higher Pass@k often means that "more sampling can rescue it," which differs from "the model itself being stronger" — the number of samples must be noted when reporting (echoing Section 8.8). 

Human evaluation, model evaluation, and length bias : Open-ended generation rarely has a unique answer, so human evaluation or LLM-as-judge [36][217] is commonly used. Model All Stages of Large-Model Training 96 

evaluation is cheap but carries judge bias (favoring long answers, favoring its own style), so debiasing means such as length control are needed [228]. Treating model-evaluation scores as absolute ground truth is one of the main risks to the credibility of current evaluation. 

Protocol sensitivity : Prompt format, the number of few-shot examples, decoding param-eters, and answer-extraction rules all significantly change scores. Numbers on "the same bench-mark" are therefore comparable only when the protocol is consistent; leaderboard scores across works should be regarded as directional evidence rather than precise measurement (Section 12.8). 

Applicability boundaries of basic metrics : Perplexity measures language modeling but does not correspond directly to downstream capability; accuracy/F1 suits tasks with definite answers; win rate (pairwise) suits open-ended generation but depends on judges. Choosing a metric means choosing "the direction to be optimized," and metric mismatch leads to "scores rise while experience falls." 

Statistical significance and variance : Score differences on small-sample benchmarks often fall within the noise range. Reports should give the mean and variance across multiple runs/seeds, or confidence intervals; treating a 0.3-point gap as a conclusion is a common error. Sample size, random seed, and decoding randomness must all be counted into uncertainty. 

Designing human evaluation : The cost and subjectivity of human evaluation demand a strict annotation protocol (scoring rubric, inter-annotator agreement, working conditions). "Crowdsourced scoring" is extremely noisy without quality control. Trustworthy open-ended generation evaluation mostly relies on controlled human evaluation or calibrated model evaluation [36]. 

Calibration and uncertainty : Beyond accuracy, whether a model "knows what it does not know" is equally important. Calibration metrics (such as ECE) and selective prediction (allowing abstention) are means of measurement; but the definition of calibration on generative tasks remains inconsistent, and RLHF often makes models overconfident (Chapter 8). 

Multi-objective conflicts among metrics : Helpfulness, truthfulness, safety, and con-ciseness often trade off against one another. Single-metric optimization leads to a "seesaw." Evaluation should therefore report a multi-metric vector and, when aggregation is needed, make the source of weights explicit; concealing conflicting metrics amounts to hiding tradeoffs. 

Actionable granularity of evaluation : An ideal evaluation should answer "which specific sub-capability is weak," not merely give an overall score. It should therefore report breakdown results by task/subset and the distribution of error types. The finer the granularity, the better it can guide the next data and training decisions (the "diagnostic" function of evaluation). 

Component vs. end-to-end evaluation : Systems such as retrieval augmentation and tool calling are composed of multiple components, and component metrics (retrieval recall) may diverge from end-to-end metrics (task success). Reporting only components overestimates the system, while reporting only end-to-end conceals bottlenecks. Both should be reported together to locate problems (Chapters 9 and 13). 

The weak association between perplexity and downstream performance : Lower validation perplexity usually accompanies better downstream performance, but how tight this relationship is depends on the data distribution and task. Treating perplexity as "capability" is misleading — it is especially sensitive to generation style, tokenization, and evaluation-set construction (Section 6.3). 

## 12.6 Contamination, Leakage, and Reproducibility 

Benchmark contamination (evaluation samples mixed into training/instruction data) is the greatest systemic threat: data cleaning [13] and contamination detection [52] are necessary steps, but "how much leaked and how large the effect is" remains hard to quantify. Detection methods include n-gram/embedding overlap [52][83], perplexity and memorization measures [371], and All Stages of Large-Model Training 97 

black-box detection [80][375]; "paraphrased samples" bypass literal detection [376], so semantic-level detection is needed. 

Estimating the impact of contamination : Some works use held-out data from the "post-training period" (retro-holdout [382]) or dynamically/automatically generated benchmarks [379][383] to isolate the effect of leakage; other calibration methods try to translate "partial leak-age" into scores [380][384]. These methods each carry their own assumptions, and there is no recognized standard — so "a benchmark score rose by X points" should by default carry the reservation of "possibly including contamination" (Section 12.10). 

Reproducibility : Reports of compute and energy consumption lack a unified accounting standard (Problem 10); it is recommended to report at least the model scale, tokens, approximate training FLOPs, peak GPU memory, and wall-clock time (Section 3.4). Reproducibility on the evaluation side also requires disclosing prompts, decoding settings, and evaluation scripts — reporting only scores without protocols amounts to non-reproducibility. 

Memorization and privacy evaluation : A model may memorize training data verbatim, implicating both copyright and privacy. "Prefix completion" and "membership inference" are commonly used to measure memorization and the memorization–generalization boundary [371]; deduplication [13][68] can reduce memorization (Section 5.7). Memorization evaluation and contamination detection share tools but differ in goal: the former asks "did it memorize," the latter asks "did it memorize the evaluation answers." 

Levels of deduplication methods : Exact matching, MinHash/approximate matching [13], semantic deduplication [68], and large-scale specialized systems [97][99] vary in cost and recall in that order. Insufficient deduplication aggravates memorization and score inflation, while excessive deduplication loses long-tail and diversity — the choice of threshold is itself a capability tradeoff. 

The relationship between contamination and capability reporting : If a benchmark is contaminated, its rising score may reflect only "memorizing the answers." Any claim of "sur-passing a benchmark" should therefore first answer "could this benchmark be in the training set." This step is often skipped, yet it is the most cost-effective step when interpreting the literature. 

Practical steps for contamination detection : An operational procedure is: 1○ record the collection timeline of training/evaluation; 2○ perform both literal and semantic overlap de-tection on evaluation samples; 3○ use perplexity/memorization as auxiliary criteria; 4○ report the suspected contamination ratio and the handling method (removal or annotation). Omit any of the four steps and the conclusion carries undeclared uncertainty. 

The ethics and law of contamination : Training on evaluation data not only inflates scores but may also constitute a violation of the benchmark publisher’s license. Reporting con-tamination and proactively removing it is both scientific integrity and a compliance requirement; "cannot detect" is no excuse for "not detecting." 

A new form of contamination introduced by synthetic data : When both training data and evaluation data may be generated by the same batch of models, "contamination" is no longer merely sample overlap but "shared-source bias" — the model may "happen to answer correctly" because it shares a generator. Detecting this kind of contamination is more difficult than literal overlap. 

The cost of contamination detection : Semantic-level detection requires embedding com-putation and manual review, at a cost far higher than n-gram; black-box detection requires large numbers of queries. Detection intensity should therefore also be layered: use cheap approxima-tions for daily work and deep detection on key benchmarks before release. Matching detection intensity to the importance of the conclusion is a sustainable practice. 

## 12.7 Fairness, Bias, and Safety Evaluation 

Bias evaluation : Benchmarks such as BBQ [360] measure a model’s bias on social attributes; bias stems both from the data distribution and from the evaluation design itself. When report-All Stages of Large-Model Training 98 

ing bias, one should distinguish "model behavior bias" from "measurement instrument bias"; otherwise one will misjudge. 

Hallucination evaluation : TruthfulQA [359] targets the imitation of human fallacies, while HaluEval [366] and FActScore [367] characterize hallucination from discrimination and fine-grained factual precision, respectively. The metric definition of hallucination (what counts as "unverifiable") is itself open, so scores from different benchmarks cannot be directly inter-changed. 

Safety evaluation : HarmBench [225] standardizes automated red-teaming, and WMDP [328] measures dangerous knowledge. The difficulty of safety evaluation lies in "coping with unknown attack surfaces" — passing a benchmark does not mean that uncovered vulnerabilities do not exist. Safety evaluation should therefore be a continuous red-teaming loop rather than one-off scoring (Chapters 8 and 13). 

The two ends of jailbreak and over-refusal : Safety evaluation must test both "whether it can be jailbroken" (false acceptance) and "whether it over-refuses legitimate requests" (false refusal). Testing only one end incentivizes degenerate solutions — refusing everything or com-plying with everything. Sound safety evaluation should report both ends and provide a tradeoff curve (alignment tax; Chapter 8). 

The sustainability of red-teaming : The attack surface evolves with capability and the deployment environment, so one-off red-teaming quickly expires. A continuous adversarial loop (discover—patch—re-evaluate) is therefore needed, along with recording attack samples to build a regression set [225]. This turns "safety" from a static metric into a process. 

Sources of bias : Bias can come from data (historical injustice), objectives (optimizing a biased preference), and evaluation (skewed benchmark design). Locating the source of bias determines the mitigation: debiasing on the data side, changing the reward on the objective side, switching protocols on the evaluation side. Attributing all bias to a "model problem" leads to ineffective fixes. 

Limitations of bias measurement : Bias is an attribute of groups under a dataset and social context, and a benchmark can only measure it by sampling. Low bias on a single bench-mark does not equal system safety, and vice versa. Bias evaluation should serve as continuous monitoring rather than one-off certification. 

## 12.8 Reproducibility and Reporting Standards 

Recommended checklist: training/evaluation splits and deduplication logs; per-model parameter and token basis; hyperparameters and schedule details; contamination detection results; evalu-ation prompts and decoding settings; data provenance and license documentation [61][78]; and safety and bias regression results. Following this checklist does not guarantee comparability, but it can turn "comparing numbers across works" from guesswork into an auditable process. 

Anti-patterns in reporting : reporting only the best single run, reporting only favorable benchmark layers, not reporting the sampling budget, not disclosing protocols, and treating model evaluation as human evaluation — these are reproducibility defects that recur in the current literature, and readers should discount any "leading" conclusion accordingly. 

Minimal reporting template : 1○ model and data basis; 2○ the protocol for each bench-mark (prompt, few-shot, decoding, number of samples); 3○ contamination detection and held-out data; 4○ safety and bias regression; 5○ compute and time. Missing any one item prevents readers from independently verifying. Appendices D and E provide templates. 

Versioning of evaluation : Benchmarks, prompts, and evaluation scripts should all be versioned and recorded alongside the model/data version. Otherwise "the same evaluation" yields incomparable numbers at different points in time (benchmarks get updated, dependencies get upgraded). A prerequisite for reproducibility is that the "evaluation stack" is traceable. 

Automated reporting and meta-evaluation : To reduce reporting costs, evaluation re-ports (benchmarks, protocols, results, contamination flags) can be generated automatically. But All Stages of Large-Model Training 99 

this requires a standardized evaluation stack; meta-evaluation (evaluating the quality of evalua-tion itself) is still scarce and is a leverage point for improving overall credibility.
