## 12.10 Key Controversies 

First, whether benchmark scores represent capability . Optimists approximate capability progress by benchmark progress; skeptics point out that contamination [83][375], protocol sensi-tivity, and "teaching to the test" inflate scores. The mainstream consensus is that benchmarks are a necessary but not sufficient condition and should be used together with held-out data and red-teaming (Section 12.6). Second, whether model evaluation can replace human evaluation . Supporters em-phasize its low cost and scalability [36]; opponents list its position/length/self-preference biases. A middle ground is "model evaluation for coarse screening, human evaluation for calibration, with debiasing such as length control [228]"; treating model evaluation as the final ground truth remains unreliable. Third, public leaderboards vs. private evaluations . Public leaderboards are compa-rable but easily "targeted" (leaderboard gaming); private evaluations resist gaming but are non-reproducible and easily questioned. The ideal third way is dynamic, reproducible, contamination-resistant benchmarks ([379][383]), but their maturity remains limited. All Stages of Large-Model Training 100 

Distorted leaderboard incentives : Public leaderboards induce "optimizing for the bench-mark" (e.g., specially tuned prompts, format pandering), making leaderboard scores increasingly unrepresentative of general capability. Mitigations include dynamic benchmarks, private held-out sets, and multi-benchmark consensus; but for an individual team, the safest approach is a self-built evaluation close to deployment. 

Human vs. model evaluation revisited : The two are not mutually exclusive — using model evaluation for high-frequency regression and human evaluation for low-frequency cali-bration, and calibrating model evaluation with human preference data, can balance cost and credibility. The key is to state clearly "who the judges are and how they are calibrated," rather than claiming one is absolutely better [36][228]. 

The reproducibility crisis of evaluation saturation : When most models approach per-fect scores on old benchmarks, discriminative power disappears and "evaluation" degenerates into "a statement of passing the line." This forces benchmarks to keep getting harder and pushes researchers toward "private/dynamic" evaluation — but the latter sacrifices comparability. The tension between "comparability vs. discriminative power" in evaluation will persist for a long time. 

Open data vs. closed data : Open data promotes reproducibility and fair comparison, but frontier teams tend to keep data closed to maintain an advantage; closed data increases confidentiality but harms scientific verifiability. This tension determines the range that a "public survey" can honestly cover — and is also one reason this survey assigns a great many Grade C labels. 

The tension between standardization and diversity : Standardized evaluation (a unified protocol) improves comparability but may stifle diverse evaluations tailored to specific scenarios; diverse evaluation is closer to real needs but hard to compare laterally. A pragmatic compromise is the coexistence of "a core standard set + scenario-specific extension sets." 

## 12.11 Practical Checklist and Open Problems 

Recommendations: define evaluation before data and training ; report by layer (understand-ing/knowledge/reasoning/code/multimodal/safety/truthfulness) rather than a single composite score; trace the protocol and contamination of every cited score; use a continuous red-teaming loop for safety; and attach provenance, license, and deduplication records to data. Open prob-lems : how to quantify the impact of contamination on capability conclusions (Problem 6); how to build contamination-resistant and reproducible dynamic benchmarks; how to make evaluation protocols comparable across works (compute/energy accounting standard, Problem 10). 

Cross-stage connections : The data selection of Chapter 5 takes evaluation as its yard-stick, and every change in Chapters 6–9 is accepted by evaluation; conversely, contamination in evaluation comes mainly from the data pipeline. "Data–evaluation" is thus a pair of systems that must be designed together, and the checklist in this chapter should be used throughout the survey (Figure 1). 

Interfaces with the other chapters : The data selection of Chapter 5, the scaling decisions of Chapter 6, and every change in Chapters 7–9 are accepted by the evaluation of this chapter; and the contamination of this chapter originates from the data pipelines of the preceding chapters. Evaluation is therefore not "the last chapter" but a horizontal axis running through the entire survey. 

Advice for different readers : Researchers should prioritize "protocol transparency + contamination disclosure"; engineers should prioritize "evaluation first + a deployment-aligned private set"; publishers should prioritize "data lineage + safety regression." The three roles have different evaluation responsibilities, but their shared baseline is "do not treat non-reproducible numbers as facts." 

An actionable evaluation research design : Take the same set of models and report the same benchmark scores under three conditions — "literal contamination detection vs. semantic All Stages of Large-Model Training 101 

contamination detection vs. no detection" — to quantify the impact of contamination on con-clusions. This kind of meta-study directly addresses Problem 6 and requires no training of new models. 

Refinement of open problems : 1○ How can reproducible, contamination-resistant dy-namic benchmarks be constructed? 2○ Can debiasing of model evaluation reach the calibration accuracy of human evaluation? 3○ How can a layered protocol commensurate with quality be established for "evaluation cost"? 4○ How can shared-source contamination of synthetic data be detected? Together, these four determine the credibility of the next generation of evaluation. 

Traffic-light interpretation : A set of actionable interpretation rules — green (safe to cite): protocol transparent + contamination checked + multiple benchmarks consistent; yellow (caution): single-benchmark lead, unclear protocol; red (do not cite): no protocol reported, suspected contamination, marketing claims only. This compresses the chapter’s checklist into an immediately usable decision tool. 

Interfaces with the appendices : The evaluation checklist of this chapter has correspond-ing templates in Appendix D (reproducibility checklist) and Appendix E (grading explanation); readers can use this chapter’s "traffic-light interpretation" directly to screen the evidence entries in the appendices. 

Mapping to the six-dimensional framework : data (training set/benchmarks/calibration set), objective (evaluation metrics are the optimization objective), optimization (evaluation-driven selection and early stopping), system (evaluation cost and throughput), evaluation (self-referential), and risk (contamination, bias, overfitting to leaderboards). Among the six di-mensions, the "evaluation" dimension of this chapter is both object and tool, and this self-referentiality is precisely the root of why evaluation is easily manipulated. 

Summary of the evidence distribution in this chapter : The methodology of evaluation (contamination detection, debiasing, documentation standards) already has a considerable body of Grade A/B literature; but "how much real capability a score represents" and "how to make it comparable across works" remain dominated by opinions and single-point methods. What this chapter provides is thus a normative framework and checklist for interpreting evaluation ,rather than a conclusion about "which model is better" — consistent with the survey-wide positioning of "providing a framework rather than a ranking." 

# 13 Practical Guide  

> This chapter is a pilot chapter, with a target of 8,000–9,000 words. It translates the mecha-nistic conclusions of the preceding text (Ch5–Ch12) into an "executable checklist," but pro-vides no magic numbers : any quantitative recommendation that cannot be synchronously verified with public evidence is labeled "this team’s prior" rather than a conclusion. All ci-tations reuse the core set of the preceding text; no new evidence is introduced here.

## 13.1 The Decision Tree (Verbalized Form of Figure 4) 

> 1

○ First define the constraints (budget/modality/context length/safety compliance) → 2○ budget determines scale: use scaling laws to estimate (N, D ) [16][119] → 3○ modality determines archi-tecture and data: choose decoder-only for pure text; choose encoder–projector–LLM or cross-attention for multimodality [17][28] → 4○ long-text requirements determine whether long-context training/extrapolation is needed [37][143] → 5○ safety compliance determines the post-training recipe: SFT first, then preference optimization (DPO family or RL) [32][45] → 6○ compress or adapt to the domain before deployment, then re-check alignment and safety. Each step gives tradeoffs rather than promised metrics. 

Making the decision tree falsifiable : Every one of the six steps above should leave a "verification point" rather than a "just-do-it point"—after 2○, run a small-scale scaling-law extrapolation to verify the curve; after 3○, do one round of modality acceptance using visual All Stages of Large-Model Training 102 Small  Medium  Large

Figure 4: Practical decision tree—budget → scale → modality → long context → post-training recipe →

checks and rollback All Stages of Large-Model Training 103 

samples; after 5○, run the "pre-alignment" evaluation of Chapter 12. The value of the decision tree is not "choose by following it," but "turning each step’s choice into a retraceable statement"; this is precisely the concrete form of the "capability–behavior–evidence" three-layer scheme of Chapter 11. 

Two default lines of defense : First, every branch first performs the "contamination– regression–held-out" triple check (Chapter 12), because these are the cheapest sources of false positives; second, every branch ends with a "rollback point"—when training is interrupted, the recipe diverges, or evaluation collapses, one can return to the previous verifiable state rather than guessing from logs. 

Making the unimodal assumption explicit : The default branch of the decision tree is a pure-text decoder (Section 6.1). Once vision/audio is added, the tree forks at 3○ into "encoder– projector" or "unified token," and introduces new knobs such as projector/freezing/resolution (Sections 10.1, 10.4). Therefore, the correct way to read the decision tree is: follow the left branch (pure text) by default, and take the right branch only when modality capability is needed, and each knob of the right branch must be re-checked at 6○, because changes on the modality side are the most easily and silently overwritten during post-training. 

Failure is data : Any "wrong branch" taken in the decision tree (excessive extrapolation, incorrect ratio) should be recorded as a new row in the failure-mode list of Section 13.3. Treating failure as garbage means paying tuition repeatedly; treating failure as data makes the decision tree a little more accurate with each iteration. This is isomorphic to the "evaluation-stack versioning" of Chapter 12. 

From-scratch vs. transfer branch : Between 2○ and 3○ the decision tree hides another ma-jor node—cold start (pretraining from scratch) or transfer (continuing from open-source/closed-source weights). The two paths impose different constraints on budget, control, and compliance (Section 11.10): transfer is fast but bound by upstream licensing and structural constraints, while cold start is expensive but fully autonomous in data/architecture. The decision tree should write the tradeoffs of this path clearly at the specification stage, rather than being forced to change course after training fails. 

## 13.2 Budget Estimation Framework 

Adopt order-of-magnitude estimation rather than fabricating numbers: autoregressive training FLOPs ≈ 6·N·D (Section 3.4); infer the throughput target backward from the available GPUs and target wall-clock time, then choose the parallelism strategy (data/tensor/pipeline/ZeRO [6]). Under the same N and D, multimodality and long context have higher budgets due to sequences and visual tokens, and must be estimated separately. No specific numbers that cannot be verified are given. 

Layering of the back-calculation formula : The upper bound (6·N·D) gives the ideal cost, which in practice must be divided by "MFU" (model utilization); MFU depends on the parallelism strategy, communication, and kernel efficiency. Therefore, the correct use of the budget framework is: first set the target wall-clock, then back-calculate the required FLOPs/s from MFU, then back-calculate N and D from the model and data scale. Use the upper bound when only an "order of magnitude" is required; when "scheduling" must be done, measured MFU must be introduced—the latter belongs to "this team’s prior." 

Hidden amplification of the post-training budget : The actual cost of the align-ment/evaluation stage is "cost × number of attempts" (Section 11.9). Therefore, the budget framework must reserve 2–3 × headroom for the grid search of SFT/preference/RL and for re-testing of the evaluation stack; otherwise the schedule will slip. This point is repeatedly empha-sized in all pilot chapters of this survey and is one of the most frequent causes of engineering failure (Section 13.3). 

A budget is an "order of magnitude," not a "commitment" : All budget figures should be written as "order-of-magnitude ranges" (e.g., 1e21–1e22 FLOPs) rather than a single All Stages of Large-Model Training 104 

committed value. The only honest reason is that it depends on variable factors such as MFU and the data-cleaning hit rate. Committing to a budget as a range amounts to admitting uncertainty in advance, whereas committing to a single value merely defers that uncertainty until it is exposed at the first evaluation gate. 

Realistic constraints of the cluster : The budget framework must accept that "mathe-matical optimality is not parallelism optimality"—when the cluster has only 8 GPUs and limited inter-node bandwidth, the optimal combination of data-parallel partitioning and pipeline depth deviates from the ideal degree of parallelism (Section 6.7). Therefore, measuring the MFU and communication overhead of a small task first and then setting the scale accordingly is more robust than fixing the scale first and then forcing it through a queue. Cluster shape is a prerequisite input to the budget framework, not a secondary customer-service parameter. The metric price of tokens/hour : The single most useful unit in a budget report is "tokens/hour + the corresponding MFU," because it encodes both throughput and utilization. All ramp-up runs, ratio A/B comparisons, and SFT rounds are counted in this unit; from it one computes the "cost per round," then converts to the units of the "exchange-rate table" of Section 11.6, so that team decisions have a common yardstick. A budget meeting without a common yardstick degenerates into a scramble for resources. 

Ramp-up and stop-loss : Besides the "planned value," the budget table must have two more rows—the "verified value" (small-scale measurement) and the "stop-loss point" (when to halt the direction). This is the meaning of ramp-up: it is not scaling without analysis, but splitting a large investment into a number of stoppable small experiments; if the data still does not support continuing after the stop-loss point, roll back to the previous verified state (Section 13.11). 

## 13.3 Failure-Mode Checklist 

High-frequency failure modes: data contamination not cleaned, leading to inflated benchmark scores; inconsistent SFT templates, leading to format leakage; length bias in preference optimiza-tion [227]; reward hacking [408]; over-refusal (alignment tax); MoE routing imbalance triggering loss spikes; long-context extrapolation yielding no gain on real long texts; editing a few models triggering collapse [324]; degradation of safety behavior after quantization. Countermeasures: establish pre-evaluation and rollback points for each category. 

Grouped countermeasures for failure modes : Equip the nine failure categories with a three-step "detect–isolate–recover" scheme: contamination →held-out warning; tem-plate leakage →two-sided protocol regression; length bias →length baseline and length-capped evaluation; reward hacking →out-of-distribution regression; over-refusal →dual benchmarks for safety/use-cases; MoE spike →routing statistics monitoring; no long-text gain →real long-text held-out; editing collapse →rollback to checkpoint; quantization degradation →post-quantization safety re-test. Three to five tenths belong to "detectable by evaluation in advance" (green list), and the rest belong to "detected only by runtime monitoring" (yellow list). 

Priority of countermeasures : The green list (detectable by evaluation in advance) should be the default "enabled at first launch," because it has the lowest cost; the yellow list (exposed only at runtime) should enter the monitoring dashboard and be paired with automatic alerts. Assigning attention to each failure category by the four quadrants of "detection cost → repair cost" is more consistent with budget discipline than "fixing whatever you see" (marginal returns of Section 11.6). 

Two misconceptions about failure : Misconception one—treating failure as a "personal error" and attributing it to people while ignoring systemic causes (data, recipe, interface); the correct approach is to attribute failure to the "stage pipeline," because most failures are repro-ducible across people. Misconception two—treating failure as "avoidable," leading to the use of excessive checks to guard against low-probability events; the correct approach is to fix high-frequency failures first according to 80/20 and keep a manual gate for low-frequency failures. All Stages of Large-Model Training 105 

Both misconceptions stem from "failure = should not happen," whereas the real philosophy is "failure = expected, and there must be a path to restoration." 

The same ledger for success and failure : Keep accounts for "success" as well—which ratio, which evaluation gate passed—in the same table as the failure list. There are two rea-sons: first, the precondition for the reproducibility of success is that the "recipe stamp" is sufficiently complete; second, the attribution of success is equally susceptible to contamination by the "common-method effect" (Section 11.16). Recording only failures and not successes makes the team pay repeatedly for duplicate verification. 

## 13.4 Configuration Recommendations 

Small budget: QLoRA [30] fine-tuning + DPO [32]/SimPO [43] alignment + training-free quantization [21][34]. Medium budget: full-parameter SFT + GRPO/RLVR [45][224] + merg-ing/distillation. Large budget: run the ratio and filtering scaling laws [87][86], then train ac-cording to the Chinchilla ratio [16]. The configuration recommendations are all annotated based on evidence grading, progressively adding system and stability measures as the budget grows. 

The common discipline of the three budget tiers : Regardless of the configuration tier, the three elements of "evaluation first + rollback point + contamination held-out" cannot be omitted—they do not shrink with the budget, because they are the only meters by which you judge whether the budget is well spent (Chapter 12). The difference between configuration tiers lies only in the "concentration of measures" (full-parameter vs. quantized, simple DPO vs. RL depth), not in the "concentration of discipline." 

Boundaries of the tier recommendations : All three tier recommendations are built on the premise of "existing reusable weights and evaluation stack"; if one starts from scratch in all three respects—data, tools, and personnel—the lowest cost is not a "small-budget configuration" but "first renting a verified mid-tier cloud service to practice the paradigm." Including the fixed cost of "from scratch to a verified platform" in the budget makes the intuition that "small = cheap" distorted. 

Budget discipline for hyperparameter sweeps : The hyperparameters of align-ment/sequential decision-making (learning rate, DPO β, RL temperature, etc.) appear to require "grid search," but in fact the first few rounds already exceed the budget. The discipline is: first use a small model to define a coarse grid (corners + center), then densify once in a small range around the best coarse point; each sweep must use the same evaluation anchor, otherwise scores under different hyperparameters are not comparable (Section 12.9). Hyperparameters are part of the budget, not a zero-cost free knob. 

## 13.5 Stage 0: Requirements and Constraints Checklist 

Before starting work, first write a "Stage 0 specification": deployment goals (three categories of metrics: capability/behavior/safety), budget ceiling (compute + time + personnel), modality and environment constraints, acceptable recall and latency, compliance boundaries (data licensing, deployment region). The specification must be able to answer "what counts as success and what counts as failure," and must give a foothold to the "capability–behavior–evidence" decomposition of Chapter 11. 

Three categories of signatories of the specification : Business (defines success and boundaries), engineering (defines cost and risk), and evaluation (defines the acceptance protocol) each sign once; if any is missing, work should not begin. Moving the "sign-off point" up to Stage 0 is an order of magnitude cheaper than discovering a mismatch in scope only at delivery—this is the part of this survey that can be directly converted into institutional practice. 

Making requirements measurable : Every goal in the specification must carry the suffix "how to measure it," otherwise it is merely a wish. Example: "answers more safely" should be written as "jailbreak success rate < X% on a private safety set (Section 8.7)"; "understands All Stages of Large-Model Training 106 

documents better" should be written as "accuracy ≥ Y on document-understanding benchmark B (Section 10.9)." The measurement suffix connects directly to the three sets of anchors of Chapter 12 (capability/behavior/safety). 

Three categories of license checklist for compliance : Stage 0 should also go through the data/model licensing triangle—training-data license (whether training/commercial use is permitted), weight license (whether secondary development/distribution is permitted), and de-ployment license (regional and industry restrictions, Sections 5.5, 12.3). The three each occupy one line in the specification; wherever licensing conflicts with budget, it should be downgraded before work begins to "a risk already evaluated by this team," rather than left for arbitration at release time. 

## 13.6 Data Pipeline Checklist 

The pretraining data pipeline is executed according to Chapter 5: collection →deduplication →filtering →mixing →tokenization →held-out. The checklist in-cludes: recording the timeline and provenance; performing two levels of deduplication, n-gram and semantic; setting dedicated held-out sets for contamination-sensitive categories (code, exams, news); setting the mixture by deployment language and domain; and confirming with validation perplexity and downstream sampling after tokenization. The statistical items of the data card are taken from Chapter 12. 

Data snapshot : Any training must freeze a "data snapshot" (version + hash), otherwise reproducibility and contamination auditing (Chapter 12) cannot even be discussed. Snapshots are also used for cross-stage interface contracts: SFT data and preference data reference the same set of provenance records, so that error attribution can be traced to the data layer. 

The tunable entry point for mixing : The data mixture (language/domain/code pro-portions) is the only knob that can change the capability distribution "without adding training budget" (Sections 5.8, 6.5). In practice, the mixture should first be A/B compared at small scale (one version each for A/B, same budget and same evaluation), then scaled up; the A/B results are brought into the "exchange-rate table" of Section 11.6 as this team’s prior values. 

The mixture is not intuition; it is a parameter that can be updated by experiment. Entry threshold for synthetic data : Synthetic data can scale up, but carries the side effects of "same-source bias" and quality risk (Sections 5.9, 12.6). The entry threshold is three checks: the accuracy of synthetic samples on a real-distribution held-out set, their distributional overlap with real samples, and contamination sensitivity (whether the synthesizer includes the evaluation in training). If any of the three fails, the volume advantage of synthetic data will be offset by its systematic bias. 

The budget of deduplication and filtering : Deduplication/filtering in the data pipeline is not a "one-time free step"—its occupancy of CPU and disk, as well as the labor hours of manual spot-checking, must be included in the budget. Ranking by cost-effectiveness: n-gram deduplication (cheap, mandatory) → semantic deduplication (medium, as needed) → manual quality inspection (expensive, sampled). When the budget is insufficient, prioritize keeping n-gram deduplication and the held-out sets; semantic deduplication/quality inspection may be reduced in frequency but must not be cut entirely (Sections 5.4, 12). 

## 13.7 Pretraining and Scale-Decision Checklist 

Scale decisions follow Chapter 6: determine the (N, D ) curve by small-scale extrapolation, then follow the Chinchilla ratio; the mixture design covers language/domain/code; the parallelism strategy is chosen by budget and cluster shape; training monitors loss, gradient norm, and routing statistics; finally, a set of downstream sampling is used to evaluate where the "capability wall" lies. Long-text requirements are evaluated separately (Section 9.5). All Stages of Large-Model Training 107 

Early-warning indicators of the "capability wall" : When scaling extrapolation pre-dictions begin to diverge from measurements (the curve plateaus faster than extrapolation), or downstream sampling enters a plateau, one should judge that the capability wall has been reached and promptly decide whether to "push the wall" (add data/scale) or "go around the wall" (add post-training/compression), rather than running to the end by brute force (Section 11.1). 

Standard protocol for small-scale extrapolation : The minimum three elements re-quired for extrapolation—the same data stream (sampled from the same distribution), the same evaluation anchor (Section 11.6), and at least three scale points (e.g., 60M/200M/600M). If any of the three is missing, the extrapolation curve is untrustworthy; the honest approach is to re-port the deviation range between extrapolation and measurement simultaneously, rather than drawing a smooth fitted line (evidence grading of Section 6.3). 

Additional budget items for long context and multimodality : If the specification includes long context or visual input, the budget framework must list two additional items—the long-sequence FLOPs and attention cost of long-text training (Section 9.5), and the additional attention and KV cache for visual tokens (Section 10.4). These two items are often underesti-mated several-fold by the "equivalent text token count"; listing them explicitly in the budget table of 13.2 is the minimal action to keep multimodal/long-text projects from blowing their budgets. 

Metrics of the monitoring dashboard : The minimal set for pretraining/post-training monitoring: loss, gradient norm, routing statistics, and evaluation-anchor sampling (every N steps). These metrics need not all be on the canvas—routing and loss go to alerts, gradient norm and sampling go to logs; the key is to map "change" to "effect": which step’s modification corresponds to which metric’s drift—this is precisely why the "evaluation gate" of Section 13.11 can open in advance during training. 

Reconciling training cost and evaluation cost : Most budgets count only training (FLOPs) and ignore the continuous occupancy of the evaluation stack (GPU for each anchor sampling, reviewer hours). Only by combining "training cost × number of attempts + evalua-tion cost × number of evaluations" does one obtain "the true cost of ownership of the scheme" (Section 11.9). The clearer the reconciliation, the better one can rebut the optimistic board that says "the estimate is only 10% off"—because that 10% is often hidden in the number of evaluations. 

## 13.8 Post-Training Checklist (SFT/Preference/RL) 

According to Chapters 7–8: SFT first passes the data-quality gate (consistency, deduplication, format); preference data clearly annotates provenance and judges (human/model); choose the depth of preference/RL according to reward verifiability (Section 11.4); before any alignment change, run a "pre-alignment evaluation," and after the change, run an "alignment regression." Cost reduction and safety matters are recorded together. 

Version stamp of alignment : Each SFT/preference/RL round must leave a "recipe stamp" (data, hyperparameters, seed, evaluation results), forming an alignment timeline. The timeline makes "which change introduced which behavior" traceable, and directly supports the contam-ination and regression auditing of Chapter 12—an alignment process without a recipe stamp amounts to an unauditable change log. 

Auditing obligations for preference data : When preference data (human- or model-annotated) enters the recipe, it must answer: who annotated it, with what protocol, and whether it is same-source as the training data (to prevent self-preference contamination). This is more important than "the more preference data the better"—the evidence of Section 8.4 supports "quality and consistency first." The audit results are written into the recipe stamp and archived together with the weights. 

Concretizing the SFT data-quality gate : The SFT data-quality gate has at least three levels: format consistency (unified template/fields), content deduplication (n-gram + semantic), All Stages of Large-Model Training 108 

and instruction diversity (proportion of repeated templates). All three gates must be given a "dry-run delta"—use a fixed set of small models to run A/B on the same data, to see whether changes in the gate value bring observable behavioral differences; if there is no difference, the gate is ineffective before it and should be replaced with a more sensitive gate (Chapter 7). 

The SFT →preference →RL handover protocol : At handover, give the next stage three things—baseline evaluation (capability/behavior/safety), the recipe stamp (data + hyperparam-eters + seed), and a list of "pits already stepped in" (which type of data triggers which type of failure). A stage transition without a handover protocol often loses all the implicit constraints of the previous stage, letting RL continue to amplify the same type of error in SFT’s blind spots (Section 8.10). 

## 13.9 Pre-Deployment Checks and Continual Optimization 

Before deployment, perform three "frozen evaluations": capability, (alignment) behavior, and safety, each retaining a baseline; then run compression/quantization and re-test the three regres-sions (Chapter 9). In the continual-optimization stage, every incremental change goes through the same regression gate; when introducing online feedback, configure "control + held-out" ac-cording to Section 11.13. 

Rollback switch : Another role of the three frozen baselines is to provide rollback targets—if a new version is found to degrade after deployment, one can quickly switch back to the previous "evaluation-passing" version. Rollback is not failure but part of discipline; continual optimization without a rollback switch is equivalent to leaving oneself no way back (Section 11.13). 

Acceptance boundaries of compression : The acceptance of compression (quantiza-tion/pruning/distillation) cannot look only at throughput and perplexity; it must check whether the three sets of numbers—capability (benchmark), behavior (alignment regression), and safety (attack surface)—are each still within tolerance (Chapter 9). If only one of the three is within tolerance, claiming "lossless compression" means narrowing the acceptance—the release check of Section 13.10 will intercept such a claim. 

Entry threshold for online feedback : The threshold for introducing online/closed-loop (Section 11.13) is: 1○ the feedback pipeline has a "control" (random traffic split for comparison); 

> 2

○ the retraining data has undergone same-source contamination checks; 3○ a switch to roll back to the old version is available. Only when all three are in place is it worth connecting the loop to production—otherwise the real-time nature of online only accelerates the spread of errors rather than accelerating learning. 

Cadence of continual optimization : Continual optimization is not "changing every day" but "changing with a cadence"—the round frequency depends on the dual signals of data feedback (emergence of new requirements) and evaluation feedback (regression achieved). Unpaced infinite fine-tuning is a breeding ground for over-optimization and drift (Section 11.13): every retraining must go through the same evaluation gate and use "control splitting" to prove that the net benefit of the change is positive, rather than the illusion that "it is better just because it was changed." If no new requirement has appeared for two weeks, one should turn off the continual-optimization sentinel rather than existing for existence’s sake. Rolling decisions for the online/offline combination : Production often runs multiple versions simultaneously (offline main version + online experimental version), using traffic buck-eting for comparison (Section 13.13). Rolling decision-making is a series of small-step advances of "evaluation gate + business metrics": each bucket passes the gate before traffic is scaled up, otherwise it is rolled back. The value of rolling decision-making is that it turns "release" from asingle bet into the accumulation of a series of verified small increments .All Stages of Large-Model Training 109 

## 13.10 Evaluation and Release Checklist 

Evaluation follows Chapter 12: the protocol (prompt/decoding/sampling count) is recorded; contamination held-out + in-depth detection on some benchmarks; the report includes context, safety, and bias regressions; numbers are versioned. At release, attach the data card, evaluation card, and reproducibility statement (Appendices D, E). 

Three lines of the release check : 1○ falsifiable (provide sufficient protocol/seed so that opponents can reproduce); 2○ auditable (data lineage + recipe stamp + evaluation timeline); 3○rollback-capable (retain the previous release baseline). Only when all three lines pass is it advis-able to claim externally that "we did better"; otherwise all performance claims are downgraded to "this team’s internal conclusion" (Chapter 3). 

One more reference to the minimal report template : The release report must at least contain the model and data scope, the protocol for each benchmark, contamination detection and held-out, safety and bias regressions, and compute and time (Section 12.11). These five sections are not an "optional appendix" but the five keys that give readers the ability to re-check you; if any one is missing, your conclusion cannot be replayed by others—no matter how successful it is claimed internally. 

Release materials for privacy and red-teaming : If the deployment surface involves privacy and security, the release materials should also contain a privacy impact statement (data-collection and deletion channels) and a red-team summary (coverage, residual risk, ongoing plan, Sections 8.7, 12.7). These materials must themselves be "versioned"—they update with model versions, otherwise old gaps will be mistaken for the current state. Release is not written once but is a record line that evolves with versions. 

Semantic traps in reported numbers : When writing reports, beware of three types of number expressions that "look honest but are misleading": 1○ reporting only relative gains without mentioning the baseline; 2○ benchmark scores transparent but one’s own goals not transparent; 3○ "average improvement" masking variance differences. The countermeasure is the reverse application of the "traffic-light interpretation" of Chapter 12—the releasing party proactively provides the protocol, intervals, and exclusion notes. Truthfulness and persuasiveness are not contradictory; on the contrary. 

## 13.11 Timeline and Milestones 

Break the overall task into milestones with an ordering and checkpoints: M0 specification and baseline evaluation → M1 data snapshot and scale extrapolation → M2 pretraining/continued pretraining → M3 SFT and preference/RL → M4 compression/pre-deployment evaluation →

M5 release and continual optimization. Each milestone ends with an "evaluation gate," not with "training complete." 

Common design of the evaluation gate : Each gate needs only three questions—"Is the capability online? (Chapter 6) Is the behavior compliant? (Chapter 8) Is the evidence credible? (Chapter 12)." If any of the three is missing, the gate does not pass; better to stop here than to advance; advancing a milestone that has not passed the gate is the most costly of all subsequent troubleshooting costs (Section 11.7). 

Parallelism and serialization of milestones : M0 →M1 is serial (the specification pre-cedes everything), after which M2/M3/M4 can advance in parallel (data and alignment trial-run simultaneously, pre-deployment evaluation and compression in parallel). Serialization exists only at "dependency" points; parallelism the rest of the time is key to saving money; but at each par-allel handover, alignment must be made through an "anchor evaluation," otherwise parallelism amounts to each going its own way. 

Discipline for rescheduling milestones : If a gate fails twice in a row, it is permissible and indeed advisable to reschedule the milestones (rather than hastily waving them through)— rescheduling must answer three questions: is the gate failure "a problem with the evaluation," All Stages of Large-Model Training 110 

"excessive ambition," or "immature implementation"? The three countermeasures are, respec-tively, fixing the evaluation, lowering the specification, and adding budget. Institutionalizing "rescheduling" bleeds far less than letting it happen quietly (the re-engagement point for the three categories of signatories of Section 13.5). 

## 13.12 Example Configurations (Three Tiers for Inspiration Only) 

Small-budget example: based on open-source weights (LLaMA/Qwen et al. [9]), QLoRA [30] + DPO [32], evaluated with mid-sized benchmarks + a private held-out set. Medium-budget example: full-parameter continued pretraining (small research scale) + full-parameter SFT + GRPO [45][224]. Large-budget example: pretraining from scratch + ratio scaling laws [87][86] + Chinchilla [16] + full-stack alignment and compression. The "numbers" in each tier are only scenario illustrations, not recommended values —the actual values must return to your own Stage 0 specification. 

Mixed tier beyond the three tiers : Most real projects fall between "medium" and "large": using an open-source large model as the base (controlling the knowledge wall), with mid-scale domain continued pretraining + full-parameter SFT + DPO/RL combination. The value of the mixed tier is that it obtains both "mature base" and "controllable recipe," at the cost of being constrained by upstream licensing (Section 13.5). This survey does not rank the tiers but requires that any tier count the "continued-training and full-stack cost" together, avoiding "seeing only the cheap, not the total account." 

Supplement to the multimodal tiers : The difference between multimodal tiers lies not in the "measures" but in "freezing scope and resolution budget" (Sections 10.1, 10.4): small budget freezes the encoder + trains the projector, medium budget unfreezes the projector and pre-aligns at low resolution, large budget unfreezes the encoder + high resolution. The higher the tier, the more compute must be reserved for "the attention cost of image tokens" (Section 10.4), and each tier requires multimodal-specific regressions (hallucination/grounding/OCR). 

Supplement to multilingual configuration : For non-English-dominant deployments, the specification must explicitly set multilingual proportions in three links—data mixture (Section 5.8), preference data (Chapter 8), and evaluation anchors (Chapter 12). If a single sentence is missing, the model will quietly degrade into "English-first, other languages following." Multilin-guality is not an "extra bonus item" but a configuration line that must be written by default in the specification. 

## 13.13 Tool and Open-Source Stack Pointers 

List the recommended tools scattered across the chapters: training frameworks and parallelism (the ZeRO [6] series), quantization ([21][34]), PEFT (QLoRA [30]), alignment (DPO [32]/SimPO [43]/GRPO [45][224]), compression (quantization/distillation/editing [324]), and evaluation and contamination detection (Section 12.9). The release scope of the list extends only to "methods and representative implementations"; specific project versions follow the reader’s contemporane-ous ecosystem (this survey is responsible only for mechanisms, not for version commitments). 

Steps for building the evaluation stack : Building the evaluation stack (Section 12.10) is threshold-free work that precedes training—select benchmarks (three sets: capabil-ity/behavior/safety), write the protocol (prompt/decoding/sampling count), make held-out sets (the base set for contamination detection), and then freeze a "baseline snapshot." It should be one of the project’s earliest outputs, because every subsequent milestone (Section 13.11) com-pares against the same baseline each time; building the baseline late amounts to leaving all early conclusions unanchored. 

Boundaries of tool selection : More tools are not better—every tool introduced also in-troduces a set of its evaluation criteria (the "evaluation-stack versioning" of Chapter 12). This survey has only two recommendations for selection: first, "interface contract" first (the tool can All Stages of Large-Model Training 111 

align with the standards of data snapshots and recipe stamps); second, "evaluation-stack stabil-ity" first (a tool with a stable evaluation protocol is more important than one with more features but changing protocols). The rest is left to the reader’s contemporaneous ecosystem to judge. 

## 13.14 Team and Collaboration 

A training project is a relay of four roles: "data–training–evaluation–safety": data is responsible for snapshots and lineage, training for the recipe stamp, evaluation for the baseline gate, and safety for attack-surface regression. Each of the four roles is responsible for the tables of Chapter 12; a change by any one introduces a regression. Factory-style collaboration usually fails at "interface distortion"—this is also the team version of the "schema interoperability" of Section 11.10. 

The four-sentence RACI mantra : Data is responsible for "where it came from and when it is snapshotted"; training is responsible for "what recipe was used and when it is re-run"; evaluation is responsible for "what the baseline is and whether the gate was passed"; safety is responsible for "where the attack surface is and whether the regression is green." If any change cannot clearly state "who is responsible for its regression," it should not enter the milestone—this moves the release check of Section 13.10 forward to the collaboration layer. 

Fork discipline for open-source collaboration : If the project contains open-source com-ponents (Section 13.13), the team should internally observe "fork discipline"—source code and configuration each versioned, upstream protocols unmodified, patches either flowed back up-stream or local differences explicitly marked. The reason is not dogma but auditability: without fork discipline, the snapshots of training and evaluation cannot be traced to "which version of the code was used" (versioning of Section 12.10). 

Knowledge management and documentation : Even a four-person small project should hand over process documentation (data, recipe, evaluation gates)—not written for outsiders but for oneself three months later. The cheapest form is "keep a markdown snapshot at each evalu-ation gate," and the most expensive is always verbal transmission. The point of documentation is not length but "replayability": every conclusion can be restored from the documentation to its inputs, outputs, and judgments at the time (Section 13.10). 

## 13.15 Closing with the Whole Survey 

This chapter is not an "operation manual" but a bridge that "turns the conclusions of Ch5– Ch12 into checkpoints": the data chapter provides the snapshot checklist, the scale chapter the extrapolation budget, the alignment chapter the recipe stamp, the evaluation chapter the baseline gate, and the continual chapter the rollback switch. If readers read only one chapter before deployment, read this one; but to truly understand why each checkpoint exists, one must return to the corresponding chapter to examine the evidence and cost—because behind every checklist item is a falsifiable mechanistic hypothesis. 

The four most common questions : 1○ "Can we do it without enough budget?"—Yes, but the three minimum disciplines of the three tiers (evaluation first + rollback point + contamination held-out) cannot be omitted; omitting them amounts to outsourcing uncertainty to the release stage; 2○ "Is it fine to just use someone else’s open model directly?"—Transfer is a reasonable default in most scenarios, but one must first go through the licensing and coordination of Section 13.5; 3○ "Which failure should be fixed first?"—Prioritize the green list of Section 13.3; what evaluation can intercept is the cheapest; 4○ "When should we retrain?"—The trigger conditions of Section 13.8: improvement saturation + inconsistency with the capability-wall hypothesis. These four can serve as lessons from other stones for this chapter; the real answer always returns to your Stage 0 specification. 

A final reminder : In a practical guide, the most "correct" things are often "proven-effective defaults," and the most dangerous are often "pruning that looks clever." The position of this All Stages of Large-Model Training 112 

survey: may you not lack astonishing ideas, but lack a stable skeleton—and the skeleton is precisely these unremarkable checkpoints of this chapter. They do not guarantee success, but they guarantee that failure is attributable, rollback-capable, and learnable (Chapter 1). 

The last mile between this chapter and the appendices : If readers wish to start hands-on, take away two kinds of checklists: 1○ the checkpoint checklist of this chapter (Stage 0 specification, evaluation stack, recipe stamp, baseline gate, rollback switch); 2○ the templates of Appendices D/E and the reporting checklist of Section 12.11. Putting the checklist into a local repository is enough to start work; this chapter never asks you to remember practices, only that you use "evaluation gates" and "snapshots" to turn every step into an auditable record (Section 13.10). 

A final sentence for all budget tiers : No matter how small the tier or how few the measures, the success or failure of a training project is ultimately determined by "whether at-tribution can be done auditably" (Sections 13.10, 13.11). A training run with an even tighter budget, as long as it leaves a traceable sub-baseline, can convert its lessons into gains in the next iteration; conversely, an ample budget without traceability will only make failure replicate more expensively. This sentence is both the master switch of the checkpoints in this chapter and the common practical landing point of all chapters throughout the survey. 

# 14 Challenges and Open Problems 

Conclusion first: the most intractable challenge in this field is not the performance of any single technical point, but the gap between "strength of evidence" and "decision-making needs"—a large number of conclusions treated as common knowledge still rest on a single technical report or on scale experiments that cannot be reproduced. This chapter surveys the challenges along four lines—technique, data, compute and engineering, and ethics and safety—and distills them into 15 verifiable open problems (numbered consistently with the problem pool of Stage 2; the complete pool is given in Appendix F). 

## 14.1 Technical Challenges 

Joint allocation of the scaling law and the post-training budget is the foremost unsolved problem. Kaplan [119] and Chinchilla [16] provide only the optimal (N, D ) trade-off "internal to pretraining"; today, as inference-time compute and post-training budgets keep expanding [395][396], how total compute should be divided among pretraining, post-training, and inference still lacks a unified theory. Whether investing more compute in post-training can buy back equivalent capability depends on the base model’s "alignability," and this property itself lacks a controllable measure (Problem 1). 

The boundary of reasoning ability remains at the center of controversy. Reasoning RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) [45][224] reports significant improvements on decidable tasks such as mathematics and code, but whether "RL expands or narrows reasoning paths" is disputed between Pass@1 and Pass@K—improving greedy-decoding accuracy may simultaneously reduce sampling diversity and the upper bound [45][400][401]. This dispute determines whether we should regard RL as capability injection rather than mere behavior shaping (Problems 5, 6). 

Reward hacking and sycophancy are systematic failures of the preference/RL stage [408]: the co-evolution of the reward model and the policy induces the policy to exploit loopholes in the proxy objective (length bias, confident phrasing, format pandering), and such failures are often masked by average metrics. There is currently a lack of suppression mechanisms and stable measures during training (rather than only at inference time) (Problems 7, 13). 

The alignment tax and its measurement : safety alignment, while improving refusal and honesty, may damage general capabilities, and the empirical relationship between its magnitude and model scale or data mixture has not yet been established [23][38]. The absence of an All Stages of Large-Model Training 113 

"alignment tax" benchmark that can be compared across models makes it difficult to trade off safety investment against capability loss (Problem 8). 

The preservation property of continual optimization : whether quantization, prun-ing, distillation, and model editing preserve alignment and safety behavior while compress-ing/correcting a model still rests on fragmentary evidence [21][34][324]; editing is often accom-panied by "ripple effects" (non-target facts are altered), while the direction of quantization’s impact on safety refusal is inconsistent (Problems 9, 14). 

A unified objective for multimodality : whether images/videos should be optimized as discrete tokens or continuous features remains undecided—unified discrete tokens [273] and continuous connectors (LLaVA-style [28]) each have trade-offs, and "unification" may mean sac-rificing both compression efficiency and alignment flexibility at the same time (Problem 3). 

## 14.2 Data Challenges 

Data quality shifts the optimal trade-off : Chinchilla’s optimum [16] was obtained under a specific data quality, whereas filtering/mixture scaling laws [87][86] show that quality and mixture shift the optimum, so the empirical value of a "fixed token/parameter ratio" may become outdated (Problem 2). 

The bootstrapping boundary of synthetic data : using model-generated data to retrain a model (Self-Instruct [24], the Evol-Instruct [29] family) may bring short-term gains, yet carries the risk of model collapse and accumulation of systematic bias, and whether a "critical point" exists remains unknown (Problem 11). 

Long context: "trained short, extrapolated long" : whether positional-encoding ex-trapolation and interpolation [37][143][149] can reliably scale on models not trained on long texts depends on a task’s need for genuinely long-range dependencies, and its reliable boundary is not yet clear (Problem 12). 

The root cause of multimodal hallucination : does hallucination come from data (image-text mismatch), the tokenizer (loss of detail through compression), or the alignment stage (lan-guage priors overwhelming visual evidence)? Attribution experiments are scarce, leaving inter-ventions often at the level of treating symptoms (Problem 15). 

## 14.3 Compute and Engineering Challenges 

Standardization of compute/energy reporting : the FLOPs, GPU hours, and energy con-sumption of the largest-scale training mostly appear only in unverifiable engineering reports (Section 11.4, Appendix C), so cross-work comparison loses a common basis; the absence of auditing conventions makes reproducibility claims difficult to verify (Problem 10). 

Reproducible approximation under constrained conditions : when both compute and data are constrained, how to approximate scaling-law predictions in a reproducible manner in-volves parallelism efficiency, numerical stability (loss spikes), and checkpoint fault tolerance, and the transferability of engineering conclusions is weak (Problems 1, 10). 

The absence of cross-stage cost attribution : a single capability improvement may come from any link among data, pretraining, post-training, or inference, yet existing reports seldom perform attribution ablations, and "which layer the money is spent on" lacks comparable accounting (Section 11.9, Problem 10). 

## 14.4 Ethics, Safety, and Governance Challenges 

Machine unlearning and copyright compliance : removing the influence of specific knowl-edge/data from a trained model without damaging general capabilities is an interdisciplinary legal and technical problem [39][328][326]; existing methods are mostly validated at small scale and lack a joint benchmark with capability preservation (Problem 14). All Stages of Large-Model Training 114 

Jailbreak, honesty, and multimodal safety : the robustness of safety alignment under out-of-distribution inputs, multi-turn inducements, and image-text combination attacks remains an open problem, and safety behavior often competes with "over-refusal" [23][38] (Problems 13, 15). 

Data licensing and traceability : the provenance, licensing, and traceable registration of training data vary in practice, affecting both legal compliance and contamination auditing as well as the credibility of conclusions (Problem 10). 

The boundary of "legitimate use" in evaluation : using public benchmarks for training selection slides toward contamination, yet ignoring benchmarks entirely makes decisions difficult; the lack of a protocol that "both exploits and preserves the validity of the test" (Section 12.9) is the challenge closest to daily practice at the governance level (Problems 10, 11). 

Cross-stage evidence asymmetry : the data and evaluation sides already have relatively more peer-reviewed evidence, whereas the largest-scale training, online closed loops, and multi-modal scaling laws are still dominated by technical reports (Appendix E.1); this asymmetry itself systematically biases research toward what is "publishable but not most critical" (Problems 2, 10). 

## 14.5 The Common Root of the Four-Line Challenges 

The four-line challenges above are not independent of one another; their common root is that "strength of evidence cannot keep pace with the granularity of decisions": we must make fine-grained choices under compute-budget and compliance constraints, yet only coarse-grained and partly unverifiable evidence is available. Therefore, the most effective way forward is not to improve metrics point by point, but first to establish "measures and definitions" that can be independently examined (Chapters 11, 12) and only then to compare methods and routes on that basis. Otherwise, discussions of technique, data, compute, and governance will each proceed from mutually incompatible assumptions and cannot converge into a single coordinate system. 

## 14.6 List of Open Problems 

The above challenges are distilled into 15 verifiable problems (numbered consistently with the problem pool of Stage 2): 1. How can the scaling law be jointly optimized with the post-training budget (allocation of train-time vs test-time compute)? 2. How does data quality change the optimal token/parameter ratio (does Chinchilla shift with data quality)? 3. How can the optimization objectives of discrete and continuous tokens be unified in multi-modal training? 4. What is the quantifiable causal relationship between vocabulary design and multilin-gual/multimodal fairness? 5. Does RL actually expand or narrow reasoning paths (systematic experiments on Pass@1 and Pass@K)? 6. Can verifiable rewards be generalized to subjective/open-ended tasks, and at what cost? 7. How should the robustness boundary of process reward models vs outcome reward models in online RL be defined? 8. Can the alignment tax be quantified and empirically related to model scale and data mixture? 9. How can continual optimization (quantization/pruning/merging/editing) preserve alignment and safety behavior? 10. How can compute/energy reporting and training-reproducibility auditing be standardized? 11. Does synthetic-data bootstrapping have a critical point of capability collapse? 12. Is long context with "trained short, extrapolated long" possible (without long-text training)? All Stages of Large-Model Training 115 

13. How can reward hacking and sycophancy be suppressed by training-time mechanisms (rather than only at inference time)? 14. How can machine unlearning and copyright compliance be achieved without harming general capabilities? 15. Does the root cause of multimodal hallucination lie in the data, the tokenizer, or the alignment stage? 

# 15 Future Directions 

The key takeaway is that future research should shift its focus from pursuing isolated improve-ments in individual components to pursuing "cross-stage verifiability." That is, the gains from each training stage must be evaluated under a unified assessment and compute accounting frame-work to measure their net impact on the overall capability and behavioral outcomes of the full training pipeline. In line with this perspective, we propose five major directions, each corre-sponding to a problem identified in Section 14.5. 

Direction 1: Joint cross-stage scaling laws (Problems 1, 2, and 8). Future work should integrate pretraining, post-training, and inference budgets into a unified (N, D, C ) extrap-olation framework [16][119], and explicitly treat data quality and data mixture as free variables [87][86]. By systematically measuring the ternary surface of "quality–mixture–model" at con-trolled scales, one can examine whether the Chinchilla-optimal point shifts with data quality and whether the alignment tax can be empirically modeled as a function of scale. The expected outcome should be a reproducible scaling-experiment protocol, not merely a single fitted curve. 

Direction 2: Systematic experiments on RL for reasoning (Problems 5, 6, and 7). 

With Pass@1 and Pass@K jointly used as anchors, future studies should systematically compare the robustness boundaries of PPO [15], DPO-family methods [32][43], and RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) [45][224] on reasoning tasks. A key experiment is to decouple "capability ex-pansion" from "behavioral shaping": by fixing the base model and the data while varying only the verifiability of the reward, one can test whether the attainable performance ceiling changes accordingly. Comparisons between process rewards and outcome rewards must also be conducted in online settings to delineate their respective robustness regimes. 

Direction 3: Training-time safety mechanisms (Problems 13, 14, and 15). Rather than relying primarily on inference-time guardrails, future efforts should aim to proactively sup-press undesirable behaviors—such as sycophancy, reward hacking, and hallucinations—directly through reward design and curriculum learning, and should pair these interventions with transfer-able evaluation metrics. For machine unlearning, joint benchmarks that measure both forgetting effectiveness and capability preservation are essential. For multimodal safety, evaluations must cover cross-modal attack combinations. The overarching goal is to make safety a trainable and measurable objective, rather than a post-hoc patch. 

Direction 4: Unified multimodality and attribution (Problems 3, 4, and 15). Con-trolled experiments on the same backbone are needed to resolve the tradeoffs between discrete and continuous token representations. In addition, researchers should design decomposable at-tribution experiments for hallucinations that independently isolate the effects of data, tokenizer, and alignment. Causal analyses of tokenizer design with respect to vocabulary coverage and multilingual/multimodal fairness should be conducted via controlled tokenizer training, rather than relying on correlational observations. 

Direction 5: Reproducible and auditable infrastructure (Problems 9 and 10). 

Standardized practices should be established for reporting compute and energy consumption, documenting data licenses, and detecting data contamination. Furthermore, quantization, model editing, and machine unlearning should be integrated into a unified safety benchmark. This di-rection provides the common foundation for the first four: without auditable reporting standards, no cross-stage conclusion can be independently verified. All Stages of Large-Model Training 116 

A unifying methodology across all five directions: three prerequisites are necessary to ensure that progress is comparable and cumulative: (1) a unified set of evaluation anchors spanning capabilities, behaviors, and safety; (2) a unified compute accounting scheme that re-ports both FLOPs magnitude/range and tokens-per-hour; and (3) a unified data snapshot that records provenance and versioning. The absence of any of these renders individual advances non-composable within the full training pipeline (see Chapters 11 and 12). 

A near-term, actionable checklist: (i) publish controlled "quality–mixture–scaling" ternary experiments at small to medium scales; (ii) report both Pass@1 and Pass@K on rea-soning tasks; (iii) incorporate reward-model robustness tests into routine regression suites; (iv) release safety-preservation reports for quantization and model editing; (v) conduct at least one three-variable decoupling experiment for multimodal hallucinations. All five are feasible with existing open-source tools and modest compute, making them well-suited as starting points for follow-up work (see Chapter 13). 

From general framework to focused sub-surveys: As outlined in Appendix F, each chapter of this survey provides an extensible skeleton that can be independently expanded. Rather than waiting for a grand unified theory, future work can proceed in parallel by stage: focused sub-surveys on data and tokenization can cover multilinguality and synthetic data; on pretraining, MoE stability and data-constrained regimes; on post-training, training-time safety; on continual optimization, the interplay between compression and safety; and on multimodality, unified objectives and hallucination attribution. Provided each sub-survey adheres to the evalua-tion and compute accounting standards defined in this general framework, its conclusions can be reintegrated into the same coordinate system to form a cumulative body of knowledge—precisely the intended dual form of "general framework + focused sub-surveys." 

Update discipline for the evaluation coordinate system: If these directions are adopted, the evaluation coordinate system itself must be periodically revised (Appendix F.8): scaling curves should be shifted in response to data quality changes, contamination detection methods should evolve with benchmark revisions, and evaluation protocols should be updated alongside new benchmark versions. We recommend an annual review cycle in which only Class C reports and benchmark versions are replaced, while the underlying mechanism remains intact. Any updated values must be accompanied by "version + source" metadata, and direct numerical comparisons across different datasets or compute regimes must be avoided (see Section 12.11). The common premise underlying all five directions is that the greatest bottleneck in this field is not compute, but rather the lack of "comparable evidence." Accordingly, instead of chasing isolated record-breaking results, the most pressing and pragmatic recommendation from the standpoint of this general framework is to first strengthen the infrastructure for evaluation and reporting. 

# 16 Conclusion 

Through a unified stage division and a six-dimensional framework, this paper systematically reviews the entire pipeline of large-model training from 2017 to 2026, clarifying the division of capabilities across stages, the bidirectional effects across stages, and the distribution of evidence. The core conclusion is: pretraining determines the upper bound of capability, post-training determines the form of behavior, and multimodality is a modality periphery on the same backbone ; any comparison that does not annotate compute and data criteria lacks cross-work credibility. The evidence throughout is predominantly peer-reviewed (A+B, approximately 79%), and the greatest uncertainty is concentrated in the details of the largest-scale training, the boundaries of reasoning RL, and the impact of continual optimization on safety. We therefore advocate an "auditable" view of training: regarding training as a series of decisions equipped with evaluation gates, recipe stamps, and rollback switches, rather than a one-All Stages of Large-Model Training 117 

off choice of recipe (Chapter 13). The unified framework, cross-stage influence graph, method-family map, practical decision tree, and 15 verifiable open problems (Section 14.5) presented in this paper together constitute a coordinate system for subsequent research; we hope that future work will take it as a benchmark and advance "training as decision-making" from empirical intuition into reproducible, auditable regularities. Finally, we must reiterate the self-imposed limitation of this paper: as a "general framework" rather than a "most comprehensive survey," its value lies not in providing final answers but in offering a set of coordinates that can be tested, revised, and decomposed into focused surveys (sub-surveys). If readers can thereby point out which of our conclusions lacks sufficient evidence or which criterion is inconsistent—this is precisely the manner of advancement that this paper hopes for.

# Appendix D: Reproducibility Checklist 

1. Tokenizer and vocabulary configuration (algorithm, vocabulary size, whether pre-tokenization is used) [1][4][5][51]. 2. Data cleaning/deduplication/contamination-detection logs [13][52][56][61]. All Stages of Large-Model Training 119 

3. Training convention (N, D, approximate FLOPs, batch, sequence, precision, parallelism strat-egy) [6][118][135][142]. 4. Optimization and stability (optimizer, schedule, warmup, clipping, initialization) [105][110][132]. 5. SFT: instruction-data source, template, loss masking (Section 7.2). 6. Alignment: preference data, RM/DPO variants, hyperparameters [32][43][224]. 7. Evaluation: prompt, decoding parameters, contamination re-check, alignment-tax re-check (Chapter 12). 

## D.8 Evaluation Stack and the "Baseline Gate–Rollback Switch" (Chapter 13) 

1. Evaluation-stack snapshot : benchmarks (three groups: capability/behavior/safety), pro-tocol (prompt/decoding/number of samples), held-out set, and contamination-detection base set, frozen as one "baseline snapshot" (Sections 12.10, 13.13). 2. Data snapshot : each version of training data is frozen with "version + hash", and the same lineage record is referenced across stages (pretraining/SFT/preference) (Section 13.6). 3. Recipe stamp : each SFT/preference/RL round records data, hyperparameters, seeds, and evaluation results, forming an alignment timeline (Section 13.8). 4. Baseline gate and rollback switch : three groups of frozen baselines (capabil-ity/behavior/safety); every change passes through the same evaluation gate, and after de-ployment there is a previous baseline that can be rolled back to (Sections 13.9, 13.11). 

## D.9 Five-Section Template for Release Reports (Sections 12.11, 13.10) 

> A release report contains at least five sections; if any section is missing, the conclusion cannot be reproduced by others. It is used in conjunction with the "three inspection lines" in Section 13.10 (falsifiable / auditable / rollback-capable).

1. Model and data convention : N, D (including the tokenizer and the conventions before and after deduplication), modality, frozen scope. 2. Per-benchmark protocol : prompt, few-shot, decoding parameters, number of samples, evaluation-script version. 3. Contamination detection and held-out : detection level (literal/semantic), suspected pro-portion, handling method. 4. Safety and bias regression : safety set, bias set, alignment-tax re-check, multilin-gual/multimodal dual track. 5. Compute and time : order-of-magnitude range of FLOPs, tokens/hour and MFU, wall-clock time and number of attempts (Section 11.9).

## F.8 Updatable Data Coordinates (Sections 11.17, 12.11)  

> The following three sets of coordinates may be updated with versions on the premise that the mechanistic conclusions of the main text remain unchanged. When updating, replace only the C-tier technical reports and benchmark versions, without overturning the structure.

1. Scaling-curve coordinates : the Chinchilla optimum [16], the Kaplan extrapolation [119], and the incremental curves missing for multimodal/long-context settings (Sections 6.3, 10.5). 2. Contamination and parsing coordinates : comparison of each benchmark’s release time with the training-data timeline, and recommended thresholds for the two-level literal/semantic detection (Section 12.6). 3. Evaluation-protocol coordinates : the current version of each benchmark, the scoring script, and the update rules for the "traffic-light" interpretation (green/yellow/red) (Sections 12.11, 13.10). 

> Update discipline (consistent with the main text): any new coordinate value must first be given as "version + source", must not compare numbers directly across datasets/compute, and if it cannot be verified, the original value is retained with an annotation.
